Examples: strategy menus with decision criteria, if-then regulatory protocols, structured response frameworks linked to monitoring outputs.
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Control-Scaffolding|Control Scaffolding]]*

> [!definition] **Controlled Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> Motivation arising from external or introjected pressures — rewards, deadlines, approval-seeking, ego-involvement, guilt, shame. The perceived locus of causality is external (or internal but pressuring). Produces compliance and effort but degrades learning quality, creativity, and well-being under pressure conditions. *See also: [[Undermining-Effect]], [[Introjected-Regulation]], [[External-Regulation]].*
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Controlled-Motivation|Controlled Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Controlled Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The umbrella category in OIT encompassing external and introjected regulation — all forms of motivation in which the learner experiences the source of their engagement as external pressure or internal compulsion (guilt, shame, ego-threat). Controlled regulation is associated with surface processing, performance orientation, higher anxiety, and reduced wellbeing. The functional marker is an external or impersonal perceived locus of causality.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Controlled-Regulation|Controlled Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Controlled Relationship Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> The motivational quality in which a person maintains a close relationship due to external pressure, introjected guilt or obligation, fear of aloneness, or excessive sunk cost reasoning. Controlled motivation is NOT equivalent to dislike of the relationship — a person can be highly satisfied with a relationship while maintaining it for controlled reasons. The psychological cost of controlled motivation accrues through resource depletion and reduced autonomous functioning in other domains.
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Controlled-Relationship-Motivation|Controlled Relationship Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Controlling Environment** **[perm-note]**
> An environment that pressures learners toward specific outcomes through rewards, punishments, surveillance, deadlines presented as sources of pressure, praise that is evaluative rather than informational, conditional regard (where teacher warmth is contingent on performance), and language that communicates "you must," "you should," or "I will reward/punish you for." Controlling environments may produce compliance, but the compliance comes at the cost of autonomous motivation, deeper processing, and wellbeing.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Controlling-Environment|Controlling Environment]]*

> [!definition] **Core DataviewJS Task Methods** **[perm-note]**
> - `dv.pages()` - Get pages as DataArray
> - `.file.tasks` - Access all tasks in a file (including subtasks)
> - `.where()` - Filter tasks (equivalent to WHERE in DQL)
> - `.groupBy()` - Group tasks by field
> - `dv.taskList()` - Render tasks as interactive checklist
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-dataview-tasks-quieries-2025120204* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Core-DataviewJS-Task-Methods|Core DataviewJS Task Methods]]*

> [!definition] **Core Definition: Epistemic Vigilance** **[perm-note]**
> 
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-epistemic-vigilance-and-epistemic-humility* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Core-Definition-Epistemic-Vigilance|Core Definition: Epistemic Vigilance]]*

> [!definition] **Corroboration** **[perm-note]**
> Karl Popper's term for the epistemic standing earned by a hypothesis that has survived rigorous attempts at falsification. A highly corroborated hypothesis is not *verified* — it has not been confirmed as certainly true — but it has demonstrated its robustness by repeatedly surviving tests designed to refute it. Corroboration is explicitly not a measure of probability of truth but of tested resilience.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Corroboration]]*

> [!definition] **Cortico-Striatal Learning Loops** **[perm-note]**
> The basal ganglia organize into parallel loops connecting specific cortical regions through striatum to specific motor/cognitive outputs. The **motor loop** (motor cortex → putamen → GPi/SNr → thalamus → motor cortex) supports motor sequence learning. The **cognitive loop** (dlPFC → caudate → GPi/SNr → thalamus → dlPFC) supports cognitive skill automatization. These loops enable both motor and cognitive automaticity through common computational principles.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-attentional-efficiency-and-skill-aquisition* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cortico-Striatal-Learning-Loops|Cortico-Striatal Learning Loops]]*

> [!definition] **Counterexample Search** **[perm-note]**
> The third and most demanding stage of the MMT inference cycle, in which the reasoner attempts to construct an alternative model consistent with the premises but inconsistent with the candidate conclusion. If a counterexample model is successfully constructed, the candidate conclusion is rejected or revised. Failure to engage in counterexample search — often due to working memory limitations or task demands — is the principal source of deductive error in the MMT account.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Counterexample-Search|Counterexample Search]]*

> [!definition] **Coupling-Constitution Fallacy** **[perm-note]**
> The alleged error of inferring, from the fact that two systems are causally coupled, that one thereby constitutes part of the other. Adams and Aizawa argue that Clark and Chalmers commit this fallacy: because Otto's notebook is causally coupled to his cognitive processes, they infer it constitutes part of his cognitive system. The objection insists that causal relevance and constitutive membership are distinct relations, and that establishing the former does not establish the latter. The mark of the cognitive — whatever distinguishes genuine cognitive processes from causally relevant non-co...
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Coupling-Constitution-Fallacy|Coupling-Constitution Fallacy]]*

> [!definition] **The Critical Lens** **[perm-note]**
> Examines whose interests the received view serves, what perspectives it excludes, and what social or political dimensions are rendered invisible by the dominant framework. This lens asks: What has been stripped from schema theory in the process of formalizing it, and why does that matter?
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Critical-Lens|The Critical Lens]]*

> [!definition] **Critical Thinking** **[perm-note]**
> Critical thinking is "purposeful, self-regulatory judgment which results in interpretation, analysis, evaluation, and inference, as well as explanation of the evidential, conceptual, methodological, criteriological, or contextual considerations upon which that judgment is based." This definition, produced by expert consensus across disciplines, emphasises that CT is not merely logical analysis but a self-aware, self-correcting cognitive activity — one that monitors and adjusts its own processes.
> 
> *Attribution: Delphi Report*
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Critical-Thinking|Critical Thinking]]*

> [!definition] **Critical Thinking as Metacognitively-Regulated Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> **[[Critical-Thinking]]** is defined operationally as the deliberate application of reasoning skills and strategies that increase the probability of reaching sound conclusions through purposeful, reflective judgment, mediated by metacognitive monitoring and control processes that regulate strategy selection, execution monitoring, and error correction. This definition emphasizes that critical thinking is fundamentally a *metacognitive process* rather than simply a collection of reasoning techniques, distinguishing skilled critical thinkers not by their possession of reasoning tools but by th...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-critical-thinking-metacognitive-regulation* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Critical-Thinking-as-Metacognitively-Regulated-Reasoning|Critical Thinking as Metacognitively-Regulated Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **Crystallization Threshold** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The level of accumulated pre-schema knowledge at which a set of loosely organized elements rapidly consolidates into a coherent, retrievable schema. Pre-threshold states are characterized by slow, effortful, element-by-element processing; post-threshold states by fluent, whole-schema activation.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** The crystallization threshold is domain-specific and learner-specific. Threshold conditions include: varied-context exposure, generative description tasks, and explicit structure-finding exercises.
> 
> **Operational Indicators:** Threshold crossing is marked by...
> 
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Crystallization-Threshold|Crystallization Threshold]]*

> [!definition] **Cue Validity** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which a monitoring cue (e.g., processing fluency, familiarity, FOK) accurately predicts the cognitive state being assessed (e.g., comprehension depth, future retrievability). High-validity cues produce accurate monitoring; low-validity cues systematically miscalibrate monitoring judgments regardless of monitoring effort.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cue-Validity|Cue Validity]]*

> [!definition] **Cultural Neutrality Assumption** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The implicit assumption in the formalized, computational version of schema theory that schemas are culturally universal cognitive structures — that everyone's "restaurant schema" or "narrative schema" is essentially similar in structure, differing only in content. After this re-examination: this assumption is false, and its embedding in the dominant account has political consequences for whose prior knowledge counts as legitimate in instructional contexts.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: Exposed by the critical lens challenge; connects this cognitive science debate to broa...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cultural-Neutrality-Assumption|Cultural Neutrality Assumption]]*

> [!definition] **Cyclical Model of Self-Regulated Learning** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A three-phase model of [[Self-Regulated-Learning]] — Forethought, Performance, and Self-Reflection — organized as a feedback cycle in which each phase's outputs function as inputs to subsequent phases. The cyclical claim (that self-reflection quality shapes subsequent forethought quality) distinguishes it from sequential or modular accounts of SRL.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The cycle's architectural claim — that phase-transition quality, not phase-content quality alone, determines SRL development — is the central analytical focus of this report.
> 
> **Operational Indic...
> 
> *Source: zimmerman-cyclical-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Cyclical-Model-of-Self-Regulated-Learning|Cyclical Model of Self-Regulated Learning]]*

## _D

> [!definition] **Data Layer Components** **[perm-note]**
> - **Lifecycle Management**: Connection initialization, capability negotiation, termination
> - **Server Features**: Tools, resources, prompts exposed to clients
> - **Client Features**: Sampling from host LLM, user input elicitation, logging
> - **Utility Features**: Notifications for real-time updates, progress tracking
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-mcp-servers-2025122412* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Data-Layer-Components|Data Layer Components]]*

> [!definition] **Declarative Memory** **[perm-note]**
> Memory for facts and events that can be consciously recalled and reported — "declared" in words or propositions. Declarative memory is subdivided into **episodic** and **semantic** memory (Tulving, 1972). Declarative memory is contrasted with non-declarative memory, which operates without conscious access. Neurologically, declarative memory formation is critically dependent on the [[Hippocampus]] and surrounding medial temporal lobe structures, as dramatically demonstrated by the patient H.M. (Henry Molaison), whose bilateral hippocampal removal eliminated his ability to form new declarativ...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Declarative-Memory|Declarative Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Declarative, Procedural, and Conditional Metacognitive Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> A tripartite taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge developed from Flavell's framework. Declarative metacognitive knowledge is knowledge *that* (e.g., knowing that spaced practice is more effective than massed practice). Procedural metacognitive knowledge is knowledge of *how* to implement strategies (e.g., knowing how to space study sessions using a retrieval practice protocol). Conditional metacognitive knowledge is knowledge of *when* and *why* to apply strategies (e.g., understanding that spaced practice is most beneficial for material requiring long-term retention rather than short-term p...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Declarative,-Procedural,-and-Conditional-Metacognitive-Knowledge|Declarative, Procedural, and Conditional Metacognitive Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Decoding** **[perm-note]**
> The translation of printed symbols (graphemes) into their phonological equivalents (phonemes) and from there to word-level representations. Decoding is the primary focus of early reading instruction and is the foundation on which fluency and comprehension are built. Decoding accuracy (getting the correct phonological representation) is distinguished from decoding automaticity (getting it without consuming working memory resources).
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Decoding]]*

> [!definition] **Deductive Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> Deductive reasoning is a form of inference in which a conclusion is drawn from premises in such a way that, if the premises are true, the conclusion *must* be true. The relationship between premises and conclusion is one of logical necessity — the conclusion is contained within, or guaranteed by, the premises. Deductive reasoning moves from the general to the specific: from universal rules or principles to particular instances or applications. It is fundamentally distinct from reasoning in which the conclusion is merely probable, plausible, or well-supported by evidence. **What it is NOT**:...
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Deductive-Reasoning|Deductive Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **Deep Processing** **[perm-note]**
> Encoding that focuses on the meaning, conceptual content, and associative network of a stimulus. For verbal material, this involves accessing lexical meaning, connecting the word to prior knowledge, generating elaborations, and embedding the stimulus in a rich semantic context. Craik and Lockhart proposed that the resulting memory trace is qualitatively richer, with more retrieval routes and greater resistance to forgetting.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Deep-Processing|Deep Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Defeasibility** **[perm-note]**
> A property of justification or reasons: a justification J for belief B is *defeasible* if there is some additional evidence E such that J together with E no longer justifies B. The concept captures the fallibilist claim that justification is always provisional. A defeasible reason is a genuine reason — it actually supports the belief — but it can be "defeated" by defeating evidence. This is to be contrasted with deductive entailment, which is indefeasible: if premises P1 and P2 deductively entail conclusion C, no additional information can make C false given P1 and P2 (though it might give ...
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Defeasibility]]*

> [!definition] **Defeat / Defeater** **[perm-note]**
> A defeater for a belief is any evidence or consideration that rationally requires the holder of that belief to revise it. *Rebutting defeaters* provide direct evidence against the target belief. *Undercutting defeaters* provide evidence against the reliability or applicability of the justification for the belief. The defeater framework, developed by John Pollock, is the primary formal vocabulary for understanding how fallibilist revision works.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Defeat-Defeater|Defeat / Defeater]]*

> [!definition] **Defensive Inference** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: A specific sub-process of the Self-Reflection Phase in which learners respond to negative self-evaluation by adopting self-protective attributional strategies and behavioral adjustments that preserve self-esteem at the cost of learning. Contrasted with *adaptive inference*, which involves modifying strategy and increasing effort in response to failure. Defensive inference (e.g., attributing failure to task difficulty or bad luck, reducing effort to preserve ability-attribution, avoiding similar tasks in the future) breaks the Self-Reflection → Forethought feedback link and p...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Defensive-Inference|Defensive Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Degenerate Cycle** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** An SRL cycle in which the functional connections between phases are absent or minimal — the learner enters the performance phase without meaningful goal setting or strategic planning (no forethought), and exits with global evaluative self-reflection ("that was hard/easy") rather than causal, strategy-specific self-reflection. A degenerate cycle proceeds through three temporal phases without the inter-phase signal transmission that makes the cycle self-modifying.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The degenerate cycle is proposed as the primary explanation for why SRL trainin...
> 
> *Attribution: Original Terminology*
> *Source: zimmerman-cyclical-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Degenerate-Cycle|Degenerate Cycle]]*

> [!definition] **Deliberate Practice** **[perm-note]**
> A highly structured form of practice specifically designed to improve performance, characterized by: activities at the edge of current ability (requiring full concentration), immediate and accurate feedback on performance, the opportunity to correct errors, and the guidance of a coach or teacher who designs practice activities targeting specific weaknesses. Deliberate practice is distinguished from mere experience or play by its explicit metacognitive structure: it requires ongoing monitoring of performance against a clear standard and strategic modification of practice based on identified ...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Deliberate-Practice|Deliberate Practice]]*

> [!definition] **Deliberative Democracy** **[perm-note]**
> A normative model of democratic governance emphasizing the role of reasoned, inclusive, and open public deliberation in the formation of legitimate collective decisions. Empirically, deliberative democratic theory has inspired a research program on "mini-publics" — citizens' assemblies, deliberative polls, participatory processes — that tests the conditions under which ordinary citizens engage in high-quality political reasoning. Fishkin's Deliberative Polling research is the most systematic empirical programme in this tradition.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Deliberative-Democracy|Deliberative Democracy]]*

> [!definition] **Delphi Consensus Definition** **[perm-note]**
> "Critical thinking is the process of purposeful, self-regulatory judgment. This process gives reasoned consideration to evidence, contexts, conceptualizations, methods, and criteria... CT is pervasive and self-rectifying human phenomenon... The ideal critical thinker is habitually inquisitive, well-informed, trustful of reason, open-minded, flexible, fair-minded in evaluation, honest in facing personal biases, prudent in making judgments, willing to reconsider, clear about issues, orderly in complex matters, diligent in seeking relevant information, reasonable in the selection of criteria, ...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-foundational-critical-thinking* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Delphi-Consensus-Definition|Delphi Consensus Definition]]*

> [!definition] **Description Field Components** **[perm-note]**
> An effective description contains:
> 
> **1. Invocation triggers** (when to use this agent):
> ```yaml
> description: Use PROACTIVELY when designing APIs, defining endpoints, or planning REST architectures.
> ```
> 
> **2. Domain keywords** (what this agent knows):
> ```yaml
> description: Expert in React, TypeScript, component architecture, hooks, and modern frontend patterns.
> ```
> 
> **3. Task verbs** (what actions this agent performs):
> ```yaml
> description: Reviews code for security, analyzes vulnerabilities, audits dependencies, scans for OWASP Top 10.
> ```
> 
> **4. Exclusions** (when NOT to use):
> ```yaml
> descri...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Description-Field-Components|Description Field Components]]*

> [!definition] **Desirable Difficulties** **[perm-note]**
> Conditions of learning that impede initial acquisition but produce superior long-term retention and transfer — including spaced practice, interleaved practice, retrieval practice, and reduced feedback. Desirable difficulties are, in the language of this report, metacognitive challenges: they require learners to monitor their own uncertainty, actively reconstruct knowledge, and notice gaps rather than having them filled.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Desirable-Difficulties|Desirable Difficulties]]*

> [!definition] **Developmental Need Satisfaction History** **[perm-note]**
> The accumulation of need-satisfaction and need-frustration experiences across development that is theorized in COT to be the primary determinant of adult causality orientations. Not directly measured in GCOS studies but operationalized through retrospective self-report of parental and educational environment in some studies.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Developmental-Need-Satisfaction-History|Developmental Need Satisfaction History]]*

> [!definition] **Differentiated Conception of Ability** **[perm-note]**
> In a differentiated conception of ability, effort and ability are understood as *separate* entities that can partially cancel each other out. If I have to try very hard to accomplish something that others accomplish easily, this is evidence that my ability is lower, not higher. Under this conception, demonstrating high ability means achieving more with less effort (or the same outcomes with less effort) relative to others. This is the conception of ability operative in *ego involvement*, and it is cognitively demanding — children typically do not fully develop it until late childhood or ear...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Differentiated-Conception-of-Ability|Differentiated Conception of Ability]]*

> [!definition] **Disposition** **[perm-note]**
> A stable cognitive tendency or habit of mind that inclines a person toward or away from CT engagement. Key CT dispositions include inquisitiveness, truth-seeking, open-mindedness, analyticity, systematicity, self-confidence in reasoning, and cognitive maturity. Dispositions are distinguishable from skills: a person may have the skill to think critically without the disposition to deploy it, and vice versa.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Disposition]]*

> [!definition] **Dissolved Assumption** **[perm-note]**
> In the Assumption Audit framework of this report: a previously accepted assumption that does not survive scrutiny and cannot be retained even in modified form. Distinguished from Revised assumptions (contain truth but need modification) and Retained assumptions (survive scrutiny with full support). Two assumptions in this report were dissolved: Assumption 5 (domain-generality) and Assumption 6 (brief intervention transfer).
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Dissolved-Assumption|Dissolved Assumption]]*

> [!definition] **Distinctiveness** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which an encoding stands out as unique relative to the surrounding set of encodings. A distinctive encoding is processed in a way that differentiates it from similar items, reducing interference and improving retrieval specificity. Together with elaboration, distinctiveness was incorporated into the LOP framework to explain within-level retention differences that simple depth accounts could not address.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Distinctiveness]]*

> [!definition] **Distributed Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> The theoretical framework holding that cognitive processes are distributed across individuals, artefacts, and environmental structures in sociotechnical systems, and that the appropriate unit of cognitive analysis is the system as a whole rather than the individual mind within it. Developed through ethnographic study of real-world cognitive systems (naval navigation, airline cockpits, scientific laboratories). Convergent with but methodologically distinct from Extended Mind Theory: Hutchins focuses on social-material systems; Clark and Chalmers focus on individual organism-environment coupl...
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Distributed-Cognition|Distributed Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Distributed Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: A conception of self-regulation in which the regulatory functions described by Zimmerman (monitoring, standard-setting, strategic adjustment) are partially or primarily instantiated in tools, artifacts, other people, and environmental structures rather than in the individual learner. Distributed regulation is not a failure of self-regulation but an alternative architecture for achieving regulatory functions. [[Situated-Cognition]] and [[Situated-Learning]] frameworks emphasize distributed regulation; the Zimmerman model, by contrast, treats environmental scaffolds as *suppor...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Distributed-Regulation|Distributed Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Hedonic Adaptation** **[perm-note]**
> The well-documented tendency for people to return to a stable baseline level of well-being following positive or negative life events. Related to the attainment paradox in GCT: extrinsic goal attainment may fail to produce durable well-being gains partly because of hedonic adaptation, while intrinsic goal pursuit may be more resistant to adaptation because its well-being benefits are process-based (ongoing during pursuit) rather than outcome-based (concentrated at the moment of attainment).
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Hedonic-Adaptation|Hedonic Adaptation]]*

> [!definition] **DOpus Built-in Variables** **[perm-note]**
> Common variables for dynamic button construction:
> 
> *Source: directory-opus-button-compendium* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[DOpus-Built-in-Variables|DOpus Built-in Variables]]*

> [!definition] **DOpus Command Modifiers** **[perm-note]**
> These prefixes modify how commands execute:
> 
> *Source: directory-opus-button-compendium* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[DOpus-Command-Modifiers|DOpus Command Modifiers]]*

> [!definition] **Double-Loop Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A concept from organisational learning theory, appropriated by Hase and Kenyon into heutagogy, describing learning that involves revision of the underlying norms, values, and assumptions that govern action — not merely revision of the strategies used to achieve existing goals (single-loop learning). Self-directed learners who engage only in single-loop learning improve their execution within a given framework; double-loop learners revise the framework itself when evidence suggests it is inadequate. Double-loop learning is the mechanism through which self-direction moves from technical compe...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Double-Loop-Learning|Double-Loop Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Dual-Process Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A broad theoretical framework distinguishing two types of cognitive processing: System 1 (fast, automatic, associative, low-effort, resistant to conscious override) and System 2 (slow, deliberate, rule-governed, effortful, sensitive to instruction). Deductive reasoning is paradigmatically a System 2 activity; deductive errors — including belief bias and affirmation of the consequent — are often explained as failures of System 2 to override System 1's rapid, heuristically generated responses. Individual differences in deductive performance correlate with System 2 capacity measures (working m...
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Dual-Process-Theory|Dual-Process Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Dyslexia** **[perm-note]**
> Dyslexia is a specific learning disability neurobiological in origin. It is characterized by difficulties with accurate and/or fluent word recognition and by poor spelling and decoding abilities. These difficulties typically result from a deficit in the phonological component of language that is often unexpected in relation to other cognitive abilities and the provision of effective classroom instruction.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Dyslexia]]*

## _F

> [!definition] **Fallibilism** **[perm-note]**
> The philosophical thesis that any belief, however well-justified, could in principle be false, and that the evidence or reasoning supporting it could be overturned by subsequent inquiry. Fallibilism does not entail that our beliefs *are* false or probably false, nor that they are unjustified — only that their justification is inherently defeasible. The thesis applies paradigmatically to empirical knowledge but has been extended by various philosophers to moral, mathematical, and logical domains. Fallibilism is a thesis about the *epistemic status* of beliefs, not a prediction about how ofte...
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Fallibilism]]*

> [!definition] **Falsificationism** **[perm-note]**
> Karl Popper's philosophy of science, proposed as an alternative to inductivism: science proceeds not by accumulating confirming instances but by formulating bold, falsifiable hypotheses and subjecting them to rigorous attempts at disconfirmation. A hypothesis gains credibility by surviving falsification attempts, not by accumulating positive evidence. Influential in science education for its emphasis on testability and its recognition of the logical asymmetry between confirmation and refutation; criticized by Bayesian philosophers for not describing actual scientific practice accurately.
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Falsificationism]]*

> [!definition] **FastMCP** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #72FFF1;'>**FastMCP**</span> is a high-level Python framework that handles protocol complexities, letting developers focus on tool logic rather than JSON-RPC implementation.
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-mcp-servers-2025122412* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[FastMCP]]*

> [!definition] **Feed-Forward** **[perm-note]**
> The prospective, action-guiding dimension of feedback: information specifying what the learner should do next, which strategies to deploy, and how to approach the next iteration of the task. Distinguished from backward-facing evaluation of what was done. Effective feedback integrates backward diagnosis with forward navigation, but should weight the latter more heavily.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Feed-Forward]]*

> [!definition] **Feeling of Knowing** **[perm-note]**
> The metacognitive sense that one knows something even though one cannot currently retrieve it — the experience underlying the "tip-of-the-tongue" phenomenon. FOK judgments influence the allocation of study time: high FOK items receive additional study on the assumption that retrieval will be unlocked. The reliability of FOK as a predictor of subsequent recall is moderate, influenced by both genuine partial encoding and superficial familiarity.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Feeling-of-Knowing|Feeling of Knowing]]*

> [!definition] **Felt Difficulty** **[perm-note]**
> The organismic experience of encountering a situation that existing habits, patterns, and expectations cannot handle smoothly — a lived sense of friction, interruption, or perplexity that serves as the initial trigger for reflective thinking. Distinguished from abstract logical problems by its essentially embodied, affective character.
> 
> *Source: report-01-the-inquiry-engine* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Felt-Difficulty|Felt Difficulty]]*

> [!definition] **Effectance** **[perm-note]**
> The subjective experience of being effective in interaction with the environment — the psychological state of genuine competence satisfaction. Effectance is not the same as objective performance: a student can perform well without experiencing effectance (if success feels attributable to luck, scaffolding, or low task difficulty) and can experience effectance from failure in the right challenge context (when effort was appropriate and feedback is informational). The SDT competence need is for effectance experience, not for objective achievement.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Effectance]]*

> [!definition] **Effort Abandonment** **[perm-note]**
> The monitoring-regulation decoupling mechanism in which a comprehension failure is correctly detected, an appropriate fix-up strategy is identified, but the reader declines to deploy it because the perceived effort cost exceeds the perceived value of correction. A motivational regulation failure rather than a monitoring or strategy knowledge failure. Addressed by interventions that explicitly alter the perceived ROI of fix-up strategy deployment.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Effort-Abandonment|Effort Abandonment]]*

> [!definition] **Figure Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The phenomenon that in syllogistic reasoning, the arrangement of terms across the two premises (the "figure" of the syllogism) systematically affects difficulty and error rates, even when the logical form is held constant. The MMT account explains figure effects as consequences of the different mental model constructions induced by different term arrangements — some arrangements make it harder to combine the information from the two premises into a coherent model.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Figure-Effect|Figure Effect]]*

> [!definition] **File Metadata Queries** **[perm-note]**
> These queries access implicit properties automatically maintained by [[Obsidian]] for every file.
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-in-note-metadata-panel-dataview-inline-queries-2025120318* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[File-Metadata-Queries|File Metadata Queries]]*

> [!definition] **Filter Bubble / Information Cocoon** **[perm-note]**
> A metaphor for the self-reinforcing information environment that results when users preferentially consume identity-confirming political content, amplified by algorithmic content personalization. The filter bubble concept (Pariser, 2011) emphasizes algorithmic filtering; the information cocoon concept (Sunstein, 2017) emphasizes voluntary selective exposure. Empirical research has complicated both accounts, finding that cross-cutting information exposure is more common than the metaphors suggest, but that exposure does not reliably produce belief updating.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Filter-Bubble-Information-Cocoon|Filter Bubble / Information Cocoon]]*

> [!definition] **Fix-Up Strategy** **[perm-note]**
> A regulatory action deployed in response to a detected comprehension failure. Fix-up strategies range on a continuum from local (re-reading a sentence) to global (returning to an earlier passage, seeking background knowledge, constructing a summary diagram). Strategy activation failure — the inability to select an appropriate fix-up strategy when a failure has been detected — is one of the three mechanisms of monitoring-regulation decoupling identified in this analysis.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Fix-Up-Strategy|Fix-Up Strategy]]*

> [!definition] **Fixed Mindset** **[perm-note]**
> The belief that intelligence and ability are fixed traits that cannot be substantially changed. Associated in Dweck's framework with challenge-avoidance, ability attribution for failure, and helpless response to difficulty. In this report's reconstruction, "fixed mindset behavior" is better understood as the result of stable-and-uncontrollable failure attributions combined with performance goal orientation, not as the downstream effect of a coherent intelligence-immutability belief.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Fixed-Mindset|Fixed Mindset]]*

> [!definition] **Flavell's Metacognitive Framework** **[perm-note]**
> Flavell distinguished **[[Metacognitive-Knowledge]]** (stable declarative knowledge about persons, tasks, and strategies — knowing that re-reading is an ineffective consolidation strategy, for instance) from **[[Metacognitive-Experiences]]** (in-the-moment feelings of knowing, confusion, or fluency that trigger regulatory responses during active learning). This distinction is foundational to SRL theory: metacognitive knowledge provides the repertoire from which the learner selects strategies; metacognitive experiences are the monitoring signals that determine when regulation is needed. A le...
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Flavell's-Metacognitive-Framework|Flavell's Metacognitive Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Fleshing Out** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which an initially minimal mental model is enriched with background knowledge from long-term memory to represent additional properties of the described situation. Fleshing out is necessary for realistic comprehension and everyday inference, but it also introduces the possibility of model enrichment from incorrect or biased background beliefs. Distinguished from schema activation in that fleshing out produces a unique representational token for a specific situation rather than importing a pre-stored template.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Fleshing-Out|Fleshing Out]]*

> [!definition] **Fluency Illusion** **[perm-note]**
> The tendency to interpret ease of cognitive processing as evidence of deep learning or durable retention. When material is recently studied, familiar, or clearly presented, it processes easily — generating a signal that metacognitive monitoring typically interprets as indicating good learning. In reality, processing fluency reflects short-term accessibility, not long-term retention. The fluency illusion is the primary mechanism behind the ineffectiveness of passive re-reading as a study strategy.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Fluency-Illusion|Fluency Illusion]]*

> [!definition] **The Fluency Illusion** **[perm-note]**
> The systematic tendency of learners to misread processing fluency — how easily material is read or processed — as evidence of comprehension and retention. High fluency during study predicts overconfidence but not retention; effortful, disfluent processing predicts better retention despite feeling less productive.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Fluency-Illusion|The Fluency Illusion]]*

> [!definition] **Fluency Trap** **[perm-note]**
> The mechanism by which reading fluency — the smooth, automatic processing of text at the lexical and syntactic levels — suppresses comprehension monitoring signals at the situational coherence level. Arises because the metacognitive system uses processing fluency as a proxy for comprehension success, generating false-positive comprehension signals when fluent decoding coexists with situation-model incoherence. A specific instantiation of the [[Fluency-Illusion]] in the context of comprehension monitoring.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Fluency-Trap|Fluency Trap]]*

> [!definition] **The Fluency Trap** **[perm-note]**
> The metacognitive error in which the subjective fluency of a writing activity is mistaken for evidence of strong encoding. Summarisation and note-taking from open sources are the primary fluency traps in the writing-to-learn domain, because they produce fluent output that reflects the source's structure rather than the learner's knowledge transformation.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Fluency-Trap|The Fluency Trap]]*

> [!definition] **Fluency-Illusion|Fluency Illusion** **[perm-note]**
> The metacognitive error of conflating the subjective ease of processing (processing fluency) with actual comprehension depth. In reading, speed-trained readers often experience high processing fluency — smooth, fast reading — while their situation model construction has degraded. The illusion is produced because monitoring failure prevents awareness of the comprehension deficit.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Fluency-IllusionFluency-Illusion|Fluency-Illusion|Fluency Illusion]]*

> [!definition] **Forethought Phase** **[perm-note]**
> The first phase of Zimmerman's cyclical model, encompassing task analysis (goal setting and strategic planning) and self-motivational beliefs (self-efficacy, outcome expectations, task interest, goal orientation). Sets the standard and strategy for the subsequent performance phase.
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Forethought-Phase|Forethought Phase]]*

> [!definition] **Formative Assessment** **[perm-note]**
> Assessment activity carried out by teachers and/or by students that provides information to be used as feedback to modify the teaching and learning activities in which they are engaged. The defining feature is *real-time instructional adjustment*: information gathered is used to adapt the learning process while it is still ongoing, in contrast to summative assessment which reports learning after the fact. Formative assessment is distinguished not by its format but by its *function* — the same quiz can be formative or summative depending on whether its results shape what happens next.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Formative-Assessment|Formative Assessment]]*

> [!definition] **Formative Feedback** **[perm-note]**
> Feedback provided *during* a learning episode, while the learner still has time and opportunity to act on it and close the gap between current and desired performance. Contrasted with summative feedback, which reports performance after the fact. The defining feature is not format or content but *functional timing*: it must arrive when it can still influence the course of learning.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Formative-Feedback|Formative Feedback]]*

> [!definition] **Four-Component Instructional Design / 4C/ID** **[perm-note]**
> An instructional design model for complex skills, grounded in CLT, specifying four components: (1) whole-task learning tasks; (2) supportive information (schema-building); (3) procedural information (just-in-time guidance for routine procedures); (4) part-task practice (for automating critical components). The model operationalizes CLT principles for complex vocational and professional training domains and represents the most elaborated CLT-derived design framework.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Four-Component-Instructional-Design-4CID|Four-Component Instructional Design / 4C/ID]]*

> [!definition] **Frederic Bartlett** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: British psychologist whose *Remembering* (1932) established that memory is reconstructive and culturally guided. Coined the term "schema" in its psychological sense. Distinguished from the later formalization tradition: Bartlett's schemas were culturally embedded, socially constituted, and involved in active reconstruction — not abstract, amodal data structures. His original data showed cultural specificity; the formalization tradition stripped this dimension.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: Founding figure whose work was transformed in transmission — the cultural critique...
> 
> *Attribution: 1969*
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Frederic-Bartlett|Frederic Bartlett]]*

> [!definition] **Free-Choice Behaviour** **[perm-note]**
> The dependent variable most commonly used in CET research to measure intrinsic motivation: the amount of time a person voluntarily spends with an activity when they are not required to do so, typically measured during a period when the experimenter is absent and no external contingencies are operating. Free-choice behaviour is considered a more direct behavioural measure of intrinsic motivation than self-reported interest, because it does not depend on verbal self-report. However, it also has limitations: it may reflect habit or accessibility rather than intrinsic motivation, and it is diff...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Free-Choice-Behaviour|Free-Choice Behaviour]]*

> [!definition] **Functional Integration** **[perm-note]**
> The organisation of domain knowledge in ways that make it productively usable — not merely stored but structured so as to enable inference, prediction, error-detection, and analogical extension. Functional integration is contrasted with information storage: the same facts can be stored without integration (rote memorisation) or integrated in ways that generate new outputs. Understanding, on the functional integration account, is about the *organisation* of knowledge rather than its *content*.
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Functional-Integration|Functional Integration]]*

> [!definition] **Functional Psychology** **[perm-note]**
> The approach to psychology that emphasizes the adaptive functions of mental processes and consciousness in enabling organisms to survive and thrive in their environments, rather than merely analyzing the structure or contents of mental states. James's functionalism contrasted sharply with [[structuralism]] practiced by [[Edward Titchener]] and instead aligned with [[Darwinian]] evolutionary principles by treating psychological capacities as solutions to adaptive problems.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-william-james* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Functional-Psychology|Functional Psychology]]*

> [!definition] **Functional Significance** **[perm-note]**
> The psychological meaning an event carries for the person who experiences it, specifically with respect to two possible aspects: the **controlling aspect** (the degree to which the event is experienced as pressuring the person to behave in a particular way, implying that the event is managing or directing behaviour) and the **informational aspect** (the degree to which the event is experienced as providing competence-relevant feedback, conveying information about how well one is doing relative to a standard). Every external event — reward, praise, deadline, feedback, surveillance — carries ...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Functional-Significance|Functional Significance]]*

## _G

> [!definition] **General Causality Orientations Scale** **[perm-note]**
> The primary measurement instrument for causality orientations. Presents 12 vignettes of motivationally relevant situations, each with three response options keyed to autonomy, control, and impersonal orientations. Respondents rate each option on a 7-point scale (non-ipsative), yielding three independent subscale scores. Reliability coefficients typically .70-.82. Validated across multiple cultural contexts with generally consistent factor structure.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[General-Causality-Orientations-Scale|General Causality Orientations Scale]]*

> [!definition] **The Generation Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The robust finding that memory for material is superior when the learner generates the material (completes a partial prompt, creates an example, explains in their own words) compared to when they simply read or copy the material. The generation advantage is not merely about effort — it is about the specific cognitive processes triggered by the need to produce rather than consume. Generation activates semantic and associative networks in ways that passive reading does not.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Generation-Effect|The Generation Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Generative Cognition Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> A structural configuration of four cognitive conditions — freed output channels, self-paced recursive engagement, private hypothesis space, and active metacognitive monitoring — that together make evaluative, analytical cognition cognitively affordable. Proposed in this analysis as the unifying framework for understanding why silent reading (and analogous practices) produces critical analysis under some conditions but not others.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Generative-Cognition-Architecture|Generative Cognition Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **The Generative Constraint** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that a writing technique's learning yield is proportional to the extent it forces the learner to generate, rather than reproduce, cognitive content. Generation requires active retrieval from long-term memory, construction of connections between ideas, or production of explanations, examples, or applications that cannot simply be copied from available input. Techniques high on the generative constraint produce durable learning; techniques low on it produce accurate records with poor retention.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Generative-Constraint|The Generative Constraint]]*

> [!definition] **Generative Learning Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The theoretical framework holding that meaningful learning occurs through the active generation of relationships between new information and existing knowledge structures. Learning is not passive reception but active construction: the learner must generate the connections, inferences, elaborations, and integrations that give new material meaning. Writing facilitates generative learning insofar as it functions as a *generative activity* — one that requires the learner to produce rather than reproduce, to construct rather than transcribe.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Generative-Learning-Theory|Generative Learning Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Generative Processing** **[perm-note]**
> Cognitive processing that requires the learner to produce — rather than reproduce — connections, inferences, elaborations, and integrations between new information and existing knowledge structures. The active ingredient in learning activities that produce durable encoding.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Generative-Processing|Generative Processing]]*

> [!definition] **The Generativity Diagnostic** **[perm-note]**
> An original framework from this analysis for evaluating any writing technique's learning potential across four dimensions: source status (closed vs. available), organisation authority (writer-generated vs. source-provided), connection mandate (connections required vs. recording only), predictive commitment (commitment required vs. optional).
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Generativity-Diagnostic|The Generativity Diagnostic]]*

> [!definition] **Germane Cognitive Load** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive load devoted to schema construction and automation — the effortful cognitive processes that directly produce durable learning. Unlike extraneous load, germane load is desirable. Unlike intrinsic load, germane load can be influenced by instructional design. Activities that prompt learners to actively construct, extend, and integrate schemas (e.g., self-explanation, variability practice, completion problems) are theorized to increase germane load. This is the "productive" load and the most theoretically contested component of the framework.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Germane-Cognitive-Load|Germane Cognitive Load]]*

> [!definition] **Germane Cognitive Load (Sweller, van Merriënboer & Paas, 1998) — Original Formulation** **[perm-note]**
> In the original three-component model, germane load referred to the cognitive effort specifically dedicated to schema formation and automation — the "good" load associated with active learning processes. The idea was that once extraneous load was minimized, designers could deliberately increase germane load (e.g., by introducing variability, by requiring self-explanation) to promote deeper schema formation. This construct became theoretically problematic (see Phase III) and was reconceptualized around 2010: subsequent accounts treat what was called germane load as a resource allocation desc...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Germane-Cognitive-Load-Sweller,-van-Merriënboer-&-Paas,-1998-—-Original-Formulat|Germane Cognitive Load (Sweller, van Merriënboer & Paas, 1998) — Original Formulation]]*

> [!definition] **Git-Based Workflow** **[perm-note]**
> **Branch-per-agent pattern**:
> ```
> main
> ├── feature/payment-system
> │   ├── feature/payment-system-requirements (requirements-analyst)
> │   ├── feature/payment-system-architecture (system-architect)
> │   └── feature/payment-system-implementation (backend-engineer)
> ```
> 
> Each agent works in its own branch, main agent merges when complete.
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Git-Based-Workflow|Git-Based Workflow]]*

> [!definition] **Ego Involvement** **[perm-note]**
> Ego involvement is a motivational state in which the person's primary concern is demonstrating superior ability — or, critically, avoiding the demonstration of inferior ability — relative to others. In this state, normative social comparison is the operative standard. Effort becomes a double-edged sword: it is necessary for success, but high effort combined with poor relative performance is damning evidence of low ability. Ego involvement is associated with defensive strategies, challenge avoidance (because failure is more costly), and vulnerability when outcomes are unfavourable.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Ego-Involvement|Ego Involvement]]*

> [!definition] **Ego-Involving Feedback** **[perm-note]**
> Feedback that triggers self-evaluation processes by implicating the learner's identity, worth, or ability. Ego involvement is produced by person-directed language, normative social comparisons, and high-stakes evaluative contexts. Under ego-involved conditions, learners tend to process feedback through the lens of self-protection rather than learning, prioritising consistent self-image over accurate performance information.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Ego-Involving-Feedback|Ego-Involving Feedback]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Content** **[perm-note]**
> Goal content refers to the *qualitative nature* of what a personal goal is *about* — the kind of outcome or state it aims toward. Goal Contents Theory specifically distinguishes between **intrinsic goal content** (outcomes that are considered directly satisfying and that tend to satisfy [[Basic-Psychological-Needs]] as a matter of their inherent nature) and **extrinsic goal content** (outcomes that are primarily valued for the social approval, comparative advantage, or material rewards they bring, rather than for their direct satisfaction of psychological needs).
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Goal-Content|Goal Content]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Content Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A sub-theory of [[Self-Determination-Theory]] proposing that personal goals differ in their qualitative content — what they aim toward — and that this qualitative dimension independently predicts psychological well-being. Distinguishes intrinsic goal content (personal growth, meaningful relationships, community contribution, physical health) from extrinsic goal content (financial success, fame, attractive appearance), with the hypothesis that intrinsic goal emphasis supports and extrinsic goal emphasis undermines the satisfaction of basic psychological needs.
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Goal-Content-Theory|Goal Content Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Framing** **[perm-note]**
> The language and rationale used to describe the *purpose* of a goal or task, independent of the task's objective content. Intrinsic goal framing emphasizes personal growth, interest, understanding, or relational value; extrinsic goal framing emphasizes grades, external evaluation, career advancement, or social comparison. Experimental manipulation of goal framing has been shown to causally affect learning depth, motivation quality, and transfer performance (Vansteenkiste et al., 2004, 2007).
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Goal-Framing|Goal Framing]]*

> [!definition] **Goal Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> A motivational construct distinguishing between mastery/learning goals (seeking to develop competence) and performance goals (seeking to demonstrate competence). Mastery goals promote deeper processing, persistence after failure, challenge-seeking, and long-term skill development. Performance goals promote performance under low-difficulty conditions but often produce avoidance under threat. Identified in this report as the proximate motivational variable mediating mindset effects.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Goal-Orientation|Goal Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Growth Mindset** **[perm-note]**
> The belief that intelligence and ability are malleable attributes that can be developed through effort, effective strategy, and persistence. Associated in Dweck's framework with challenge-seeking, effort attribution, and mastery-oriented response to failure. Contrasted with fixed mindset. As analyzed in this report: a culturally influential packaging of attribution-theory insights about failure response, with overstated causal claims and an underspecified mechanism.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Growth-Mindset|Growth Mindset]]*

> [!definition] **Guidance Fading Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The CLT-derived instructional principle prescribing that the level of instructional scaffolding and support should decrease progressively as learner expertise grows, to avoid the expertise reversal effect. Operationalized most concretely in the [[faded worked example]] paradigm, where successive problems in a sequence include progressively fewer completed steps, tracking the learner's schema development. Supported by empirical research on faded examples and consistent with the expertise reversal mechanism.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Guidance-Fading-Principle|Guidance Fading Principle]]*

## _I

> [!definition] **ICAP Framework** **[perm-note]**
> The [[ICAP-Framework]] classifies cognitive activities by their depth and the nature of learner engagement: Passive (receiving information without overt activity), Active (manipulating information physically without necessarily changing it mentally), Constructive (generating new knowledge beyond what was presented), and Interactive (engaging in dialogue that produces knowledge through socio-cognitive conflict). Each mode predicts different learning outcomes, with Constructive and Interactive modes producing significantly better understanding and transfer. Silent reading is *not* automatical...
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[ICAP-Framework|ICAP Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Identified Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The regulatory style in which the learner consciously values the goal and engages with the activity because they judge it personally important, even if it is not intrinsically interesting. Identified regulation is classified as autonomous because the source of regulation is the learner's own evaluative judgment. A student who studies for a required course because they genuinely see its value for their goals — even if they find the specific material dry — is exhibiting identified regulation.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Identified-Regulation|Identified Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Identified Techniques** **[perm-note]**
> 
> 
> *Source: reference-analysis-cog-psy-theories-of-attention-202512140013-2025121404* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Identified-Techniques|Identified Techniques]]*

> [!definition] **Identified-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> Behavior regulated by conscious valuing — the person has endorsed the goal or activity as personally important, even if it is not intrinsically enjoyable. The [[Perceived-Internal-Locus-of-Causality|locus of causality]] has shifted inward: the person acts because *they* see the value, not because an external agent rewards them for it or because they fear internal sanction. Identified regulation is typically associated with substantially better outcomes than introjection — greater persistence, higher quality engagement, and better psychological wellbeing.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Identified-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Tight-Loop Assumption** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The implicit architectural commitment in the [[Zimmerman-SRL-Model]] that the three phases form a *functionally integrated* feedback loop in which information flows reliably from Performance Phase monitoring through Self-Reflection Phase evaluation to Forethought Phase modification. The assumption is architectural (embedded in the circular representation of the model) rather than propositional (stated as a testable claim). The dialectical process shows this assumption does not survive scrutiny in its strong form; a conditional version — that tight coupling is *possible* give...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Tight-Loop-Assumption|Tight-Loop Assumption]]*

> [!definition] **Illusion of Explanatory Depth** **[perm-note]**
> A cognitive phenomenon documented by Rozenblit and Keil (2002): people systematically overestimate their understanding of causal/mechanical systems before attempting to explain them. When asked to explain in detail, the overestimation collapses. The illusion arises because familiarity with the *function* of a system (what it does) is mistaken for understanding of its *mechanism* (how and why it does it). The phenomenon demonstrates that the *feeling* of understanding is an unreliable guide to its presence.
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Illusion-of-Explanatory-Depth|Illusion of Explanatory Depth]]*

> [!definition] **Impersonal Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> The tendency to orient toward the environment as if one's behavior were beyond intentional control — as if outcomes are determined by forces (internal or external) that cannot be managed. Individuals high in impersonal orientation characteristically experience themselves as incapable of producing desired outcomes, attribute performance to luck, fate, or overwhelming circumstances, and often disengage from effortful pursuit. Impersonal orientation is theoretically associated with [[Amotivation]] in OIT's motivational continuum and with [[Learned-Helplessness]] in Seligman's framework. Unlike...
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Impersonal-Orientation|Impersonal Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Implications and Consequences** **[perm-note]**
> **Implications** are what logically follows from your reasoning—the conclusions that must be accepted if you accept your premises and reasoning. **Consequences** are the likely results or effects that would occur if your conclusions were acted upon. Together they answer: *If I accept this reasoning, what else follows? What would happen if I acted on these conclusions?* Tracing implications and consequences projects reasoning forward to examine what it commits you to.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-fundamental-structures-of-reasoning-202512050320* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Implications-and-Consequences|Implications and Consequences]]*

> [!definition] **Implicit Theory of Intelligence** **[perm-note]**
> A lay person's intuitive, largely non-conscious theory about the nature of intelligence and ability. Unlike explicit, carefully reasoned positions, implicit theories are often tacit — operating in the background of cognition without being consciously articulated. They are called "implicit" not because they are unconscious in the Freudian sense but because people rarely surface them to deliberate examination. Research shows these theories powerfully predict behavior under conditions of challenge, setback, and evaluation.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Implicit-Theory-of-Intelligence|Implicit Theory of Intelligence]]*

> [!definition] **Incidental Learning** **[perm-note]**
> Learning that occurs without explicit intent to memorize, typically studied using cover stories that direct participants' attention away from any awareness that a memory test will follow. LOP research demonstrated that incidental semantic learners often remember as much as intentional learners, supporting the claim that processing depth, not intent, is the primary determinant of retention.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Incidental-Learning|Incidental Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Incremental Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The belief that ability is malleable — capable of development through effort, strategy, and learning. Under incremental theory, performance outcomes are *feedback about current level of competence*, not diagnostic verdicts about fixed traits. Failure is informative rather than definitional; effort is a path to growth rather than evidence of low ability. Incremental theorists tend to seek challenge, persist in the face of difficulty, and process negative feedback as useful data.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Incremental-Theory|Incremental Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Incremental Theory of Intelligence** **[perm-note]**
> Dweck's technical term for the belief that intelligence is malleable and develops through effort. The theoretical basis for growth mindset interventions. This report finds the evidence for its cross-domain stability and its causal independence from experience to be weak.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Incremental-Theory-of-Intelligence|Incremental Theory of Intelligence]]*

> [!definition] **Inductive Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> A form of inference that moves from particular observations or instances to general conclusions, principles, or hypotheses. Unlike deductive reasoning, which guarantees its conclusions if its premises are true, inductive reasoning produces conclusions that are *probable* rather than *certain* given the evidence. The strength of an inductive argument is a matter of degree — it can be stronger or weaker — and even a maximally strong inductive argument can, in principle, be overturned by new evidence. Inductive reasoning is also called *ampliative* inference because the conclusion contains inf...
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inductive-Reasoning|Inductive Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **Infallibilism** **[perm-note]**
> The contrasting view that genuine knowledge requires certainty — that a knower can only be said to *know* something if their justification is strong enough to exclude the possibility of error. On this view, if there is any chance (however remote) that a belief is false, it does not qualify as knowledge in the strict sense. [[Descartes]]'s method of doubt in the *Meditations* (1641) is the classical expression: he withholds the label "knowledge" from any belief that can be doubted. Many ordinary language uses of "know" implicitly invoke infallibilist standards.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Infallibilism]]*

> [!definition] **Inference** **[perm-note]**
> Inference is the cognitive act of drawing conclusions that go beyond what is explicitly stated, using available evidence and reasoning to arrive at justified beliefs. In education, inference involves identifying what follows from the evidence, recognising alternative interpretations, and understanding the degree of certainty warranted by the evidence at hand. Inference is not guessing — it is reasoned extension from known to unknown, constrained by the quality of available evidence.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Inference to the Best Explanation** **[perm-note]**
> The analytic philosophy reconstruction of Peircean abduction, understood as the inferential practice of selecting the hypothesis that, if true, would best explain the observed data according to criteria including explanatory power, fit with background knowledge, simplicity, and what Lipton calls "loveliness" (depth of illumination). IBE is simultaneously a description of how reasoners actually infer and a normative standard for how they should infer. Subject to the *bad lot problem* (van Fraassen): even the best available hypothesis may not be good in any absolute sense if the pool of candi...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inference-to-the-Best-Explanation|Inference to the Best Explanation]]*

> [!definition] **Inferential Bridging** **[perm-note]**
> The generation of implicit inferences needed to establish coherence between textual propositions that are not directly connected by explicit linguistic devices. A core component of situation model construction, requiring activation of world knowledge and causal reasoning. Inferential bridging demand is the primary determinant of text difficulty beyond the sentence level.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inferential-Bridging|Inferential Bridging]]*

> [!definition] **Informational Feedback** **[perm-note]**
> Feedback whose primary functional significance is informational — it conveys genuine competence-relevant information to the recipient in a context where PLOC remains internal. Informational feedback is characterised by specificity (pointing to particular aspects of performance), mastery-referencing (comparing performance to personal standards or task standards rather than to other people), process orientation (attributing performance to strategies and effort rather than global ability), and non-controlling language (describing rather than evaluating). CET predicts that informational feedbac...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Informational-Feedback|Informational Feedback]]*

> [!definition] **Thinking Tag Semantics** **[perm-note]**
> **[Thinking-Tag-Semantics**:: XML tags (`<thinking>...</thinking>`) that mark content boundaries for differential processing - content within thinking tags treated as internal reasoning subject to logic optimization rather than presentation polish, exempt from brevity pressures, and hidden from standard user interfaces.]**
> 
> *Source: prompt-report-advanced-thinking-and-reasoning-tags-in-claude-202601070811* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Thinking-Tag-Semantics|Thinking Tag Semantics]]*

> [!definition] **Inoculation Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A framework for building cognitive resistance to misinformation, modeled on medical vaccination. Pre-emptive exposure to weakened forms of misinformation techniques — accompanied by explicit identification of the persuasion tactic employed — generates cognitive "antibodies" that reduce susceptibility to subsequent misinformation encounters. Inoculation theory grounds "prebunking" interventions, which aim to expose manipulation tactics before they are used, rather than "debunking" interventions, which correct misinformation after belief has formed.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inoculation-Theory|Inoculation Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Inquiry-Based Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A family of pedagogical approaches in which students are the primary agents of inquiry: they formulate or are presented with questions, generate hypotheses, design or conduct investigations, and draw conclusions from evidence.
