IBL is grounded philosophically in Dewey's *How We Think* (1910) and developed empirically through decades of science education research. In its most abductive instantiation, IBL deliberately creates anomalies and withholds pre-formed explanations to ensure that students engage in genuine hypothesis generation rather than simply verifying known outcomes.
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Inquiry-Based-Learning|Inquiry-Based Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Instance Theory Core Mechanism** **[perm-note]**
> Instance theory proposes that each encounter with a stimulus creates a separate memory trace (instance) that can be retrieved in response to subsequent encounters. With sufficient practice, memory retrieval of solutions from past instances becomes faster than executing the algorithm de novo, leading to a race between algorithmic processing and memory retrieval. When memory consistently wins this race, performance appears automatic.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-attentional-efficiency-and-skill-aquisition* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Instance-Theory-Core-Mechanism|Instance Theory Core Mechanism]]*

> [!definition] **Integrated Cognitive Personhood** **[perm-note]**
> The emergent insight of Report 15: the claim that the examined life does not add cognitive capabilities to a pre-existing person but constitutes personhood in its fullest expression. When the fifteen dimensions of the Architecture of the Examined Life framework operate concurrently and fluidly — inquiry activation, fallibilist epistemology, autonomous motivation, constructive attentiveness, intellectual virtue, metacognitive governance, self-directed development, embodied awareness, emotional calibration, practical wisdom, social epistemic virtue, predictive flexibility, and narrative coher...
> 
> *Source: report-15-the-examined-life-revisited* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Integrated-Cognitive-Personhood|Integrated Cognitive Personhood]]*

> [!definition] **Integrated-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The most autonomous form of extrinsic motivation. The person has assimilated the identified value into a coherent sense of self — the activity is pursued not merely because it is valued in isolation but because it coheres with the person's broader values, goals, and sense of identity. Integration differs from identification in its *systemic* character: identified regulation can be isolated and context-specific, while integrated regulation is woven into the self-concept as a whole. Integration is associated with the best outcomes short of intrinsic motivation and, unlike intrinsic motivation...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Integrated-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Integration** **[perm-note]**
> The fullest form of internalization, in which an externally-sourced regulation has been fully assimilated into the person's coherent sense of self — their identity, values, and other regulations. Integration is not merely endorsing a value as important (that is identification); it is the synthesis of that value with the whole person. Integrated values do not feel like additions to the self; they feel like expressions of it.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Integration]]*

> [!definition] **Integration-Paradox** **[perm-note]**
> The structural paradox within OIT in which genuine identification with an externally sourced regulation requires a prior base of felt autonomy — the very resource that controlled motivational environments deplete. This paradox implies that motivation change cannot begin at the internalization step but must first attend to autonomy restoration.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Integration-Paradox]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual Humility** **[perm-note]**
> An intellectual virtue consisting in the accurate recognition of one's epistemic limitations, susceptibility to error, and dependence on others for knowledge. Intellectual humility is the dispositional correlate of fallibilism: if we could be wrong, intellectual integrity demands that we hold beliefs with appropriate tentativeness, acknowledge the force of opposing arguments, and remain genuinely open to revision. Distinct from intellectual timidity (excessive self-doubt) and intellectual arrogance (unwarranted certainty).
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intellectual-Humility|Intellectual Humility]]*

> [!definition] **Intellectual Standards** **[perm-note]**
> A set of criteria — clarity, accuracy, precision, relevance, depth, breadth, logic, significance, and fairness — used to evaluate the quality of reasoning. Applying intellectual standards to the elements of reasoning (purpose, question, information, inference, concepts, assumptions, implications, perspective) constitutes the core act of critical evaluation in the Paul-Elder framework. The standards are domain-transferable, though their application requires domain knowledge.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intellectual-Standards|Intellectual Standards]]*

> [!definition] **Internalisation** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which externally regulated behaviours are gradually taken in and re-regulated by the self — moving along the self-determination continuum from external regulation toward identified regulation and ultimately integrated regulation. While CET specifically addresses intrinsic motivation (the far end of the intrinsic pole), Organismic Integration Theory (another SDT sub-theory) addresses internalisation of extrinsic motivation. The two processes are related: creating conditions that support autonomy and competence facilitates both intrinsic motivation (when activities are inherent...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Internalisation]]*

> [!definition] **Internalism** **[perm-note]**
> The position that the intrinsic, physical properties of a system's internal states — specifically, states inside the skin or skull of the organism — are both necessary and sufficient to determine the content and character of mental states. Internalism is the target of both content externalism (Putnam, Burge) and vehicle externalism (Clark, Chalmers). NOT the same as claiming that nothing external is causally relevant to cognition — only that the cognitive system itself is internally bounded.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Internalism]]*

> [!definition] **Internalization** **[perm-note]**
> The broad process of taking in a value, rule, or regulation from the social environment and making it one's own to varying degrees. Internalization spans the entire range from introjection to integration — it is the overarching process, not a single outcome. One can internalize a value incompletely (introjection), partially (identification), or fully (integration).
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Internalization]]*

> [!definition] **Internalization Continuum** **[perm-note]**
> SDT's model of how external regulation is progressively taken over by internal regulatory processes, from external regulation (behaviour driven by external rewards/punishments) through introjection (partially internalised but controlled by internal pressure/guilt), identification (valued and self-endorsed), integration (fully assimilated into the self), to intrinsic motivation (activity is intrinsically rewarding). The reconstruction treats this as an empirically supported graded ordering while contesting its characterisation as a strict continuum with intrinsic motivation as its endpoint.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Internalization-Continuum|Internalization Continuum]]*

> [!definition] **The Internalization Continuum** **[perm-note]**
> [[Organismic-Integration-Theory]] describes how externally prompted behavior can be taken in and made progressively more self-determined. The continuum runs from [[External-Regulation|external regulation]] (compliance to obtain reward or avoid punishment) through [[Introjected-Regulation|introjected regulation]] (taking in but not accepting external pressure; ego-involvement, "should") to [[Identified-Regulation|identified regulation]] (endorsing the value of an activity even if not intrinsically enjoyable) to [[Integrated-Regulation|integrated regulation]] (full congruence of the activity'...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Internalization-Continuum|The Internalization Continuum]]*

> [!definition] **Internalization-Continuum** **[perm-note]**
> The progression from external regulation through introjection, identification, and integration to intrinsic motivation — not a spectrum of motivation amounts but a spectrum of degrees of integration between a regulatory process and the self-system. Position on the continuum reflects both the phenomenological locus of causality and the degree of structural integration of the regulation into the self.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Internalization-Continuum]]*

> [!definition] **Interpretation and Inference** **[perm-note]**
> [**Inference**:: is the intellectual act of drawing conclusions from information and reasoning.] [**Interpretation**:: is the process of making sense of information by placing it within conceptual frameworks.] Together, they represent the *active cognitive work* of reasoning—<span style='color: #ff6e00;'>moving from premises to conclusions, from data to understanding, from observations to explanations</span>. Inference answers: *What conclusions am I drawing? How am I interpreting this information?*
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-fundamental-structures-of-reasoning-202512050320* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Interpretation-and-Inference|Interpretation and Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic Cognitive Load** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive load imposed by the inherent complexity of the material to be learned, determined by the number and degree of interconnection among elements that must be simultaneously processed. Intrinsic load cannot be reduced by instructional design without changing the material itself or changing the learner's prior knowledge (which reduces element interactivity by automating some elements as schemas). For a given learner at a given expertise level, intrinsic load is essentially fixed. This is the "necessary" load of learning.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intrinsic-Cognitive-Load|Intrinsic Cognitive Load]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic Goal Content** **[perm-note]**
> The four canonical intrinsic goal domains are: (1) **Personal growth** — developing oneself, learning new things, increasing self-understanding and psychological depth; (2) **Meaningful relationships** — building deep, authentic, mutually supportive connections with others; (3) **Community contribution** — working to make a positive difference in one's broader community or society; and (4) **Physical health and fitness** — maintaining and developing one's physical vitality and well-being. What unites these four domains, according to GCT, is that pursuing them tends to directly satisfy the b...
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intrinsic-Goal-Content|Intrinsic Goal Content]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> The motivation to engage in an activity for its inherent interest and enjoyment, rather than for separable external rewards or to avoid punishment. Intrinsically motivated learners are characterised by spontaneous curiosity, willingness to take on challenges, and sustained engagement. SDT posits that intrinsic motivation is neither rare nor fragile by nature — it is the natural state of healthy psychological functioning — but it is highly sensitive to the social conditions in which learning occurs, including, critically, the design and delivery of feedback.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intrinsic-Motivation|Intrinsic Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Intrinsic-Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> Engagement driven by inherent satisfaction in the activity itself — the activity is its own reward. This is the form of motivation that first drove Deci and Ryan's research program, and it remains the paradigm case of self-determined motivation. However, [[Cognitive-Evaluation-Theory]] established that intrinsic motivation is fragile: it can be undermined by the wrong kinds of external events. This fragility is central to understanding the practical implications of the continuum.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Intrinsic-Motivation]]*

> [!definition] **Introjected Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The regulatory style in which the learner is driven by internalized pressures: guilt, shame, ego-threat, or the need to maintain contingent self-esteem. Introjected regulation is classified as controlled even though the source of pressure is internal, because the learner is not freely choosing engagement — they are responding to internal compulsions that feel obligatory rather than chosen. Students who study primarily to avoid feeling like a failure are exhibiting introjected regulation.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Introjected-Regulation|Introjected Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Introjected-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> Behavior regulated by partially internalized external constraints, now represented *within* the person as ego-involving demands. The person acts to avoid shame, protect [[Self-Esteem|self-esteem]], or maintain conditional approval — but these demands are experienced as pressures, not as genuine expressions of the self. Introjection is the theoretical lynchpin of the internalization analysis: it is the site where external regulation enters the person but does not become part of the self. It is internal in location but external in phenomenological character.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Introjected-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Introjection** **[perm-note]**
> A regulatory style in which an external rule or value has been taken in but not genuinely made one's own — the person regulates themselves through internalized approval-seeking or shame-avoidance. Introjected regulation is internal in *location* (the pressure comes from within) but external in *quality* (its source is an internalized critic rather than a genuine value). Associated with ego-involvement, contingent self-esteem, performance anxiety, and — counterintuitively — psychological costs that may exceed those of external regulation.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Introjection]]*

## _J

> [!definition] **Judgment of Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A metacognitive judgment made during or after study assessing how well particular material has been learned and how likely it is to be remembered later. JOLs are the most extensively studied metacognitive monitoring judgment. Delayed JOLs (made after a brief delay from study) are more accurate than immediate JOLs because they require actual retrieval rather than relying on current processing fluency as a proxy for memory.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Judgment-of-Learning|Judgment of Learning]]*

## _K

> [!definition] **Knowing-That / Knowing-How** **[perm-note]**
> Gilbert Ryle's distinction between two types of knowledge: *knowing-that* (propositional knowledge: knowing that Paris is the capital of France) and *knowing-how* (procedural knowledge: knowing how to ride a bicycle). Ryle argued that knowing-how is not reducible to knowing-that — you cannot derive skilled performance from a set of propositions, however complete. This distinction is directly relevant to the tacit knowledge problem: expert skill represents knowing-how that cannot be converted into knowing-that without loss.
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowing-That-Knowing-How|Knowing-That / Knowing-How]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge of Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> **Knowledge of Cognition** encompasses three subtypes of metacognitive knowledge that inform strategic deployment decisions. This represents the "database" that metacognitive control queries when selecting strategies.
> 
> **Declarative Knowledge**: Awareness of yourself as a learner—your strengths ("I excel at spatial reasoning"), weaknesses ("I struggle with probabilistic thinking"), and preferences ("I need concrete examples before abstraction"). This self-model enables resource pre-allocation: if you know probability is a weakness, you budget extra time and deploy verification strategies wh...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-critical-thinking-metacognitive-regulation* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-of-Cognition|Knowledge of Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge Telling** **[perm-note]**
> A writing process in which the writer retrieves and transcribes existing knowledge without encountering resistance between the content knowledge and the rhetorical demands of the writing situation. Produces fluent output without reorganising the underlying knowledge representation.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Telling|Knowledge Telling]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge Telling vs. Knowledge Transforming** **[perm-note]**
> The foundational distinction in composition research between two fundamentally different writing processes. *Knowledge telling* is the retrieval and transcription of existing knowledge representations — the writer asks "what do I know about this?" and writes what comes to mind. *Knowledge transforming* involves a dialectical interaction between the writer's content knowledge and their rhetorical problem space — writing to an audience, for a purpose, under constraints that force the writer to evaluate, reorganize, and sometimes revise what they believe. Knowledge transforming cannot occur wi...
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Telling-vs.-Knowledge-Transforming|Knowledge Telling vs. Knowledge Transforming]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge Transforming** **[perm-note]**
> A writing process in which the writer's content knowledge is pressed against the rhetorical demands of the writing situation, producing a dialectical interaction that reorganises understanding. Requires some form of communicative purpose that the writer's current knowledge cannot straightforwardly satisfy.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Transforming|Knowledge Transforming]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge-Telling** **[perm-note]**
> A writing strategy in which the writer retrieves and transcribes information already present in working memory or available in external sources, without significantly transforming, reorganising, or extending their understanding. The output accurately reflects pre-existing knowledge; the process adds nothing to it. Novice writers and most conventional note-taking operate in this mode.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Telling]]*

> [!definition] **Knowledge-Transforming** **[perm-note]**
> A writing strategy in which the compositional process itself generates new understanding — through the need to resolve contradictions, fill explanatory gaps, reformulate confusing ideas, or integrate concepts that did not previously connect in the writer's mental model. Expert writers move between the knowledge space and the rhetorical space in a recursive dialogue that transforms both. Learning is a byproduct of this dialectic.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Knowledge-Transforming]]*

## _L

> [!definition] **Elaboration** **[perm-note]**
> Within the semantic processing level, the degree of richness, complexity, and associative spread with which a stimulus is encoded. More elaborated encodings recruit more prior knowledge nodes and create more retrieval pathways. Distinct from processing depth: elaboration modulates trace durability *within* a level, while depth determines the *class* of processing (structural, phonemic, semantic).
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elaboration]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Inference** **[perm-note]**
> An inference generated during reading that goes beyond what is necessary for local text coherence, adding information from the reader's prior knowledge to enrich the mental model. Distinct from bridging inferences (necessary for coherence) and evaluative inferences (assessing text quality or truth). Elaborative inferences require cognitive resources and constructive engagement.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elaborative-Inference|Elaborative Inference]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Interrogation** **[perm-note]**
> A prior knowledge activation strategy in which learners are prompted to answer "Why is this true?" or "How does this make sense given what I know?" for each new fact or proposition encountered. Distinguished from advance organizers by occurring *during* rather than *before* instruction, and by requiring active integration rather than providing scaffolding.
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elaborative-Interrogation|Elaborative Interrogation]]*

> [!definition] **Elaborative Rehearsal** **[perm-note]**
> Processing that increases depth by connecting a stimulus to existing knowledge, generating implications, creating associative links, or embedding it in meaningful context. In contrast to maintenance rehearsal, elaborative rehearsal moves processing down (deeper) the levels-of-processing continuum and produces meaningfully improved long-term retention. This distinction between the two types of rehearsal is one of LOP's most practically significant contributions.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elaborative-Rehearsal|Elaborative Rehearsal]]*

> [!definition] **Late Selection** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #27FF00;'>The theoretical position that selection operates **after** complete perceptual and semantic processing. All information is analyzed for meaning; only the most pertinent information enters conscious awareness and working memory. Unattended stimuli influence processing implicitly even without conscious recognition.</span>
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-evolution-of-selective-attention-theory-202512142019* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Late-Selection|Late Selection]]*

> [!definition] **Learning Contracts** **[perm-note]**
> Negotiated agreements between learner and facilitator specifying learning goals, strategies and resources, evidence of learning, and evaluation criteria. The primary pedagogical instrument for operationalising SDL process in formal educational settings, learning contracts externalise and make visible the planning and goal-setting activities that natural SDL performs implicitly. Effective learning contracts are genuinely negotiated rather than merely approved; require iterative revision as learning proceeds; and are evaluated against jointly agreed criteria rather than normative benchmarks. ...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Learning-Contracts|Learning Contracts]]*

> [!definition] **Learning Projects** **[perm-note]**
> A concept from Allen Tough's empirical research designating any deliberate effort lasting at least seven hours to gain and retain specific knowledge or skills. Research using structured interviews found that average adults undertake roughly five to ten learning projects per year, approximately seventy percent of which are self-planned rather than institutionally directed. Learning projects data established the empirical foundation for the claim that SDL is not merely an educational ideal but a description of how the majority of adult learning actually occurs.
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Learning-Projects|Learning Projects]]*

> [!definition] **Learning Session Overview** **[perm-note]**
> **Topic**: <% tp.frontmatter.learning_objective %>
> **Learning Stage**: <% tp.frontmatter.learning_stage %>
> **Comprehension Level**: <% "⭐".repeat(tp.frontmatter.comprehension_level) %>
> **Quality**: <% tp.frontmatter.conversation_quality %>
> 
> *Source: reference-technical-advanced-templater-note-templates-20251118225816* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Learning-Session-Overview|Learning Session Overview]]*

> [!definition] **Element Interactivity** **[perm-note]**
> A property of instructional material describing the degree to which its component elements must be processed simultaneously rather than sequentially for understanding to occur. High element interactivity material (e.g., understanding how grammar rules interact to produce a complex sentence; understanding how enzyme kinetics depends on simultaneous knowledge of substrate concentration, inhibitor presence, and pH) imposes high intrinsic cognitive load because the elements cannot be meaningfully separated. Low element interactivity material (e.g., memorizing vocabulary definitions, learning a ...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Element-Interactivity|Element Interactivity]]*

> [!definition] **Elenchus** **[perm-note]**
> The Socratic method of cross-examination: a systematic procedure in which the Questioner draws out the interlocutor's commitments through apparently innocent questions, then demonstrates that those commitments, taken together, generate a contradiction or inadequacy. The elenchus does not refute from the outside; it shows the interlocutor that their own beliefs are internally inconsistent. Vlastos distinguishes the *elenchus* (which aims at exposing contradictions) from the *maieutic method* (which aims at helping the interlocutor give birth to insight).
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Elenchus]]*

> [!definition] **Teleological Directionality** **[perm-note]**
> The implicit or explicit assumption in a developmental theory that development has a natural endpoint toward which it tends under appropriate conditions. In OIT, the teleological directionality is the assumption that internalization naturally tends toward integration. Philosophically, teleological directionality in developmental theories should be examined for: (1) the degree to which the "natural endpoint" is empirically demonstrated vs. normatively prescribed; (2) whether the endpoint reflects universal human development or the theorist's cultural context; and (3) whether the directionali...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Teleological-Directionality|Teleological Directionality]]*

> [!definition] **Levels of Processing** **[perm-note]**
> A theoretical framework proposing that stimuli can be analyzed at multiple levels of abstraction — from shallow physical/structural features to deep semantic/meaningful features — and that the level at which analysis occurs determines the durability of the resulting memory trace. "Deeper" processing, involving analysis of meaning and semantic associations, produces stronger, more durable traces than "shallower" processing focused on physical features. Importantly, this does not require more time — it requires different *kinds* of cognitive operations.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Levels-of-Processing|Levels of Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Lexical Automaticity** **[perm-note]**
> The state in which word recognition (mapping orthographic form to phonological and semantic representations) requires minimal attentional resources. Lexical automaticity is achieved through massive exposure to a word form across varied contexts. It is a graded property — recognition can be partially automatized — not a binary state.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Lexical-Automaticity|Lexical Automaticity]]*

> [!definition] **Literature Review** **[perm-note]**
> A systematic literature review represents a comprehensive approach to identifying, selecting, and analyzing relevant literature on specific research questions through structured protocols that minimize bias while maximizing the validity and reliability of findings. Within [[Personal-Knowledge-Management]] systems, this definition expands to encompass not merely the production of a review document but the *integration of sources into a living knowledge network* where individual insights become reusable nodes supporting multiple future investigations.
> 
> *Source: pkb-report-literature-review-methodology-for-pkb-research-202512040139* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Literature-Review|Literature Review]]*

> [!definition] **Locus of Control** **[perm-note]**
> An attributional construct measuring the degree to which individuals believe outcomes are caused by their own behavior (internal LOC) versus external forces (external LOC). Distinguished from control orientation: LOC concerns causal attribution; control orientation concerns motivational register. The distinction is sharpest for the case of internally-controlled motivation (guilt, shame, ego-involvement), which implies internal LOC but control orientation.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Locus-of-Control|Locus of Control]]*

> [!definition] **Long-Term Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive system responsible for the long-term storage of knowledge, skills, procedures, and episodic memories. Unlike working memory, long-term memory has no known capacity limit and no intrinsic decay rate for well-consolidated memories. Information in long-term memory is organized into networks of interconnected structures. For CLT, the crucial feature of long-term memory is that its contents — particularly schemas — can be activated and used in working memory without consuming working memory capacity in proportion to their complexity.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Long-Term-Memory|Long-Term Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Helpless Response Pattern** **[perm-note]**
> A behavioral pattern in which a learner responds to failure or setback by attributing the difficulty to stable, uncontrollable factors (lack of ability), disengaging from the task, showing negative affect, and performing worse on problems previously mastered. The original empirical phenomenon that motivated Dweck's research program on implicit theories of intelligence.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Helpless-Response-Pattern|Helpless Response Pattern]]*

## _M

> [!definition] **Maintenance Rehearsal** **[perm-note]**
> The repetitive cycling of information at a constant level of processing depth — typically at the phonemic level (saying a phone number over and over to keep it in mind). Craik and Lockhart proposed that maintenance rehearsal produces minimal improvement in long-term retention because it does not increase processing depth; it merely sustains the trace at a shallow level. This was a direct challenge to the rehearsal-based account in the [[Atkinson-Shiffrin-Multi-Store-Model]].
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Maintenance-Rehearsal|Maintenance Rehearsal]]*

> [!definition] **Manipulative Abduction** **[perm-note]**
> A form of abductive inference that occurs through and in the course of physical manipulation of the environment — experimentation, prototyping, tool use, diagrammatic reasoning. Distinct from *sentential abduction* (hypothesis formation through linguistic or logical deliberation) and *model-based abduction* (hypothesis formation through mental model manipulation). Manipulative abduction grounds the epistemological value of hands-on learning: physical interaction with materials can generate hypotheses that purely mental deliberation would not produce, because the material world offers unexpe...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Manipulative-Abduction|Manipulative Abduction]]*

> [!definition] **Mark of the Cognitive** **[perm-note]**
> The property or properties that distinguish genuinely cognitive processes from merely causally relevant ones. Identifying the mark of the cognitive is the central challenge posed by critics of Extended Mind Theory. Adams and Aizawa propose intrinsic, non-derived representational content as the mark; Clark rejects this criterion as theoretically unmotivated and argues that functional role provides the appropriate criterion. The debate about the mark of the cognitive has proved productive: it has forced more precise accounts of what cognitive status means.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mark-of-the-Cognitive|Mark of the Cognitive]]*

> [!definition] **MarkMind** **[perm-note]**
> [**MarkMind**:: A comprehensive [[Mind-Mapping]], [[outlining]], and [[PDF annotation]] plugin for [[Obsidian]] that enables visual knowledge structuring, hierarchical note organization, and integrated reading-annotation workflows.]^established-stable
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-excalidraw-and-markmind-2025121812* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[MarkMind]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery Experience** **[perm-note]**
> In Bandura's framework, an actual experience of successfully performing a challenging task, which is the most powerful source of self-efficacy. Contrasted with vicarious experience, verbal persuasion, and physiological states as sources of efficacy information. This report's reconstruction identifies mastery experience architecture as more fundamental to growth mindset outcomes than mindset instruction.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mastery-Experience|Mastery Experience]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery Goal** **[perm-note]**
> A mastery goal is an achievement goal in which the person defines success as developing competence — acquiring new skills, deepening understanding, or improving relative to one's own prior performance. The standard of reference is intrapersonal and/or task-inherent. Mastery goals are sometimes called *learning goals* (Dweck's terminology) or *task goals* (Nicholls's terminology). They are associated with the adoption of deep cognitive strategies, help-seeking when confused, persistence in the face of difficulty, positive affect during challenge, and long-term retention. Under mastery-orient...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mastery-Goal|Mastery Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery Goal Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> A motivational orientation characterized by the desire to develop competence, deepen understanding, and improve skill — regardless of how performance compares to others. Learners with mastery goals seek challenges, value learning over looking competent, and interpret failure as information. Strongly associated with growth mindset, though the two constructs are related but distinct.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mastery-Goal-Orientation|Mastery Goal Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery-Approach Goal** **[perm-note]**
> Oriented toward the positive possibility of intrapersonal competence: developing one's skills, deepening understanding, mastering the task. The person asks "Am I learning as much as possible?" This is the goal orientation most consistently associated with adaptive outcomes: deep processing, intrinsic motivation, positive affect, and genuine intellectual engagement.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mastery-Approach-Goal|Mastery-Approach Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery-Avoidance Goal** **[perm-note]**
> Oriented toward the negative possibility of intrapersonal incompetence: failing to learn, declining in ability, not living up to one's prior standard. The person asks "Am I avoiding regression?" This goal type is theoretically interesting but practically less common and somewhat harder to study. It may be common among experts, high-performing athletes, or older adults who are concerned with avoiding cognitive decline. It is associated with worry and exhaustion even when performance remains high.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mastery-Avoidance-Goal|Mastery-Avoidance Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Mastery-Oriented Response Pattern** **[perm-note]**
> A behavioral pattern in which a learner responds to failure or setback by maintaining task engagement, trying new strategies, showing neutral or positive affect, and maintaining or improving performance on subsequent problems. Characteristic of growth-mindset learners; associated with mastery goal orientation in Achievement Goal Theory.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mastery-Oriented-Response-Pattern|Mastery-Oriented Response Pattern]]*

> [!definition] **Matthew Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The compounding advantage in reading development named after the Gospel of Matthew ("to him who has, more will be given"): readers who read more develop larger vocabularies, enabling more fluent decoding, freeing more cognitive resources for meaning-making, increasing comprehension and motivation, leading to more reading. Relevant to critical analysis development through its underlying mechanism of vocabulary-mediated working memory availability.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Matthew-Effect|Matthew Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Matthew Effect in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> The compounding inequality in reading development whereby early reading skill advantages generate increased reading volume, vocabulary growth, and world knowledge, creating a positive feedback loop that accelerates skill development in already-skilled readers — while early skill deficits generate reading avoidance, reduced word exposure, and slower vocabulary and knowledge growth, creating a negative feedback loop. Named after the biblical observation that "to he who has, more shall be given."
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Matthew-Effect-in-Reading|Matthew Effect in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Maturity Level Definitions & Criteria** **[perm-note]**
> **🔴 needs-review** — Note exists but hasn't been processed
> - *Criteria*: Raw capture, unverified content, no connections established
> - *Review Interval*: Immediate (within 48 hours)
> - *Advancement Requires*: One complete review pass, basic connections added
> 
> **🌱 seedling** — Initial processing complete
> - *Criteria*: Content verified, 2-3 connections made, structure established
> - *Review Interval*: 7 days
> - *Advancement Requires*: Successful recall test, 2+ new connections, practical application identified
> 
> **🌿 developing** — Active integration in progress
> - *Criteria*: Applied in at least o...
> 
> *Source: reference-instructional-pkb-review-system-2025120322* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Maturity-Level-Definitions-&-Criteria|Maturity Level Definitions & Criteria]]*

> [!definition] **Embodied Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The theoretical and empirical program holding that cognitive processes are grounded in sensorimotor experience — that concepts, schemas, and knowledge structures are constituted by patterns of embodied simulation rather than abstract amodal representations. After this re-examination: the primary philosophical challenge to schema theory's representationalist foundation; not merely a different vocabulary for the same phenomenon.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: Motivates the primary philosophical challenge to schema theory.
> **Cross-References**: [[Embodied-Cognition]], [[Embo...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Embodied-Cognition|Embodied Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Meaningful Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A mode of learning in which new information is intentionally related to existing knowledge structures in the learner's cognitive field, producing genuine integration between new and existing concepts. Meaningful learning contrasts with *rote learning*, in which material is memorized in isolation without connection to existing knowledge — producing fragile, non-transferable, rapidly decaying retention. Ausubel argued that almost all formal instruction produces rote learning by default because it fails to establish the cognitive conditions required for assimilation: relevant anchoring ideas m...
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Meaningful-Learning|Meaningful Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Memory Consolidation** **[perm-note]**
> The processes by which newly formed memory traces are stabilised over time. Operates at two levels: (1) Synaptic consolidation — cellular/molecular stabilisation occurring over hours, requiring protein synthesis; and (2) Systems consolidation — hippocampal-to-neocortical transfer occurring over months to years (Standard Model) or ongoing hippocampal contribution throughout the lifetime of episodic memories (Multiple Trace Theory). Consolidation is distinct from encoding: encoding determines the initial trace; consolidation determines how robustly it is stabilised. Consolidation is supported...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Memory-Consolidation|Memory Consolidation]]*

> [!definition] **Memory Trace Durability** **[perm-note]**
> In the LOP framework, the persistence and accessibility of a memory over time, understood as a function of encoding quality rather than trace strength in a simple quantitative sense. Deeper processing does not simply create "more" of the same kind of trace — it creates a qualitatively different trace, characterized by richer elaboration, more retrieval routes, and greater resistance to interference. The concept is deliberately distinguished from mere rehearsal frequency.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Memory-Trace-Durability|Memory Trace Durability]]*

> [!definition] **Mental Image** **[perm-note]**
> A quasi-pictorial representation that preserves perceptual surface features — the appearance of an object from a specific viewpoint. Mental images are viewpoint-dependent and encode phenomenal properties (colour, texture, apparent size). Johnson-Laird argues that images are *representations of models from a particular perspective*, not a separate representational format.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mental-Image|Mental Image]]*

> [!definition] **Mental Model** **[perm-note]**
> A cognitive representation of a state of affairs that is: (a) structurally analogous to that state of affairs rather than merely encoding propositional descriptions of it; (b) constructed from semantic content rather than syntactic form; (c) token-based (containing representations of specific individuals, not just variables); and (d) capable of underwriting inference by inspection rather than rule application. Distinguished from: (i) propositional representations, (ii) images in the narrow visual sense, (iii) "mental model" in the folk-cognitive or HCI sense.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mental-Model|Mental Model]]*

> [!definition] **Mental Models** **[perm-note]**
> Internal representational structures that capture the states of affairs described by premises in a deductive argument. On Johnson-Laird's Mental Models Theory, deductive reasoning proceeds by (a) constructing one or more mental models of the premises, (b) drawing a putative conclusion that is true in the initial model, and (c) checking whether alternative models of the premises exist in which the conclusion is false. Reasoning difficulty correlates with the number of models required to represent the premises. What it is NOT: mental models are not visual images, though they may have visuospa...
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mental-Models|Mental Models]]*

> [!definition] **Mental Simulation** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive process of running a mental model forward in time to predict consequences, analogous to a physical simulation. Mental simulation is implicated in mechanical reasoning (imagining how gears turn), spatial reasoning (imagining how a figure would look rotated), causal reasoning (imagining what would happen if a component failed), and counterfactual reasoning (imagining what would have happened under different conditions). Mental simulation is closely related to — and may be partially explained by — embodied simulation in [[Perceptual Symbol Systems]].
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mental-Simulation|Mental Simulation]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognition** **[perm-note]**
> Metacognition, in the context of critical thinking, refers to the capacity to think about one's own thinking — to monitor, evaluate, and regulate one's cognitive processes. A critical thinker is not merely someone who reasons well; they are someone who is aware of *how* they reason, who can detect when their own reasoning is going wrong, and who can deliberately adjust. Metacognition is, in this sense, the executive capacity that makes sustained critical thinking possible. The term was formally introduced by John Flavell in 1976 and has since become a cornerstone of learning science.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognition]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Accuracy** **[perm-note]**
> The degree of correspondence between a learner's metacognitive judgments (confidence, judgments of learning, feelings of knowing) and their actual cognitive performance. A well-calibrated learner knows when they know something and knows when they do not. Metacognitive accuracy is conceptually distinct from metacognitive *level* — a learner can be highly confident (high level) while being systematically wrong about their actual knowledge state (poor accuracy). The Zimmerman model requires not merely that learners monitor, but that their monitoring *accurately reflects* their cognitive state ...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Accuracy|Metacognitive Accuracy]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Calibration** **[perm-note]**
> The accuracy of a learner's or knower's assessment of their own understanding: the degree to which their confidence in their knowledge matches the actual quality of that knowledge. Poor calibration (overconfidence) is associated with shallow learning; good calibration — knowing what you understand and knowing where your understanding breaks down — is a hallmark of expertise and, on the functional integration account, part of what understanding *consists* in.
> 
> *Source: what-does-it-mean-to-truley-understand-something-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Epistemology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Calibration|Metacognitive Calibration]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Capacity** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #27FF00;'>The cognitive resources available for monitoring, evaluating, and regulating one's own thinking processes, including: (1) [[Metacognitive-Knowledge]] (understanding of cognitive processes), (2) [[Metacognitive-Monitoring]] (awareness of current cognitive states), and (3) [[metacognitive control]] (ability to regulate cognitive processes based on monitoring)</span>
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-compounding-negative-impact-of-unmitigated-cognitive-biases-202512150535* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Capacity|Metacognitive Capacity]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Experience** **[perm-note]**
> In-the-moment affective or cognitive signals about the quality of one's current cognitive processing — feelings of knowing, of confusion, of fluency, or of effort. Serve as the monitoring signals that activate control processes. Inaccurate metacognitive experiences (feeling that you understand when you don't) constitute the primary failure mode of metacognitive monitoring.
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Experience|Metacognitive Experience]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Feelings** **[perm-note]**
> Phenomenological, affective states that arise during cognitive processing and carry implicit information about the quality and progress of that processing. Unlike metacognitive knowledge (explicit, declarative) or regulation (deliberate, procedural), metacognitive feelings are automatic, immediate, and valenced — they feel positive or negative. A feeling of non-comprehension (a subjective sense of confusion when reading a dense passage) carries both cognitive information ("I am not understanding this") and motivational signal ("what do I do now?").
> 
> *Source: metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Feelings|Metacognitive Feelings]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Infrastructure** **[perm-note]**
> The proposed reconceptualization of metacognitive skill as a system of interdependent components — monitoring cue ecology, threshold calibration, and strategic repertoire — rather than a unitary competence. Infrastructure must be *built* (through practice, calibration, and explicit instruction) rather than *taught* (through awareness alone).
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Infrastructure|Metacognitive Infrastructure]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> The declarative and conditional beliefs a person holds about cognition — their own and cognition in general. Subdivided into person knowledge (beliefs about oneself and others as cognitive agents), task knowledge (understanding of how task characteristics affect cognitive demands), and strategy knowledge (awareness of cognitive strategies, their costs, benefits, and conditions of applicability). Strategy knowledge is the most instructionally tractable component.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Knowledge|Metacognitive Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Monitoring** **[perm-note]**
> The ongoing process of tracking one's own cognitive states — detecting comprehension failures, accuracy lapses, or strategy-goal mismatches — during active learning. Distinct from metacognitive knowledge (knowing about learning) and from metacognitive control (taking regulatory action). The monitoring-control coupling is the mechanism by which monitoring signals trigger regulatory responses.
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Monitoring|Metacognitive Monitoring]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> The executive processes by which individuals actively manage their own cognition: planning (selecting and sequencing strategies before engaging a task), monitoring (tracking comprehension, progress, and quality during task performance), and evaluation (assessing outcomes and cognitive processes after task completion). Regulation is the functional payoff of metacognitive knowledge — it is where knowledge translates into improved performance.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Regulation|Metacognitive Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Metacognitive Scaffolding** **[perm-note]**
> Externally provided support structures that target the monitoring and/or control components of the metacognitive system, enabling learners to perform metacognitive functions they cannot yet perform reliably independently. Distinguished from content scaffolding by its target (the metacognitive system rather than domain knowledge) and from generic support by its function (developing internal capacity rather than merely enabling task completion).
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Metacognitive-Scaffolding|Metacognitive Scaffolding]]*

> [!definition] **Mindset Intervention** **[perm-note]**
> A brief educational program (typically one to three hours) designed to shift students' implicit theories of intelligence from entity to incremental theories, typically by teaching about neuroplasticity, providing examples of growth, and reframing challenge as opportunity. This report finds that such interventions have not reliably demonstrated meaningful academic outcome effects in independent replications.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mindset-Intervention|Mindset Intervention]]*

> [!definition] **Modality Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The finding that learning from a visual presentation accompanied by auditory narration typically exceeds learning from the same visual presentation accompanied by written text. Explained by the dual-channel architecture of working memory: audio + visual distributes load across the phonological loop and visuospatial sketchpad simultaneously, while written text + visual overloads the visual channel. Conditional on learner expertise, content complexity, and presentation pace.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Modality-Effect|Modality Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Model Context Protocol** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #FFC700;'>**Model Context Protocol**</span> is a universal interface specification enabling bidirectional communication between AI applications (hosts) and external services (servers). It abstracts away integration complexity, allowing developers to write tools once and have them work across multiple AI platforms—Claude Desktop, Claude Code, VS Code extensions, custom agents, and more.
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-mcp-servers-2025122412* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Model-Context-Protocol|Model Context Protocol]]*

> [!definition] **Model Multiplicity** **[perm-note]**
> The number of distinct mental models required to fully represent the possibilities consistent with a set of premises. Model multiplicity is the primary predictor of reasoning difficulty in MMT: problems requiring only one model are systematically easier than those requiring two or three. Multiplicity places demands on working memory (all models must be held active during counterexample search) and on the cognitive effort required for comprehensive exploration of the possibility space.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Model-Multiplicity|Model Multiplicity]]*

> [!definition] **Model Selection Decision Tree** **[perm-note]**
> ```
> Is this task complex reasoning or novel problem-solving?
> ├─ YES → Use Opus 4 (deepest reasoning)
> └─ NO → Is routine/repetitive work with clear patterns?
>     ├─ YES → Use Haiku 4.5 (3x cost savings, 2x speed)
>     └─ NO → Use Sonnet 4.5 (balanced default)
> 
> Special cases:
> - Orchestrator/coordinator: Sonnet 4.5 (needs maximum context understanding)
> - Quality validation: Opus 4 (critical quality gate)
> - Worker agents: Haiku 4.5 (volume tasks)
> ```
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Model-Selection-Decision-Tree|Model Selection Decision Tree]]*

> [!definition] **Model-Completeness Hypothesis** **[perm-note]**
> The hypothesis, central to Johnson-Laird's performance theory, that reasoning errors arise from constructing an *incomplete* set of models — failing to consider all logically possible models of the premises. A conclusion appears to hold because it holds in the partial model set constructed, but fails because a non-constructed model falsifies it. The primary explanation for the difficulty effects and error rates in syllogistic and propositional reasoning.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Model-Completeness-Hypothesis|Model-Completeness Hypothesis]]*

> [!definition] **Moderator-Conditionality** **[perm-note]**
> The property of a behavioral science finding whereby the effect holds only under specific conditions defined by moderating variables — variables that affect the direction or magnitude of a relationship. Many sweeping behavioral science claims (including SDT's undermining effect, stereotype threat, ego-depletion, and others) are categorical in their popular formulation but moderator-conditional in their careful empirical form. The gap between categorical transmission and moderator-conditional evidence is a structural problem in science dissemination.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Moderator-Conditionality]]*

> [!definition] **Modus Ponens** **[perm-note]**
> *If P then Q. P. Therefore, Q.* This is the most basic and psychologically robust valid inference form. If the antecedent (P) is affirmed and the conditional link holds, the consequent (Q) must follow. Example: "If it is raining, the ground is wet. It is raining. Therefore, the ground is wet." Modus ponens is so psychologically natural that it is often described as an "inference ticket" — humans execute it automatically, without deliberate effort.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Modus-Ponens|Modus Ponens]]*

> [!definition] **Modus Tollens** **[perm-note]**
> *If P then Q. Not-Q. Therefore, not-P.* This form denies the consequent to deny the antecedent. It is logically valid but psychologically more demanding than modus ponens, because it requires reasoning backward through the conditional. Example: "If it is raining, the ground is wet. The ground is not wet. Therefore, it is not raining." Modus tollens is central to falsificationist science: if a theory predicts a result and the result does not occur, the theory must be revised.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Modus-Tollens|Modus Tollens]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring Gap** **[perm-note]**
> The documented discrepancy between what monitoring reveals (a comprehension failure) and what regulation enacts (a suboptimal response such as re-reading). The monitoring gap is evidence for coupling failure distinct from monitoring inaccuracy. See [[Monitoring-Gap]].
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[Monitoring-Gap|Monitoring Gap]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring Scaffolding** **[perm-note]**
> Scaffolding that targets the upward information flow in the Nelson-Narens model — helping learners generate more accurate, explicit, or frequent meta-level representations of their cognitive state. Examples: prediction prompts, summary generation requirements, calibration exercises, conceptual checkpoints.
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Monitoring-Scaffolding|Monitoring Scaffolding]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring-Control Coupling** **[perm-note]**
> The mechanism by which a detected discrepancy between current performance and the goal standard (monitoring) triggers a specific regulatory response (control). The coupling can decouple — accurate monitoring can fail to trigger control response due to attribution failure, motivational withdrawal, or insufficient strategy repertoire — and the quality of this coupling is the proposed critical bottleneck in the SRL cycle.
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Monitoring-Control-Coupling|Monitoring-Control Coupling]]*

> [!definition] **The Monitoring-Control Model** **[perm-note]**
> A two-level architecture in which (1) the *object level* represents cognitive processing — in reading, the processes of word identification, syntactic parsing, inference generation, and situation model construction; (2) the *meta level* monitors the object level and can issue control signals to it; and (3) crucially, these levels communicate through two distinct channels: **monitoring** flows upward (from object to meta) and **control** flows downward (from meta to object). [[Monitoring-Control-Coupling]] occurs when a monitoring signal reliably activates a corresponding control response. [...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Monitoring-Control-Model|The Monitoring-Control Model]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring-Regulation Coupling** **[perm-note]**
> The functional link between metacognitive *monitoring* (the flow of information from object-level processing to meta-level awareness) and metacognitive *control* (the flow of regulatory decisions from meta-level to object-level processing). The [[Nelson-Narens-Model]] treats monitoring and control as conceptually distinct operations that operate through separable mechanisms. The Zimmerman model implicitly assumes tight coupling: monitoring during the Performance Phase generates data that smoothly flows into the Self-Reflection Phase's self-evaluation, which then flows into modified Forethou...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Monitoring-Regulation-Coupling|Monitoring-Regulation Coupling]]*

> [!definition] **Monitoring-Regulation Decoupling** **[perm-note]**
> The failure of detected comprehension failures to trigger corresponding regulatory actions. Distinguished from monitoring failure (not detecting a failure) by the presence of a monitoring signal that does not result in regulatory action. The three mechanisms identified in this analysis are signal threshold failure, strategy activation failure, and effort abandonment. Central concept from [[Monitoring-Control-Coupling]] theory (Nelson & Narens, 1990) applied to the reading domain.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Monitoring-Regulation-Decoupling|Monitoring-Regulation Decoupling]]*

> [!definition] **Emotional Granularity as Epistemic Precision** **[perm-note]**
> From Report 10: the capacity to make fine-grained distinctions between emotional states — distinguishing, for example, anxiety from excitement, frustration from disappointment, moral indignation from personal offense — which is itself a form of cognitive precision that improves both reasoning and decision-making. Where coarse emotional categories generate coarse behavioral responses, fine-grained emotional distinctions enable calibrated, context-appropriate responses. Developing Emotional Granularity is not a merely psychological project but an epistemic one.
> 
> *Source: report-15-the-examined-life-revisited* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Emotional-Granularity-as-Epistemic-Precision|Emotional Granularity as Epistemic Precision]]*

> [!definition] **Motivated Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> A pattern of reasoning in which prior commitments, identity stakes, or desired conclusions drive the direction of information search and evaluation, while the appearance of dispassionate inference is maintained. Kunda's formulation distinguishes motivated reasoning from biased reasoning simpliciter: motivated reasoning involves the deployment of genuine cognitive capacity in the service of directional goals. The motivation operates by constraining the stopping point of evidential search — accepting the first conclusion consistent with the desired outcome rather than the most evidentially su...
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Motivated-Reasoning|Motivated Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Affordance** **[perm-note]**
> Adapted from Gibson's (1979) ecological psychology concept of affordance (the action possibilities that an environment offers to a particular perceiver). In the context of COT, motivational affordances are the motivationally relevant features of an environment that an individual perceives and can act on. Different orientations make different motivational affordances salient: autonomy orientation makes intrinsic interest and choice affordances salient; control orientation makes evaluative and directive affordances salient.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Motivational-Affordance|Motivational Affordance]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Climate** **[perm-note]**
> Motivational climate refers to the achievement goal structure perceived to be operative in a particular setting. It is determined by the structural features of the environment — how tasks are designed, how authority is distributed, how recognition is given, how students are grouped, how evaluation is conducted, and how time is managed. Ames (1992) used the acronym TARGET (Task, Authority, Recognition, Grouping, Evaluation, Time) to identify the key structural dimensions that shape perceived motivational climate. Crucially, motivational climate influences which goals individuals *adopt* in t...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Motivational-Climate|Motivational Climate]]*

> [!definition] **Motivational Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The deliberate attempt by learners to initiate, maintain, or modify motivational states during learning — including monitoring motivational decline, attributing it appropriately, and deploying repair strategies such as self-consequating, interest enhancement, goal-oriented self-talk, and environmental structuring. Distinct from the more common framing of motivation as a relatively stable input to learning.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This is the most theoretically contentious component of Pintrich's framework. The evidence base exists but relies heavily on self-report...
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Motivational-Regulation|Motivational Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **The Empirical Lens** **[perm-note]**
> Scrutinizes what the evidence base actually supports, disentangling what schema research shows from what popular summaries claim. This lens asks: Where is the evidence genuinely strong, and where does the evidence support a narrower or more conditional claim than the received view asserts?
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Empirical-Lens|The Empirical Lens]]*

> [!definition] **MSLQ** **[perm-note]**
> A self-report instrument developed by Pintrich and colleagues (1991) to assess motivational orientations and cognitive learning strategies. Widely used in SRL research but subject to criterion contamination: scores reflect self-reported strategy frequency rather than directly observed regulatory process quality.
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[MSLQ]]*

> [!definition] **The MULTI_AGENT_PLAN.md Pattern** **[perm-note]**
> A structured markdown file acting as the coordination hub:
> 
> ```markdown
> # Multi-Agent Development Plan
> 
> ## Task: Implement User Authentication
> - **Assigned To**: builder
> - **Status**: In Progress  
> - **Dependencies**: None
> - **Notes**: Using JWT tokens, coordinate with validator for test cases
> - **Last Updated**: 2026-01-06 14:32 by architect
> 
> ## Task: Write Integration Tests
> - **Assigned To**: validator
> - **Status**: Pending
> - **Dependencies**: Waiting for builder to complete auth module
> - **Notes**: Need to verify token expiration, refresh flow
> - **Last Updated**: 2026-01-06 14:35 by vali...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-MULTI-AGENT-PLAN.md-Pattern|The MULTI_AGENT_PLAN.md Pattern]]*

> [!definition] **Mutuality** **[perm-note]**
> The bidirectional quality of need support within a close relationship — both partners simultaneously functioning as sources of support for the other's basic needs. RMT and Canevello and Crocker's research on compassionate vs. self-image goals suggest that dyadic mutuality (both partners oriented toward genuinely supporting the other) produces outcomes substantially better than additive models predict — suggesting that mutual need support is not simply twice the need support of one-directional support, but that the alignment of both partners' motivational orientations creates a qualitatively...
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Mutuality]]*

## _N

> [!definition] **Narrative Coherence as Cognitive Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> From Report 14: the claim that the construction of a coherent life narrative is not a luxury of reflective personalities but a cognitive necessity. In predictive processing terms, the narrative self-model functions as the highest-level generative model — predicting not sensory data but the trajectory of one's life. When the narrative is coherent, this highest-level model provides appropriate contextual priors for all downstream processing; when it is incoherent (as in identity crises), the uncertainty propagates down the hierarchy, generating what clinicians describe as anxiety, dissociatio...
> 
> *Source: report-15-the-examined-life-revisited* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Narrative-Coherence-as-Cognitive-Architecture|Narrative Coherence as Cognitive Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **Encoding Specificity Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that retrieval success depends on the overlap between conditions at encoding and conditions at retrieval. Information encoded in a particular context (emotional state, physical environment, semantic context) is best retrieved in a context that provides cues overlapping with those encoded alongside the original trace. This explains why learning material in exactly the conditions under which it will be tested (or constructing those conditions at study) can improve recall — and why material learned in a radically different context may fail to be retrieved even though it is demons...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Encoding-Specificity-Principle|Encoding Specificity Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Need Absence** **[perm-note]**
> A state in which a basic psychological need is not being satisfied in a given context without active thwarting. Distinguished from need frustration by the absence of the specific defensive responses that active thwarting produces. The distinction is operationalized in the Basic Psychological Need Satisfaction and Frustration Scale (BPNSFS; Chen et al., 2015).
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Need-Absence|Need Absence]]*

> [!definition] **Need Frustration** **[perm-note]**
> Active thwarting or undermining of a basic psychological need by social conditions — distinct from mere absence of need satisfaction. SDT's expanded BPNT (post-2010) treats need satisfaction and need frustration as partially independent dimensions, with frustration producing stronger ill-being effects than unsatisfaction alone. The original SDT formulation assumed a unipolar need dimension; the distinction was a conceptual revision within the programme.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Need-Frustration|Need Frustration]]*

> [!definition] **Need Satisfaction** **[perm-note]**
> The state of having one's basic psychological needs for autonomy, competence, and relatedness met in an environment. In the COT developmental account, chronic need satisfaction cultivates autonomy orientation; chronic need frustration produces control or impersonal orientations depending on the specific nature and pattern of frustration.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Need-Satisfaction|Need Satisfaction]]*

> [!definition] **Need Substitute** **[perm-note]**
> An activity or psychological state that partially mimics the satisfaction of a basic psychological need without genuinely fulfilling it. Characterized by compulsive or rigid engagement patterns and by the need for continuous renewal. Examples include ego-protective performance goals (competence substitute), aggressive social dominance (relatedness substitute), and obsessive passion (autonomy substitute).
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Need-Substitute|Need Substitute]]*

> [!definition] **Need Support** **[perm-note]**
> Partner behavior that actively creates conditions for the other person's basic psychological needs to be satisfied. Autonomy support involves perspective-taking, acknowledging feelings, providing meaningful choice, and minimizing controlling language. Competence support involves providing optimal challenge, positive feedback, and scaffolding that enables genuine mastery. Relatedness support involves genuine attention, responsiveness, and investment in understanding the other person. Need support is the active form; its absence (neutrality) differs from need thwarting.
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Need-Support|Need Support]]*

> [!definition] **Need Thwarting** **[perm-note]**
> Need thwarting refers to the *active frustration* of a psychological need — instructional behaviors, social conditions, or environmental features that specifically undermine need satisfaction. Distinguished from need deprivation (the passive absence of need satisfaction) by its causal specificity and its stronger association with ill-being, controlled motivation, and amotivation. Thwarting can occur even when overall need satisfaction is moderate, and its effects are not simply the mirror image of satisfaction effects.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Need-Thwarting|Need Thwarting]]*

> [!definition] **Need-Frustration** **[perm-note]**
> The active blockage of basic psychological need satisfaction by the environment — not merely the absence of need support but the presence of conditions that actively prevent needs from being met. Need frustration is distinguished from need dissatisfaction by its distinct causal profile and its more severe and less reversible consequence profile.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Need-Frustration]]*
