> [!definition] **The Nelson-Narens Monitoring-Control Model** **[perm-note]**
> A two-level hierarchical model distinguishing a meta-level (which holds representations of the object-level cognitive system and generates monitoring judgments) and an object-level (where actual cognitive work occurs). Information flows upward from the object level to the meta level via **monitoring** — processes such as judgments of learning, feeling of knowing, and ease-of-learning assessments. Information flows downward from the meta level to the object level via **control** — decisions such as whether to continue studying, allocate more time, seek help, or deploy a different strategy. T...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Nelson-Narens-Monitoring-Control-Model|The Nelson-Narens Monitoring-Control Model]]*

> [!definition] **Engagement-Contingent Reward** **[perm-note]**
> A reward contingent simply on engaging in or starting an activity, without reference to completion or quality. This is the most purely controlling reward type in CET's taxonomy: the contingency "if you do it, you get the reward" makes the external reason for the behaviour maximally salient. Empirically, engagement-contingent and task-contingent rewards show the strongest undermining effects in the Deci/Koestner/Ryan (1999) meta-analysis.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Engagement-Contingent-Reward|Engagement-Contingent Reward]]*

> [!definition] **Non-Declarative Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The broad category of long-term memory that does not involve conscious recollection and cannot readily be reported verbally. Non-declarative memory encompasses at least four distinct systems: (1) **procedural memory** — skills and habits encoded through repeated practice (motor sequences, cognitive routines); (2) **priming** — facilitated processing of stimuli due to prior exposure, independent of conscious recollection; (3) **conditioning** — classically and operantly conditioned associations; and (4) **perceptual learning** — improvements in the ability to detect or discriminate stimuli t...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Non-Declarative-Memory|Non-Declarative Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Normative vs. Descriptive Model** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: A normative model specifies what *ought* to occur under idealized conditions — what optimal performance looks like — without claiming to describe what typically occurs. A descriptive model characterizes what *actually* occurs across the population of interest. Many influential models in cognitive and educational psychology are *normative* but are *applied descriptively* — treated as though they describe typical behavior when they describe expert or ideal behavior. Post-reconstruction: the Zimmerman cycle is most accurately a normative framework for expert [[Self-Regulated-Le...
> 
> *Source: zimmermans-cyclical-model-srl-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Normative-vs.-Descriptive-Model|Normative vs. Descriptive Model]]*

> [!definition] **Entity Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The belief that ability is a fixed, stable trait — something one either has or does not have in a given domain. Under entity theory, performance outcomes are treated as *diagnostic* of underlying ability. Failure reveals low ability; success confirms high ability. The rational response to this belief system is to protect one's self-image by avoiding challenges and seeking easy wins. Entity theorists tend to experience criticism and difficulty as threats to identity rather than information about performance.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Entity-Theory|Entity Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Entity Theory of Intelligence** **[perm-note]**
> Dweck's technical term for the belief that intelligence is a fixed, stable entity. Operationalized through items like "You have a certain amount of intelligence, and you can't really do much to change it." Distinguished from incremental theory. This report finds that entity theory may function as a domain-specific attribution tendency rather than a domain-general stable belief.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Entity-Theory-of-Intelligence|Entity Theory of Intelligence]]*

> [!definition] **Enumerative Induction** **[perm-note]**
> The simplest form of inductive inference: observing that all examined members of a class have a property, and concluding that all members of the class have that property. Example: "All the metals I have tested conduct electricity; therefore, all metals conduct electricity." Vulnerable to the single counterexample (the "black swan" problem). Distinguished from statistical induction, which explicitly quantifies the probabilistic strength of the generalization.
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Enumerative-Induction|Enumerative Induction]]*

## _O

> [!definition] **Obliterative Subsumption** **[perm-note]**
> Ausubel's account of forgetting: over time, specific details subsumed under a superordinate concept lose distinctiveness and are absorbed into the general concept, leaving only the higher-level schema. Explains the common experience of remembering gist while losing specific detail, and implies that the quality of the anchoring concept determines the persistence of the detailed learning it subsumes.
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Obliterative-Subsumption|Obliterative Subsumption]]*

> [!definition] **Observability Layers** **[perm-note]**
> **1. Execution traces**:
> - Which agents were invoked?
> - In what order?
> - With what inputs?
> - How long did each take?
> 
> **2. Decision logs**:
> - Why was this agent selected?
> - What alternatives were considered?
> - What confidence score triggered selection?
> 
> **3. Performance metrics**:
> - Token consumption per agent
> - Wall clock time per agent
> - Success/failure rates
> - Retry counts
> 
> **4. State evolution**:
> - How did shared state change over time?
> - Which agent made which state updates?
> - Conflicts or rollbacks?
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Observability-Layers|Observability Layers]]*

> [!definition] **Holistic Critical Thinking Scoring Rubric Framework** **[perm-note]**
> Performance-based assessment using standardized rubrics enables direct calibration of self-assessment accuracy by comparing learner self-ratings to expert ratings on identical reasoning products. The **Holistic Critical Thinking Scoring Rubric** (Facione and Facione) evaluates reasoning across core dimensions including analysis quality, interpretation accuracy, evaluation rigor, inference validity, explanation clarity, and metacognitive self-regulation effectiveness, using a four-level scale distinguishing strong, acceptable, unacceptable, and weak performance.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-critical-thinking-metacognitive-regulation* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Holistic-Critical-Thinking-Scoring-Rubric-Framework|Holistic Critical Thinking Scoring Rubric Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Tool Access Patterns** **[perm-note]**
> **Full tool access** (omit `tools` field):
> - Agent inherits all tools from main thread
> - Use for: General-purpose agents, orchestrators, implementation agents
> - Risk: Can perform destructive operations
> 
> **Read-only access** (`tools: Read, Grep, Glob`):
> - Agent can analyze but not modify
> - Use for: Code reviewers, analyzers, documentation generators
> - Safety: Cannot accidentally break anything
> 
> **Restricted write** (`tools: Read, Write(src/**), Bash(npm test)`):
> - Agent can modify specific directories, run specific commands
> - Use for: Feature developers limited to their module
> - Balance: Pro...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Tool-Access-Patterns|Tool Access Patterns]]*

> [!definition] **Open Society** **[perm-note]**
> Karl Popper's term for a society organised on fallibilist principles — one that treats its institutions, policies, and governing ideas as revisable and maintains the structural conditions (free inquiry, free press, independent institutions) necessary for error-detection and correction. Contrasted with the "closed society," which treats its foundational commitments as certain and therefore inviolable, eliminating the mechanisms of self-correction.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Open-Society|Open Society]]*

> [!definition] **Operational Targets** **[perm-note]**
> **Relevance**: outputs that stay on-task and on-audience.
> **Precision**: constrained claims, calibrated uncertainty, reproducible methods.
> **Consistency**: stable persona voice, stable method, stable formatting and metadata across sessions.
> 
> *Source: prompt-report-advanced-persona-crafting-and-instructional-scaffolding-techniques-for-maximizing-output-20251021215103* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Operational-Targets|Operational Targets]]*

> [!definition] **Oral Reading Fluency** **[perm-note]**
> The integrated ability to read connected text aloud with accuracy (low error rate), appropriate rate (words per minute), and prosody (expression, phrasing, and intonation reflecting comprehension of grammatical and semantic structure). ORF assessments, typically 1-minute timed passages, yield a Words Correct Per Minute (WCPM) score that is among the strongest brief predictors of reading comprehension. WCPM does not capture prosody and systematically underestimates fluency problems in accurate-but-slow readers.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Oral-Reading-Fluency|Oral Reading Fluency]]*

> [!definition] **The Organismic Dialectical Perspective** **[perm-note]**
> SDT's metatheoretical foundation — the view that human beings are active organisms with inherent growth tendencies (the "organismic" part) that continually interact with, and are shaped by, social environments (the "dialectical" part). The perspective holds that neither biology alone nor environment alone determines outcomes; instead, the continuous transaction between the person's inherent tendencies and the nutritive or impoverishing qualities of social contexts is the proper unit of analysis for motivational psychology. This perspective explains why SDT insists on studying both person an...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Organismic-Dialectical-Perspective|The Organismic Dialectical Perspective]]*

> [!definition] **Organismic Integration Tendency** **[perm-note]**
> OIT's theoretical claim that humans have an innate tendency to synthesize externally-sourced experiences and regulations into a coherent, autonomous self. This tendency is not an achievement but a default — it proceeds naturally when basic psychological needs are satisfied and is thwarted when they are frustrated. The "organismic" framing positions humans as active, self-organizing systems rather than passive recipients of social influence.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Organismic-Integration-Tendency|Organismic Integration Tendency]]*

> [!definition] **Organismic Integration Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The SDT sub-theory addressing the internalization and integration of extrinsic motivation — the process by which externally-imposed regulations can be progressively taken in and made one's own. OIT proposes the regulatory spectrum as a model of internalization progress, and identifies autonomy support, competence scaffolding, and relational security as the conditions that facilitate movement toward more autonomous regulatory styles.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Organismic-Integration-Theory|Organismic Integration Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Organismic Meta-Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The philosophical foundation of SDT: the view that humans are active, growth-oriented organisms with natural tendencies toward integration, internalization, and psychological coherence. These tendencies are understood as biological dispositions requiring specific environmental nutriments (the three basic psychological needs) for their expression.
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Organismic-Meta-Theory|Organismic Meta-Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Organismic-Integration-Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The sub-theory within [[Self-Determination-Theory]] that addresses the process by which external regulations and values are internalized and integrated into the self. OIT posits an organismic tendency toward integration and identifies the environmental conditions that support or undermine the movement from external to autonomous regulation across the internalization continuum.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Organismic-Integration-Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Orienting Task** **[perm-note]**
> An experimental technique, widely used in LOP research, in which participants are given a question or instruction that directs their processing toward a specific level before encountering a target word. For example: "Is this word in capital letters?" (structural), "Does this word rhyme with TRAIN?" (phonemic), or "Does this word fit in the sentence: 'The man kicked the ___'?" (semantic). Orienting tasks allow researchers to experimentally manipulate processing depth while measuring subsequent retention, enabling controlled comparison of trace durability across levels.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Orienting-Task|Orienting Task]]*

> [!definition] **Orienting Task Paradigm** **[perm-note]**
> The experimental methodology developed in association with LOP research, in which participants are given a specific instruction (orienting question) that directs their cognitive processing toward a specific level before encountering target material. Used as a controlled manipulation of encoding depth. Enables incidental learning designs in which participants do not know they will be tested for memory, controlling for strategic encoding.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Orienting-Task-Paradigm|Orienting Task Paradigm]]*

> [!definition] **Theory-Evidence Coordination** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive capacity to separately evaluate one's existing theoretical commitments and the evidence bearing on them, assigning evidential weight based on the evidence's properties rather than its fit with prior beliefs. Identified by Deanna Kuhn as a central component of scientific reasoning and one of the most difficult to develop. Learners without this capacity systematically distort evidence interpretation to confirm existing theories, undermining productive inductive reasoning.
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Theory-Evidence-Coordination|Theory-Evidence Coordination]]*

> [!definition] **Theory-Ladenness of Observation** **[perm-note]**
> The philosophical thesis that observations are not theory-neutral sense-data but are already organized and interpreted by the theoretical frameworks of the observer. "Seeing" in a scientific context is always "seeing as" — as a metal, as a bacterium, as evidence for a hypothesis. The theory-ladenness thesis has direct consequences for abduction: what counts as an anomaly, and therefore what triggers abductive inference, is determined by the observer's background theory. This makes the development of richer and more accurate background knowledge a prerequisite for, not merely complementary t...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Theory-Ladenness-of-Observation|Theory-Ladenness of Observation]]*

> [!definition] **Output Style Modes** **[perm-note]**
> Three primary workflow modes in Claude Code:
> 
> **Research Mode** (`/output-style research`)
> - Focus: Deep exploration and understanding
> - Agent pattern: Single agent deep dives, sequential investigation
> - Tools: Heavy use of Explore, web search, documentation fetching
> - Output: Comprehensive analysis documents
> 
> **Planning Mode** (`/output-style planning`)
> - Focus: Architecture and design
> - Agent pattern: Architect-focused, may invoke research agents
> - Tools: Codebase analysis, design document generation
> - Output: Architectural decision records (ADRs), design specs
> 
> **Execution Mode** (`/outp...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Output-Style-Modes|Output Style Modes]]*

> [!definition] **Overjustification Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The specific psychological phenomenon in which adding an external justification (reward) to a behaviour that is already intrinsically motivated reduces the person's subsequent intrinsic interest in that behaviour. The term "overjustification" refers to the idea that the external reason provides more justification than is needed — and in the process, changes the person's interpretation of why they were performing the activity in the first place. Originally named by Lepper, Greene, and Nisbett (1973); theoretically explained by CET's PLOC mechanism.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Overjustification-Effect|Overjustification Effect]]*

## _P

> [!definition] **The Parity Principle** **[perm-note]**
> If, as we confront some task, a part of the world functions as a process which, were it done in the head, we would have no hesitation in recognising as part of the cognitive process, then that part of the world is (for that time) part of the cognitive process. The principle is explicitly functionalist in character: cognitive status is determined by causal-functional role, not by material constitution or spatial location. It does NOT entail that all external processes count as cognitive — only those meeting the functional equivalence criterion.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Parity-Principle|The Parity Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Parity Principle** **[perm-note]**
> The central argumentative tool of Extended Mind Theory: if a process performed outside the head would count as cognitive if it were performed inside the head (meeting standard criteria of cognitive status), then it counts as cognitive when performed outside the head. The principle is explicitly functionalist: cognitive status is determined by causal-functional role, not by material constitution or spatial location. It does NOT entail that all external processes are cognitive — only those satisfying functional equivalence with internal processes. Critics argue it commits the coupling-constit...
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Parity-Principle|Parity Principle]]*

> [!definition] **Partisan Identity** **[perm-note]**
> Identification with a political party understood not merely as a summary of policy preferences but as a social identity — with the affective, motivational, and categorization properties of in-group membership. Partisan identity functions as a perceptual frame that shapes the interpretation of political events and information. The shift from understanding partisan identification as primarily a policy-aggregation mechanism to understanding it as a social identity mechanism is one of the most important developments in political psychology over the past twenty years.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Partisan-Identity|Partisan Identity]]*

> [!definition] **Passive Externalism** **[perm-note]**
> The view, associated with Putnam and Burge, that the *contents* of mental states are partly determined by factors external to the individual — the physical environment (natural kind terms) or social community (linguistic norms) — while the *vehicles* or physical realisers of those states remain brain-bound. Active externalism is a stronger, more radical claim: it extends the vehicles themselves.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Passive-Externalism|Passive Externalism]]*

> [!definition] **PENCRISAL Framework** **[perm-note]**
> Developed by Rivas and Saiz (2012) at the University of Salamanca, PENCRISAL (Prueba de Evaluación de Pensamiento Crítico en Razonamiento, Argumentación y Solución de problemas) operationalizes critical thinking through 35 situational problems requiring open-ended responses. Scoring applies a 0-2 rubric: 0 = incorrect response, 1 = correct but inadequately justified, 2 = correct with rigorous justification. Total scores range 0-70, with five dimensional subscales (14 points each):
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-critical-thinking-metacognitive-regulation* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[PENCRISAL-Framework|PENCRISAL Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Perceived Competence** **[perm-note]**
> The subjective sense of effectiveness in one's interactions with the environment — feeling capable, skilled, and able to produce intended outcomes. Distinct from objective competence: a person can be highly competent but have low perceived competence (as in the Impostor Phenomenon), or low competence but high perceived competence (overconfidence). In CET, perceived competence is a basic psychological need whose satisfaction by external events (through positive informational feedback) can enhance intrinsic motivation, and whose frustration (through negative feedback or task failure without i...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Perceived-Competence|Perceived Competence]]*

> [!definition] **Perceived Internal Locus of Causation** **[perm-note]**
> The subjective experience of one's own behavior as originating from internal, self-determined sources rather than external pressure or internal compulsion (introjected pressure). The technical psychological mechanism underlying the autonomy need. Contrasts with perceived external locus of causation, in which one's behavior is experienced as controlled by forces outside the integrated self.
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Perceived-Internal-Locus-of-Causation|Perceived Internal Locus of Causation]]*

> [!definition] **Perceived Locus of Causality** **[perm-note]**
> The learner's subjective experience of the origin of their behavior — whether they feel that their engagement arises from their own choices and values (internal PLOC) or from external demands and pressures (external PLOC). PLOC is the central mechanism through which autonomy support affects motivational quality: autonomy-supportive environments shift PLOC toward internal, while controlling environments shift it toward external.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Perceived-Locus-of-Causality|Perceived Locus of Causality]]*

> [!definition] **Perceived Partner Responsiveness** **[perm-note]**
> Reis, Clark, and Holmes's (2004) construct capturing the phenomenological quality of connection within a close relationship: the experience of feeling understood (the partner accurately perceives who you are), validated (the partner affirms the legitimacy of your experience), and cared for (the partner is genuinely invested in your well-being). PPR is empirically the strongest relational predictor of relatedness need satisfaction and serves as the proximate mechanism through which need-supportive partner behavior translates into felt connection.
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Perceived-Partner-Responsiveness|Perceived Partner Responsiveness]]*

> [!definition] **Perceptual Attunement** **[perm-note]**
> The proposed mechanism by which causality orientations produce their effects: orientations sensitize individuals to different features of the motivational environment, so that autonomy-relevant signals (interest, choice, value) are more salient to autonomy-oriented individuals and control-relevant signals (evaluation, pressure, comparison) are more salient to control-oriented individuals. Not explicitly theorized in Deci & Ryan's original COT account but derivable from the orientation construct and supported by outcomes patterns.
> 
> *Source: causality-orientations-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Perceptual-Attunement|Perceptual Attunement]]*

> [!definition] **Perceptual Span** **[perm-note]**
> The region from which useful information is extracted during a single eye fixation during reading. Approximately 14–15 characters to the right and 3–4 characters to the left of the fixation point in alphabetic scripts. Sets the physical upper limit on information available per fixation and is not substantively modifiable through training.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Perceptual-Span|Perceptual Span]]*

> [!definition] **Performance Goal** **[perm-note]**
> A performance goal is an achievement goal in which the person defines success as outperforming others or appearing more competent than others. The standard of reference is normative — anchored in social comparison. Performance goals are sometimes called *ego goals* or *ability goals*. The motivational consequences of performance goals are, as we will see, more complex than early theorists recognised, and they have been significantly refined by subsequent work distinguishing approach from avoidance orientations.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Performance-Goal|Performance Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Performance Goal Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> A motivational orientation characterized by the desire to demonstrate high ability and avoid demonstrating low ability relative to others. Performance-oriented learners prioritize looking smart over becoming smart, leading to risk-avoidance and vulnerability to setback. Strongly associated with fixed mindset.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Performance-Goal-Orientation|Performance Goal Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Performance Phase** **[perm-note]**
> The second phase of the cyclical model, comprising self-control (deliberate application of the selected strategy) and self-monitoring (ongoing tracking of performance quality against the goal standard established in forethought). The phase in which regulatory behavior is executed and observable.
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Performance-Phase|Performance Phase]]*

> [!definition] **Performance-Approach Goal** **[perm-note]**
> A performance-approach goal is oriented toward the positive possibility of normative competence — demonstrating that one is more competent than others. The motivational focus is appetitive: moving toward success in the normative sense. Under this orientation, competition and social comparison are energising rather than threatening, and success in outperforming others is the primary desired outcome.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Performance-Approach-Goal|Performance-Approach Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Performance-Avoidance Goal** **[perm-note]**
> A performance-avoidance goal is oriented toward the negative possibility of normative incompetence — avoiding demonstrating that one is less competent than others. The motivational focus is aversive: moving away from the threat of looking stupid or inferior. Under this orientation, the primary motivation is fear of failure and social humiliation rather than desire for success. This goal type is consistently associated with the most maladaptive outcomes in the research literature: surface processing, avoidance of challenging tasks, high anxiety, poor long-term learning, and burnout.
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Performance-Avoidance-Goal|Performance-Avoidance Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Personal Responsibility Orientation** **[perm-note]**
> A conceptual framework that resolves the SDL field's definitional ambiguity by distinguishing two orthogonal dimensions: SDL as a *teaching-learning process* (the degree to which the learner controls the instructional context) and *learner self-direction* as a personal attribute (the individual's dispositional orientation toward taking initiative in learning). The model clarifies that process and person are independent — one can be high on one dimension and low on the other — which explains why SDL-structured environments do not automatically produce self-directed learners, and why self-dir...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Personal-Responsibility-Orientation|Personal Responsibility Orientation]]*

> [!definition] **Personal Workflow Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> A formalized, engineered structure of tools, protocols, and information pathways designed to capture, process, and synthesize information with minimal `[[Cognitive Friction]]`. It acts as an externalized cognitive scaffolding that supports high-order thinking by automating low-order management.
> 
> *Source: cog-sci-report-system-desighn-priciples-in-personal-workflow-architecture-202511230649* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Personal-Workflow-Architecture|Personal Workflow Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **Phase vs. Area — A Critical Distinction** **[perm-note]**
> Phases and areas interact, but they are not the same thing. A *phase* describes a temporal moment in the regulatory cycle; an *area* describes a domain of regulatory attention. The monitoring phase involves monitoring cognition, monitoring motivation, monitoring behavior, and monitoring context — four distinct monitoring activities happening within the same temporal window. Confusing phases with areas leads to systematically incomplete models that effectively reduce to single-area monitoring (usually cognition) spread across time.
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Phase-vs.-Area-—-A-Critical-Distinction|Phase vs. Area — A Critical Distinction]]*

> [!definition] **Phenomenological Substitution** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which monitoring answers the question "how does this feel?" when the functionally relevant question is "how well is this encoded?" A consequence of monitoring's indirect, cue-based access to cognitive states.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[Phenomenological-Substitution|Phenomenological Substitution]]*

> [!definition] **The Philosophical Lens** **[perm-note]**
> Examines the conceptual assumptions that schema theory takes for granted — its theory of mental representation, its implicit ontology of cognition, and the logical structure of its core arguments. This lens asks: What must be true for schema theory to work, and are those presuppositions defensible?
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Philosophical-Lens|The Philosophical Lens]]*

> [!definition] **Philosophical Lineage** **[perm-note]**
> **[CT-Philosophical-Foundation**:: Critical thinking traces its intellectual roots to Socratic dialectic, Aristotelian logic, and medieval scholasticism, establishing the normative dimension where CT serves not merely as cognitive process but as epistemic virtue - the disciplined pursuit of truth through systematic questioning of assumptions, logical analysis of arguments, and commitment to evidence-based reasoning.]**
> 
> The philosophical tradition established critical thinking as fundamentally concerned with **justified belief** - not merely thinking *about* things, but thinking *well* abou...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-foundational-critical-thinking* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Philosophical-Lineage|Philosophical Lineage]]*

> [!definition] **Phonological Loop** **[perm-note]**
> The component of working memory responsible for maintaining and rehearsing verbal and acoustic information. It consists of two subcomponents: a *phonological store* (a passive acoustic buffer holding sounds for approximately 2 seconds before decay) and an *articulatory rehearsal process* (an inner voice that refreshes traces in the phonological store through sub-vocal rehearsal, effectively resetting the 2-second decay clock). Evidence for the phonological loop comes from the *phonological similarity effect* (sequences of similar-sounding words are harder to recall than dissimilar ones), th...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Phonological-Loop|Phonological Loop]]*

> [!definition] **Phonological-Loop|Phonological Loop** **[perm-note]**
> A component of [[Working-Memory|working memory]] responsible for maintaining phonological traces of verbal information in an active state for approximately 2 seconds, refreshable through subvocal rehearsal. In reading, the phonological loop maintains the phonological trace of sentences to support syntactic parsing across clausal boundaries.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Phonological-LoopPhonological-Loop|Phonological-Loop|Phonological Loop]]*

> [!definition] **Physiological Metacognition** **[perm-note]**
> Introduced in Report 09: the capacity to monitor and regulate one's bodily states as a form of cognitive self-management. Analogous to epistemic metacognition (monitoring one's thinking), Physiological Metacognition involves recognizing how current physiological states — fatigue, stress, hunger, autonomic arousal — are modulating cognitive performance, and making informed adjustments accordingly. At advanced stages, this monitoring becomes automatic and continuous rather than effortful and intermittent.
> 
> *Source: report-15-the-examined-life-revisited* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Physiological-Metacognition|Physiological Metacognition]]*

> [!definition] **Pintrich's 4×4 Matrix** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A theoretical architecture for self-regulated learning consisting of four regulatory phases (Forethought/Planning/Activation; Monitoring; Control; Reaction/Reflection) crossed with four regulated areas (Cognition; Motivation/Affect; Behavior; Context), generating 16 cells each representing a distinct regulatory activity. The matrix is analytic — it decomposes simultaneous regulatory processes for examination — not sequential.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The matrix structure is both Pintrich's principal contribution and the source of the tractability problem identified...
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Pintrich's-4×4-Matrix|Pintrich's 4×4 Matrix]]*

> [!definition] **Pintrich's Motivational Integration** **[perm-note]**
> Pintrich extended Zimmerman's primarily cognitive account by foregrounding [[Motivational-Regulation]] as a co-equal component of self-regulation alongside cognitive and metacognitive regulation. His framework distinguishes: **[[Goal-Orientation]]** (mastery vs. performance approach vs. performance avoidance — which shapes not just effort level but the qualitative nature of regulatory behavior); **[[Task-Value]]** (intrinsic interest, utility value, and attainment value — which determine whether regulation is initiated in the first place); and **[[Self-Efficacy]] for learning and performanc...
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Pintrich's-Motivational-Integration|Pintrich's Motivational Integration]]*

> [!definition] **Pipeline Stages** **[perm-note]**
> The canonical three-stage pattern:
> 
> **Stage 1: Requirements/Planning** (PM agent)
> - Input: User request, problem statement
> - Process: Clarifying questions, acceptance criteria definition
> - Output: Specification document, status set to READY_FOR_ARCH
> - Hand off: Architect agent triggered
> 
> **Stage 2: Architecture/Design** (Architect agent)  
> - Input: Specification from PM stage
> - Process: Design review, constraint validation, ADR creation
> - Output: Architecture document, status set to READY_FOR_BUILD
> - Handoff: Implementer agent triggered
> 
> **Stage 3: Implementation** (Implementer agent)
> - Inp...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Pipeline-Stages|Pipeline Stages]]*

> [!definition] **Episodic Buffer** **[perm-note]**
> Added by Baddeley in 2000 to address the original model's difficulty explaining how information from different working memory components and from long-term memory is integrated into coherent, multimodal representations (episodes). The episodic buffer is a limited-capacity store that holds integrated representations in a multimodal code, binding information from the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and long-term memory into unified chunks. It is assumed to be accessible to conscious awareness and under the control of the central executive.
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Episodic-Buffer|Episodic Buffer]]*

> [!definition] **Episodic Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The subsystem of declarative memory responsible for autobiographical, event-specific memories — personally experienced events situated in time and place. Tulving described episodic memory as involving *mental time travel*: the capacity to consciously re-experience past events (and to project oneself into future scenarios, which Tulving called *episodic future thinking*). Episodic memories are context-rich, often involving spatial, temporal, emotional, and perceptual details. They are reconstructive rather than reproductive — recall involves reassembling stored traces rather than playing bac...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Episodic-Memory|Episodic Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Action** **[perm-note]**
> An action performed on an external artifact not to advance a task directly but to change the cognitive state of the agent performing it — making thinking easier, revealing structure, or prompting new connections. Writing functions as an epistemic action insofar as the act of externalising thought reveals knowledge gaps and prompts integration that private cognition does not.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Epistemic-Action|Epistemic Action]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Calibration** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which a person's confidence in their beliefs matches the actual probability of those beliefs being true. A well-calibrated agent who says "I'm 80% confident in this" is right approximately 80% of the time across such claims. Fallibilism implies the importance of calibration: if all beliefs are potentially mistaken, then overconfidence is a systematic epistemic failing. Research in [[cognitive-psychology]] consistently shows that humans tend toward overconfidence — we are typically more certain than our evidence warrants. Calibration is thus both a theoretical commitment of fal...
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Epistemic-Calibration|Epistemic Calibration]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Citizenship** **[perm-note]**
> The set of cognitive capacities, epistemic virtues, and information-engagement practices required for effective participation in democratic public life. Epistemic citizenship goes beyond knowledge of political facts to include calibrated uncertainty about contested claims, awareness of one's own cognitive biases, disposition toward evidence-responsiveness, and skill in navigating complex information environments. It is the application domain in which political reasoning research most directly meets civic education practice.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Epistemic-Citizenship|Epistemic Citizenship]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> An individual's beliefs, attitudes, and reasoning about the nature of knowledge and the process of knowing — including beliefs about the certainty of knowledge, the source of knowledge, and the criteria by which claims can be evaluated as more or less justified. Epistemic cognition underlies but is distinct from domain-specific knowledge; it constitutes the meta-epistemological framework within which critical thinking occurs.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Epistemic-Cognition|Epistemic Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Holism** **[perm-note]**
> The thesis, associated primarily with W.V.O. Quine, that individual beliefs do not face empirical evidence in isolation but as part of an interconnected system. When experience conflicts with our expectations, we must revise our beliefs somewhere, but logic does not uniquely determine where. Holism supports fallibilism by showing that revision is always possible, and complicates Popperian falsificationism by showing that individual statements cannot be straightforwardly refuted without affecting surrounding assumptions.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Epistemic-Holism|Epistemic Holism]]*

> [!definition] **Epistemic Humility** **[perm-note]**
> The intellectual virtue of recognising the limits of one's knowledge, the possibility of one's errors, and the genuine epistemic standing of others who disagree. [[Epistemic-Humility]] is the dispositional correlate of the theoretical claim of fallibilism: if our beliefs could be wrong, then intellectual integrity demands that we hold them with a degree of tentativeness proportioned to our evidence, remain open to correction, and acknowledge what we do not know. Epistemic humility is not the same as intellectual timidity or the performance of uncertainty for social purposes — it is a cognit...
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Epistemic-Humility|Epistemic Humility]]*

> [!definition] **Point of View** **[perm-note]**
> **Point of View** is the perspective, frame of reference, or orientation from which you reason. It encompasses your vantage point, the lens through which you view issues, and the orientation that shapes your thinking. Point of View answers: *From what perspective am I viewing this? What is my frame of reference?* Every instance of reasoning necessarily proceeds from some point of view—thinking cannot occur from nowhere.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-fundamental-structures-of-reasoning-202512050320* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Point-of-View|Point of View]]*

> [!definition] **Political Decision Making** **[perm-note]**
> The subset of political reasoning that terminates in an action-relevant judgment: a voting choice, a donation decision, an expression of support or opposition, a policy preference. Political decision-making is the output of political reasoning processes. The distinction matters because a person may engage in extensive political reasoning without reaching a decision, and may reach political decisions with very little deliberate reasoning at all.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Political-Decision-Making|Political Decision Making]]*

> [!definition] **Political Reasoning** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive processes by which an individual forms, evaluates, and updates beliefs about political actors, institutions, issues, and events. Political reasoning encompasses both the fast, automatic processes that generate immediate political responses and the slower, more deliberate processes that evaluate those responses against evidence and argument. It is broader than formal logical inference — it includes all the ways that humans make sense of political reality, including through narrative, analogy, emotion, and social comparison.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Political-Reasoning|Political Reasoning]]*

> [!definition] **The Practical Lens** **[perm-note]**
> Investigates how the theory performs when it guides instructional design, pedagogical practice, and knowledge management. This lens asks: Where does the textbook version of schema theory diverge from what experienced practitioners discover when they apply it?
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Practical-Lens|The Practical Lens]]*

> [!definition] **Pragmatic Construction** **[perm-note]**
> The novel insight from Phase IV: representation construction in reasoning is guided not by logical completeness but by pragmatic relevance — people construct the representations that are most informative and relevant given their goals and context. This explains why "reasoning errors" in laboratory tasks are often adaptive responses to realistic prior probabilities, and why everyday reasoning performs much better than laboratory deductive reasoning studies suggest.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Pragmatic-Construction|Pragmatic Construction]]*

> [!definition] **Pragmatic Reasoning Schema** **[perm-note]**
> A content-specific cluster of production rules organized around a recurring social or causal situation, particularly permission and obligation structures. Unlike formal logical rules, pragmatic schemas are activated by thematic content and encode the inferential patterns appropriate to that content domain. The permission schema (roughly: "if action A is to be taken, precondition P must be satisfied") explains why people reason correctly on permission-framed versions of the Wason Selection Task even without formal logical training.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Pragmatic-Reasoning-Schema|Pragmatic Reasoning Schema]]*

> [!definition] **Pragmatic Reasoning Schemas** **[perm-note]**
> Domain-specific, content-sensitive rule-like structures (proposed by Cheng & Holyoak) that govern reasoning in particular pragmatic contexts — especially permission schemas (if action then precondition must be satisfied) and obligation schemas. Represent a hybrid position: rule-like in form but content-sensitive in application. Used to explain the facilitation of Wason selection task performance with thematic materials without positing mental models.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Pragmatic-Reasoning-Schemas|Pragmatic Reasoning Schemas]]*

> [!definition] **Principle of Truth** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that mental models represent only what is *true* in the described situation, not what is false. Initial models are "minimally" constructed: they contain only the tokens needed to satisfy the true conditions of the premises. This parsimony conserves working memory but produces systematic errors when correct reasoning requires explicit representation of false cases or consideration of multiple alternative models.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Principle-of-Truth|Principle of Truth]]*

> [!definition] **Prior Knowledge** **[perm-note]**
> The totality of information, concepts, experiences, beliefs, and cognitive structures a learner brings to a new learning situation. Prior knowledge is not a simple inventory of facts but an organized architecture — a network of [[Schema|schemas]], [[Procedural-Knowledge|procedures]], [[Episodic-Memory|episodic memories]], and [[Mental-Model|mental models]] — that actively shapes how new information is perceived, interpreted, encoded, and retrieved. Crucially, prior knowledge can be relevant or irrelevant, accurate or inaccurate, elaborated or sparse, and these qualities dramatically affect ...
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Prior-Knowledge|Prior Knowledge]]*

> [!definition] **The Problem of Induction** **[perm-note]**
> The epistemological challenge, formulated by David Hume and restated by Karl Popper, that no finite number of particular observations can logically guarantee a universal generalization. The inference from "all observed swans have been white" to "all swans are white" cannot be deductively valid — a single black swan suffices to refute it (as was discovered when Europeans reached Australia). The problem has no universally accepted solution; it motivates probabilistic, Bayesian, and pragmatic accounts of inductive reasoning that treat its conclusions as *probable* rather than *certain*.
> 
> *Source: inductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Problem-of-Induction|The Problem of Induction]]*

> [!definition] **Process Praise** **[perm-note]**
> Evaluative feedback that attributes performance outcomes to the learner's specific strategies, effort allocation, choices, or processes rather than to global, stable personal traits. Example: "The systematic approach you used in checking your reasoning paid off here." Distinguished from person praise, which attributes outcomes to fixed traits and has been shown to produce entity-theory orientation with associated motivational costs.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Process-Praise|Process Praise]]*

> [!definition] **Processing Depth** **[perm-note]**
> The level of abstraction at which a stimulus is analyzed during encoding, conventionally distinguished as structural (physical features), phonemic (acoustic features), and semantic (meaning). Depth is a qualitative distinction in the *kind* of cognitive operation performed, not a quantitative measure of effort or time. Operationally defined via orienting tasks; theoretically constrained by independent converging evidence from neuroimaging and behavioral studies.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Processing-Depth|Processing Depth]]*

> [!definition] **Productive Failure** **[perm-note]**
> A pedagogical design principle and empirical finding holding that student engagement with complex problems that they are not yet equipped to solve correctly can produce superior subsequent learning outcomes compared to direct instruction, when followed by instructor-provided explanation. The mechanism is partly abductive: the prior engagement with the problem activates prior knowledge, surfaces misconceptions, creates awareness of knowledge gaps, and generates a schema of failed attempts that structures the subsequent encoding of correct information. Productive failure is one of the best-em...
> 
> *Source: abductive-reasoning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Productive-Failure|Productive Failure]]*

> [!definition] **Productive Friction** **[perm-note]**
> The author's term for the family of cognitive resistances — articulation constraints, retrieval demands, naive audience requirements, causal questioning — that activate generative processing mechanisms when present in a writing task. The active ingredient that distinguishes knowledge-transforming from knowledge-telling writing.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Productive-Friction|Productive Friction]]*

> [!definition] **Productive Struggle** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitively effortful state of grappling with genuinely challenging material without immediate access to a solution pathway. Distinguished from mere frustration (which produces withdrawal) by the learner's continued engagement and cognitive effort. Growth-mindset environments deliberately create and protect space for productive struggle, treating it as the primary site of learning rather than a failure state to be quickly resolved.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Productive-Struggle|Productive Struggle]]*

> [!definition] **Progressive Approximation** **[perm-note]**
> The fallibilist model of epistemic progress as an asymptotic approach toward truth — each stage of inquiry producing theories that are more accurate, more comprehensive, and better supported than their predecessors, while remaining imperfect and in principle revisable. Associated with Nicholas Rescher's philosophy of inquiry. Preserves the objectivity and meaningfulness of knowledge claims while honouring the permanent possibility of revision.
> 
> *Source: fallibilism-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Progressive-Approximation|Progressive Approximation]]*

> [!definition] **Progressive Disclosure** **[perm-note]**
> The instructional principle of managing complexity by revealing information progressively, calibrated to the learner's current state. In metacognitive scaffolding design, progressive disclosure applies to both the complexity of the metacognitive tasks required (from simple monitoring checkpoints to richer regulatory protocols) and to the fading of scaffolding support (from high support to low support as competence develops).
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Progressive-Disclosure|Progressive Disclosure]]*

> [!definition] **Prokoptōn** **[perm-note]**
> The Stoic term for the person making progress — the one who is neither the unreflective ordinary person nor the achieved sage, but is genuinely oriented toward the ideal and actively developing toward it. The prokoptōn is the appropriate self-description at every stage of the developmental model: not "I have arrived" but "I am genuinely on the way." The concept captures an important philosophical point: the examined life is not a state to be achieved but an orientation to be maintained, and the orientation itself — the genuine commitment to honest engagement with reality — is the primary ac...
> 
> *Source: report-15-the-examined-life-revisited* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Prokoptōn]]*

> [!definition] **Prompty Asset Class** **[perm-note]**
> [**Prompty**:: a file format specification (`.prompty`) and VS Code extension designed to enhance observability, understandability, and portability for LLM prompt development by unifying prompt content, execution context, and sample data in a single version-controlled markdown asset.]^verified-stable
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-prompty-exstenion-for-vs-code-2025122805* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Prompty-Asset-Class|Prompty Asset Class]]*

> [!definition] **Propositional Representation** **[perm-note]**
> A cognitive representation in a language-like format: discrete, compositional, amodal (not tied to any sensory modality), and rule-governed. Operations on propositional representations are syntactic — they apply to the form of the representation regardless of its content. The mental logic tradition (Rips' PSYCOP, Braine's natural deduction) posits that deductive reasoning operates by applying inference rules to propositional representations.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Propositional-Representation|Propositional Representation]]*

> [!definition] **Prosody** **[perm-note]**
> The suprasegmental features of spoken language — including pitch variation, stress, rhythm, tempo, and phrasing — applied to oral reading. Prosodic oral reading requires simultaneous processing of syntactic structure and semantic content during production, making appropriate prosody both a marker of comprehension and potentially a mechanism that scaffolds syntactic parsing. Prosodic fluency assessment goes beyond rate and accuracy to capture the quality dimension of fluent reading.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Prosody]]*

> [!definition] **Pseudometacognition** **[perm-note]**
> The production of metacognitive-sounding language or behavior without genuine metacognitive functioning — accurate self-monitoring or effective self-regulation. Pseudometacognition can be inadvertently cultivated by explicit metacognitive requirements that reward the performance of metacognition rather than its substance, or when learners learn to produce appropriate responses to metacognitive prompts without genuine cognitive engagement.
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Pseudometacognition]]*

## _Q

> [!definition] **Question at Issue** **[perm-note]**
> The [**Question at Issue**:: is the specific problem, question, or issue that the thinking addresses.] It defines the focal point of inquiry and determines the scope of relevant considerations. While Purpose establishes *why* you are thinking, the <span style='color: #ff0075;'>Question at Issue establishes *what* you are thinking about.</span> It converts general purpose into specific inquiry.
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-fundamental-structures-of-reasoning-202512050320* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Question-at-Issue|Question at Issue]]*

> [!definition] **Equilibration** **[perm-note]**
> Equilibration is the regulatory process that governs the balance between assimilation and accommodation. When a learner's existing schemas are adequate to handle incoming experience, cognitive equilibrium exists. When experience produces systematic anomalies that schemas cannot accommodate, disequilibrium arises — a productive state of cognitive conflict that motivates accommodation. Piaget saw equilibration as the fundamental engine of cognitive development: the organism's drive toward increasingly stable and adequate cognitive organization.
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Equilibration]]*

## _R

> [!definition] **Transfer** **[perm-note]**
> The application of knowledge, skills, or strategies learned in one context to a different context. Near transfer involves application to closely similar situations; far transfer involves application across significantly different domains or contexts. Research on critical thinking transfer indicates that it is not automatic and requires deliberate instructional support — including explicit attention to the general principles underlying domain-specific instances of reasoning.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Transfer]]*

> [!definition] **Transfer of Learning** **[perm-note]**
> The application of knowledge or skills acquired in one context to a new, different context. Transfer is the ultimate goal of CT instruction but the most persistent challenge: CT skills learned in one setting frequently fail to transfer without explicit instruction in the principles underlying the skills, practice across varied contexts, and metacognitive awareness of when to apply CT.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Transfer-of-Learning|Transfer of Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Transfer-Appropriate Processing** **[perm-note]**
> The principle that memory performance is optimized when the type of cognitive processing performed at encoding *transfers appropriately* to (i.e., overlaps with) the type of cognitive processing required at retrieval. Rather than claiming that deep processing is universally superior, TAP claims that the best encoding for any given test is the encoding that most closely matches what the test requires. Shallow processing can outperform deep processing if the retrieval test demands shallow features.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Transfer-Appropriate-Processing|Transfer-Appropriate Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Reading Fluency** **[perm-note]**
> Reading fluency is the ability to read connected text with accuracy, appropriate rate, and prosody — with sufficient ease that comprehension resources are available to focus on meaning-making rather than word identification. Fluency is not merely reading speed; it integrates accuracy (getting words right), automaticity (retrieving them without deliberate effort), and prosody (reading with appropriate expression, phrasing, and intonation that reflects comprehension of grammatical and semantic structure).
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Reading-Fluency|Reading Fluency]]*

> [!definition] **Reconstructive Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which recall involves active reconstruction of a plausible account from schema-consistent fragments, defaults, and elaborations, rather than retrieval of a faithful, intact record. Proposed by [[Frederic-Bartlett]] (1932) and supported by extensive subsequent research. Reconstructive memory explains systematic recall errors, schema-consistent intrusions, and the rationalization of unfamiliar material toward familiar frameworks. Boundary: the reconstructive account does not deny that verbatim surface memory is sometimes possible; it claims that schema-guided reconstruction is ...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Reconstructive-Memory|Reconstructive Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Redundancy Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The counterintuitive finding that adding redundant information to a presentation can impair rather than improve learning. When learners can understand a presentation from a single self-contained source, requiring them to also process a second source that conveys the same information imposes extraneous load without informational benefit. Implies that spoken narration + diagram + identical written text may be worse than spoken narration + diagram alone.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Redundancy-Effect|Redundancy Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Tree of Thoughts Framework** **[perm-note]**
> [**Tree-of-Thoughts**:: An extension of CoT that structures reasoning as a tree where each node represents a partial solution state (a "thought"), edges represent reasoning steps, and the model systematically explores this solution space through search algorithms (breadth-first search, depth-first search, or beam search), evaluating branch quality via self-generated assessments before committing to paths.]^established
> 
> *Source: prompt-report-chain-of-thought-logic-2025122305* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Tree-of-Thoughts-Framework|Tree of Thoughts Framework]]*

> [!definition] **Three-Tier Monitoring Architecture** **[perm-note]**
> An integrative model of comprehension monitoring distinguishing Tier 1 (lexical-syntactic monitoring), Tier 2 (propositional coherence monitoring), and Tier 3 (situational coherence monitoring). Each tier has distinct failure modes and remediation strategies. The central claim is that most consequential comprehension failures occur at Tier 3, while most strategy instruction targets Tier 1.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Three-Tier-Monitoring-Architecture|Three-Tier Monitoring Architecture]]*

> [!definition] **Reflective Judgment** **[perm-note]**
> The capacity to reason well about ill-structured problems — problems in which evidence is incomplete, experts disagree, and certainty is not achievable. Reflective judgment develops through seven stages from pre-reflective (assuming knowledge is certain) to genuinely reflective (constructing well-reasoned positions in conditions of acknowledged uncertainty). A developmental prerequisite for mature critical thinking.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Reflective-Judgment|Reflective Judgment]]*

> [!definition] **Reflective Thinking** **[perm-note]**
> "Active, persistent, and careful consideration of any belief or supposed form of knowledge in the light of the grounds that support it, and the further conclusions to which it tends" (*How We Think*, 1910). Distinguished from asocial or "idle" speculation by its orientation toward the resolution of genuine problems in lived experience.
> 
> *Source: report-01-the-inquiry-engine* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Reflective-Thinking|Reflective Thinking]]*

> [!definition] **Region of Proximal Learning** **[perm-note]**
> The motivationally optimal zone of moderate epistemic uncertainty — close enough to understanding that curiosity is activated, far enough from certainty that inquiry is necessary. The psychological sweet spot where learning is most efficiently activated, connecting metacognitive research to Vygotsky's Zone of Proximal Development (see Report 04).
> 
> *Source: report-01-the-inquiry-engine* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Region-of-Proximal-Learning|Region of Proximal Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Regulation of Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> **Regulation of Cognition** consists of five control processes that actively manage cognitive operations during task execution. This is the "operating system" executing strategic decisions.
> 
> **Planning**: Goal specification and strategy selection before execution. Setting sub-goals ("First, identify the argument structure; second, evaluate each premise; third, assess inference validity"), activating relevant knowledge ("I'll need to apply Bayes' theorem here"), allocating time and attention resources.
> 
> **Information Management**: Organizational strategies for processing information efficien...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-critical-thinking-metacognitive-regulation* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulation-of-Cognition|Regulation of Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Overhead** **[perm-note]**
> Original analytical concept developed in this report. The additional cognitive-affective burden imposed by controlled motivation (specifically introjected regulation) — the self-surveillance, performance anxiety, and contingent self-esteem management that compete with cognitive resources available for task engagement. Explains why autonomous motivation predicts not just greater effort but qualitatively deeper and more creative processing.
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Overhead|Regulatory Overhead]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Poverty** **[perm-note]**
> A state in which a learner's strategic repertoire is insufficiently developed to provide adequate responses to monitoring signals. Even accurate monitoring coupled with threshold-crossing salience produces only generic, minimally effective regulatory responses when the repertoire is thin. Distinguished from monitoring failure and threshold insensitivity as a third independent source of coupling breakdown.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Poverty|Regulatory Poverty]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Reflexivity Problem** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The theoretical challenge created by Pintrich's inclusion of motivation as a regulated domain: if motivation is regulated by a regulatory executive, that executive must itself have some motivational basis — what motivates the motivation-regulator? The problem resolves not by positing a fifth unregulated level but by recognizing that regulatory activity is bootstrapped from dispositional motivational tendencies (trait self-efficacy, general mastery orientation) that are products of developmental history. See Phase VI Synthesis.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** This is the r...
> 
> *Source: pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Reflexivity-Problem|Regulatory Reflexivity Problem]]*

> [!definition] **The Regulatory Spectrum** **[perm-note]**
> OIT proposes six regulatory styles arranged from least to most autonomous:
> 
> 1. **Amotivation** — absence of intentional regulation; the person does not act or acts without intention
> 2. **External Regulation** — behavior controlled by external rewards and punishments; fully controlled regulation
> 3. **Introjected Regulation** — regulation by internalized pressures: ego-involvement, guilt, shame, contingent self-worth; still controlled, but the controlling force is now internal
> 4. **Identified Regulation** — the person consciously values the goal and acts from that valuation; autonomous, thoug...
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Regulatory-Spectrum|The Regulatory Spectrum]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Style** **[perm-note]**
> The characteristic reason or perceived locus of causality underlying a given behavior. Regulatory styles describe *why* a person engages in an activity, ranging from pure external compulsion through various degrees of internalization to fully autonomous self-regulation. OIT is specifically concerned with the continuum of regulatory styles applied to *extrinsic* behaviors — those not initially intrinsically motivated.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Style|Regulatory Style]]*

> [!definition] **Regulatory Threshold** **[perm-note]**
> The minimum signal strength required to activate regulatory processes. Below the threshold, monitoring signals are registered but produce no behavioral change (the cognitive "dead band"). The threshold is modulated by motivational orientation, cognitive load, self-efficacy, and temporal discounting. See [[Monitoring-Control-Coupling]].
> 
> *Source: metacognition-in-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: cognitive-psychology/metacognition*
> *Permanent Note: [[Regulatory-Threshold|Regulatory Threshold]]*

> [!definition] **Relatedness** **[perm-note]**
> The need to feel securely connected to others, to experience care and belonging, and to feel that one matters to the people around them. Relatedness in SDT does not require close friendship or intimacy — it can be satisfied by a classroom culture where students feel respected and valued by their teacher and peers. The critical functional requirement is felt security: that one's standing in the relational environment is not contingent on performance.
> 
> *Source: sdt-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relatedness]]*

> [!definition] **Relatedness Need** **[perm-note]**
> The need for [[Relatedness]] is the need to experience *genuine connection* with others — to feel that one belongs, that one matters to others, and that others matter to oneself. This is more than affiliation; it requires a sense of mutual care and significance rather than merely proximity or social contact. In educational contexts, the relatedness need encompasses connections to instructors, peers, and the broader learning community. Importantly, relatedness satisfaction does not require large social networks — a single deeply satisfying relational connection can substantially fulfill the ...
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relatedness-Need|Relatedness Need]]*

> [!definition] **Relational-Emergent Autonomy** **[perm-note]**
> The reconstruction's key novel concept: autonomous motivation as a property that emerges in the transaction between a goal-directed person and an autonomy-supportive social context, rather than as an internal biological need that contexts either satisfy or frustrate. Reframes intervention from "satisfy the need" to "create the transaction conditions in which autonomous motivation can emerge."
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relational-Emergent-Autonomy|Relational-Emergent Autonomy]]*

> [!definition] **Relationship Motivation Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The seventh mini-theory of Self-Determination Theory, developed by Deci and Ryan, which examines how the basic psychological needs for autonomy, competence, and relatedness operate within close personal relationships and how the need-support or need-thwarting character of a relationship determines both its quality and its effects on participants' broader well-being. RMT does NOT reduce to the claim that good relationships are beneficial; it specifies the motivational mechanisms that distinguish beneficial from harmful relationships.
> 
> *Source: relationship-motivation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relationship-Motivation-Theory|Relationship Motivation Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Relative Autonomy Index** **[perm-note]**
> A composite measure of regulatory style derived by weighting individual regulation subscale scores: external and introjected regulations receive negative weights, identified and integrated regulations receive positive weights, intrinsic motivation receives the highest positive weight. The RAI produces a single continuous index from highly controlled to highly autonomous, operationalizing OIT's core dimension for research purposes. Subject to scoring convention debates in the literature.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Relative-Autonomy-Index|Relative Autonomy Index]]*

> [!definition] **Repeated Reading** **[perm-note]**
> An evidence-based fluency intervention in which students read short passages multiple times until reaching a fluency criterion (typically a target WCPM score), with corrective feedback from a tutor or teacher. Samuels demonstrated that repeated reading produces not only fluency gains on practiced passages but generalization to unpracticed text, indicating that practice builds transferable decoding efficiency rather than merely word-specific memorization.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Repeated-Reading|Repeated Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Research Context** **[perm-note]**
> **Primary Question**: <% tp.frontmatter.research_question %>
> **Research Phase**: <% tp.frontmatter.research_phase %>
> **Discipline**: <% tp.frontmatter.academic_discipline %>
> **LLM Used**: <% tp.frontmatter.llm_model %> (<% tp.frontmatter.generation_date %>)
> 
> *Source: reference-technical-advanced-templater-note-templates-20251118225816* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Research-Context|Research Context]]*

> [!definition] **Research Question** **[perm-note]**
> **Primary Question**: <% tp.frontmatter.research_topic %>
> 
> **Sub-Questions:**
> 1. 
> 2. 
> 3.
> 
> *Source: reference-technical-advanced-templater-note-templates-20251118225816* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Research-Question|Research Question]]*

> [!definition] **Restructuring** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The process by which entirely new schemas are created, typically through: (a) *patterning* — inducing a new schema from regularities across experience, (b) *schema induction* — creating a new schema by abstracting a pattern from an analogous domain, or (c) *analogy* — building a new schema by structural mapping from an existing schema in a different domain.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** Restructuring is the closest Schema Theory comes to a genuine construction mechanism. It is, however, underspecified in Rumelhart & Norman's original account — the trigger conditions, the time co...
> 
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Restructuring]]*

> [!definition] **Retrieval Practice Effect** **[perm-note]**
