> The robust finding that attempting to retrieve information from memory produces substantially better long-term retention than re-studying the same material for equivalent time, even when initial retrieval is difficult or incomplete. The benefit persists and often increases over longer retention intervals. Mechanisms proposed include strengthening of retrieval routes, elaborative encoding during effortful retrieval, and error-correction processes engaged by retrieval failure. A core instance of the broader principle of desirable difficulties. Practically, this means that low-stakes frequent ...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Retrieval-Practice-Effect|Retrieval Practice Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Retrieval-Writing** **[perm-note]**
> Writing performed without access to source material, requiring the writer to retrieve from memory rather than transcribe from a source. Activates the testing effect mechanism and the articulatory construction mechanism simultaneously. Produces less fluent, less complete notes than note-taking — and, in most circumstances, more durable encoding.
> 
> *Source: writing-to-learn-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Retrieval-Writing]]*

> [!definition] **Reward Contingency Types** **[perm-note]**
> The structural relationship between performance and reward. Three types matter most for CET: (1) **Task-contingent rewards** — given simply for participating in or completing the task, without reference to quality (e.g., "here is a sticker for drawing"). (2) **Completion-contingent rewards** — given for completing the task to a basic standard. (3) **Performance-contingent rewards** — given in proportion to quality of performance (e.g., "top marks get a prize"). CET predicts that task-contingent and completion-contingent rewards carry primarily a controlling functional significance and relia...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Reward-Contingency-Types|Reward Contingency Types]]*

> [!definition] **Hermeneutic Circle** **[perm-note]**
> The interpretive principle, derived from hermeneutic philosophy, that understanding a part of a text requires reference to the whole, and understanding the whole requires reference to the parts. Silent reading's recursive re-entry affordance operationalizes this principle cognitively, enabling the iterative revision of interpretation that constitutes genuine textual analysis.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-critical-analysis-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Hermeneutic-Circle|Hermeneutic Circle]]*

> [!definition] **Routing Decision Factors** **[perm-note]**
> The main agent considers:
> 
> **1. Task complexity**:
> - Simple (single agent)
> - Moderate (2-3 agents sequentially)  
> - Complex (5+ agents, potentially parallel)
> 
> **2. Domain matching**:
> - Keywords in user query vs. agent descriptions
> - Trigger phrase alignment
> - Explicit user requests ("use X agent")
> 
> **3. Dependency analysis**:
> - Sequential: B depends on A's output
> - Parallel: A and B are independent
> - Conditional: C only needed if A finds issues
> 
> **4. Resource constraints**:
> - Token budget (prefer Haiku agents for volume)
> - Time sensitivity (prefer parallel for speed)
> - Quality requirements ...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Routing-Decision-Factors|Routing Decision Factors]]*

> [!definition] **Error Categories in Multi-Agent Systems** **[perm-note]**
> **1. Agent invocation errors**:
> - Agent not found (typo in name)
> - Agent unavailable (resource limits)
> - Tool permission denied
> 
> **2. Agent execution errors**:
> - Agent reaches token limit mid-task
> - Tool execution fails (file not found, command error)
> - Agent produces invalid output format
> 
> **3. Coordination errors**:
> - Circular dependencies between agents
> - Conflicting state updates
> - Deadlock (agents waiting on each other)
> 
> **4. Quality errors**:
> - Agent completes but output doesn't meet criteria
> - Security agent finds critical vulnerabilities
> - Test agent reports failures
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Error-Categories-in-Multi-Agent-Systems|Error Categories in Multi-Agent Systems]]*

> [!definition] **RSVP** **[perm-note]**
> A text display mode presenting words one at a time at a fixed rate, eliminating saccadic eye movements. Used in speed-reading applications and research. RSVP disables strategic rereading and comprehension-monitoring-driven gaze control, producing comprehension deficits relative to normal reading at equivalent word rates for complex text.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[RSVP]]*

> [!definition] **Rubric** **[perm-note]**
> A structured framework describing levels of performance on relevant criteria. In autonomy-supportive feedback design, rubrics serve primarily as a *metacognitive scaffold* — externalising the evaluative standards that learners must internalise in order to self-assess — rather than as a scoring device. Effective rubrics describe *qualitative differences* in performance at each level rather than accumulating points, and use language accessible to learners at the relevant developmental stage.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Rubric]]*

## _S

> [!definition] **Satisfaction-Frustration Asymmetry** **[perm-note]**
> The empirically established principle that need frustration and need absence are not points on the same continuum but qualitatively distinct states with different outcome profiles. Operationalized in the BPNSFS, which uses separate subscales for satisfaction and frustration of each need and shows that frustration predicts ill-being and controlled motivation above and beyond what is predicted by low satisfaction alone.
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Satisfaction-Frustration-Asymmetry|Satisfaction-Frustration Asymmetry]]*

> [!definition] **Satisfaction-Frustration-Asymmetry** **[perm-note]**
> The finding that need satisfaction and need frustration are not mirror images (endpoints of a single scale) but qualitatively distinct processes with different causal antecedents and consequence profiles. Need frustration produces active damage that cannot be fully remediated by restoring need satisfaction, constituting SDT as a theory of psychological damage as well as flourishing.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Satisfaction-Frustration-Asymmetry]]*

> [!definition] **Scaffold Dependence** **[perm-note]**
> The failure mode in which a learner develops reliable performance in a scaffolded environment without developing the internal capacity to perform independently when the scaffold is removed. Scaffold dependence results from scaffolding that is too effective at reducing challenge, from insufficient fading, or from failure to transfer metacognitive strategies to conditions that lack the scaffolding's contextual cues.
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Scaffold-Dependence|Scaffold Dependence]]*

> [!definition] **Scaffolded Fading** **[perm-note]**
> The planned, gradual withdrawal of scaffolding support as learner competence develops, ultimately requiring independent performance. [[Scaffolded-Fading]] is the mechanism by which scaffolding becomes a developmental rather than a permanent support. Without fading, scaffolding produces competence at performing tasks with support but not competence at the task itself. The timing and gradient of fading is one of the most important and underspecified aspects of scaffolding design.
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Scaffolded-Fading|Scaffolded Fading]]*

> [!definition] **Scaffolding — Instructional Design** **[perm-note]**
> Contingent support provided to a learner working within their [[Zone-of-Proximal-Development]] that enables task performance currently beyond independent capability. Six defining functions: recruitment (engaging learner interest in the task), reduction of degrees of freedom (simplifying the task without eliminating its essential structure), direction maintenance (keeping the learner focused on the goal), marking critical features (highlighting key errors and relevant task features), frustration control (managing the affective response to difficulty), and demonstration (modelling ideal solut...
> 
> *Source: metacognitive-scaffolding-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Scaffolding-—-Instructional-Design|Scaffolding — Instructional Design]]*

> [!definition] **Schema** **[perm-note]**
> In cognitive science, a [[Schema]] is an organized pattern of thought or behavior that organizes information and relationships among information. Schemas act as mental frameworks that help us interpret new information in relation to existing knowledge structures.
> 
> *Source: topic-set-cognitive-science-pkb-achitecture-202511202330* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Attractor** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: A reconceptualization of schemas as soft stable states (attractors) in a cognitive landscape shaped by statistical learning over embodied, culturally situated experience. Unlike a data structure (discrete, pre-formed, retrieved), an attractor is reconstructed on each instantiation, is sensitive to context (the full constellation of current cues), and emerges from regularization over distributed experience. NOT identical to a connectionist "attractor network" in the technical sense, though informed by that architecture.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: The core reconstructiv...
> 
> *Attribution: this report*
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Attractor|Schema Attractor]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Automation** **[perm-note]**
> The process by which frequently activated [[knowledge-schemas]] in long-term memory become faster and less resource-demanding in working memory, eventually retrievable and deployable as single units rather than networks of individually active elements. Schema automation is the mechanism that underlies expertise: as schemas automate, effective element interactivity decreases, freeing working memory capacity for new learning. Schema automation is theorized to be the goal of all instructional schemas — what [[Germane-Load]] is supposed to support.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Automation|Schema Automation]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Automation in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> [[Schema-Automation]] in the reading context refers to the process by which orthographic patterns — from individual grapheme-phoneme correspondences to morphemes, syllables, and whole words — become encoded as unified long-term memory schemas that can be retrieved as wholes rather than constructed from parts. When the spelling pattern "tion" has been encountered sufficient times, its phonological equivalent /ʃən/ can be retrieved as a unit from long-term memory rather than assembled from four separate grapheme-phoneme correspondences. The automated schema bypasses the capacity-limited worki...
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Automation-in-Reading|Schema Automation in Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Construction** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** The cognitive process by which a new schema is created where no adequate prior schema existed. Distinguished from accommodation (modifying an existing schema) and accretion (adding information to an existing schema). Primary mechanisms: analogical induction from structurally isomorphic source domains, pattern induction from regularities across multiple examples.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** Schema construction is not the same as learning. Most learning involves schema activation (assimilation) or accommodation; schema construction occurs only when input cannot be assimilated or...
> 
> *Attribution: restructuring mode*
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Construction|Schema Construction]]*

> [!definition] **Schema Crystallization Event** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A qualitative threshold event in schema construction at which an accumulating set of loosely organized knowledge elements undergoes rapid structural consolidation into a coherent, retrievable schema. Pre-crystallization, knowledge elements are available but not organized into a single retrievable unit; post-crystallization, the schema can be activated as a whole.
> 
> **Report-Specific Significance:** The crystallization event model is central to the analysis of Phase IV. It captures something that smooth, incremental accounts of schema formation miss: the discontinuous, thresho...
> 
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Schema-Crystallization-Event|Schema Crystallization Event]]*

> [!definition] **SDL as Educational Goal** **[perm-note]**
> The aspiration that learners develop the capacity and inclination to take increasing responsibility for their own learning throughout life. In this sense, SDL names what educators are working toward when they design autonomy-supportive environments, employ learning contracts, or engage in coaching rather than direct instruction. This usage is normative rather than descriptive — it says what education *should* produce, rather than what is happening in a given learning episode.
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[SDL-as-Educational-Goal|SDL as Educational Goal]]*

> [!definition] **Secure Base** **[perm-note]**
> In Attachment Theory, the relational foundation provided by a secure attachment figure that enables exploratory behavior. Used in this report as a cross-theoretical parallel to the BPNT mechanism by which relatedness satisfaction (relational security) enables autonomous engagement.
> 
> *Source: basic-psychological-needs-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Secure-Base|Secure Base]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Agent Coordinator** **[perm-note]**
> <span style='color: #FFC700;'>**Self-Agent Coordinator:**</span> An AI agent operating mode where the agent autonomously manages context loading, session continuity, resource discovery, and task execution within a structured Personal Knowledge Base. The agent acts as both executor and orchestrator, intelligently navigating vault resources without requiring step-by-step user instruction.
> 
> *Source: pkb-report-llm-agent-coordination-system-implemntation-guide-pkb+codebase-scaffold-2025122311* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Agent-Coordinator|Self-Agent Coordinator]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Concordance Model** **[perm-note]**
> A related SDT framework proposing that goals vary in how concordant they are with the person's authentic values and core self. Self-concordant goals — pursued because they reflect genuine values and interests rather than external pressure — are associated with greater sustained effort, more positive affect during pursuit, and greater well-being benefits upon attainment. The Self-Concordance Model complements GCT by explaining the motivational quality of goal engagement, while GCT explains the content of what is aspired toward.
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Concordance-Model|Self-Concordance Model]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Consistency** **[perm-note]**
> [**Self-Consistency**:: A prompting reliability technique that generates multiple independent [[Chain-of-Thought]] reasoning paths for a single query through high-temperature sampling, then aggregates final answers via [[Majority Voting]] to select the most statistically consistent conclusion, thereby mitigating the stochasticity and potential errors inherent in single-sample LLM generation.]^established
> 
> *Source: prompt-report-self-consistency-complexity-based-consistency-202512250916* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Consistency]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Determination** **[perm-note]**
> The degree to which behavior arises from a person's own volition rather than from external compulsion or internal pressure. Self-determination is the overarching dimension that the regulatory continuum tracks — from fully non-self-determined (amotivation, external regulation) through partially self-determined (introjected) to fully self-determined (identified, integrated, intrinsic). Self-determination is the conceptual core of SDT and the measured quantity in OIT research.
> 
> *Source: organismic-integration-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Determination]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Determination Theory** **[perm-note]**
> A macro-theory of human motivation and personality developed by **Edward L. Deci** and **Richard M. Ryan**. It asserts that humans are active organisms with innate tendencies toward psychological growth and integration. SDT posits that optimal functioning, well-being, and high-quality motivation depend on the continuous satisfaction of three universal psychological needs: **Autonomy** (the need to feel volition and self-endorsement of one's actions), **Competence** (the need to feel effective in one's interactions with the environment), and **Relatedness** (the need to feel connected to and...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-self-determination-theory-as-a-driver-for-sustainable-knowledge-management-habits-20251126101939* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Determination-Theory|Self-Determination Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Determined Self-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> Integrative concept developed in this report. The state in which both motivational quality (autonomous, need-satisfying — SDT level) and regulatory architecture (sophisticated, cyclical, well-calibrated — SRL level) are jointly optimal. Characterised by deliberate practice that is phenomenologically experienced as vitalising rather than depleting. Neither high autonomous motivation nor sophisticated SRL alone produces this state; it requires their integration.
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Determined-Self-Regulation|Self-Determined Self-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Directed Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A process in which individuals take the initiative, with or without the assistance of others, in diagnosing their learning needs, formulating learning goals, identifying human and material resources for learning, choosing and implementing appropriate learning strategies, and evaluating learning outcomes. SDL in this sense is a description of *how* learning is organised and managed — it specifies a set of activities and their sequence, not a personal characteristic. A learner engages in SDL when they perform these activities; they fail to engage in SDL when these activities are performed by ...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Directed-Learning|Self-Directed Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Directed Learning Readiness Scale** **[perm-note]**
> A 58-item psychometric instrument measuring attitudes, abilities, and characteristics associated with SDL readiness across eight factors: openness to learning, self-concept as an effective learner, initiative and independence, informed acceptance of responsibility, love of learning, creativity, future orientation, and basic study and problem-solving skills. Widely used in research and practice for over four decades; subject to ongoing psychometric debate regarding factor structure stability and cross-cultural validity. Most defensible as a general screening tool rather than a diagnostic pro...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Directed-Learning-Readiness-Scale|Self-Directed Learning Readiness Scale]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Directedness — Trait or Readiness Sense** **[perm-note]**
> A relatively stable cluster of attitudes, abilities, and personal characteristics associated with taking initiative and responsibility in learning. This includes openness to learning opportunities, self-concept as an effective and independent learner, initiative and independence in learning, informed acceptance of responsibility for one's own learning, love of learning, creativity, future orientation, and the ability to use basic study and problem-solving skills. Self-directedness, in this sense, is not something one does but something one *has* — a readiness or propensity that varies acros...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Directedness-—-Trait-or-Readiness-Sense|Self-Directedness — Trait or Readiness Sense]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Efficacy** **[perm-note]**
> Belief in one's capacity to perform a specific task in a specific domain. Distinguished from general confidence or general intelligence beliefs by its situational specificity. High self-efficacy is predictive of choice, effort, and persistence in that specific domain. Self-efficacy is primarily built through mastery experiences (actual successes), not through instruction about ability beliefs. The empirical foundation for Axiom 2 of this report.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Efficacy]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Explanation Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The finding that learners who generate explanatory text while studying material — articulating the causal mechanisms behind worked examples, definitions, or claims — develop significantly deeper understanding than those who read the same material without generating explanations.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Explanation-Effect|Self-Explanation Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Management** **[perm-note]**
> In Garrison's three-component SDL model, the behavioural dimension of SDL: the learner's organised control of the external learning environment, including time allocation, resource identification and selection, and task structuring. Self-management is necessary but not sufficient for SDL; it must be accompanied by self-monitoring and appropriate motivation. Self-management failures (disorganisation, resource misselection, time management breakdown) are a common proximate cause of SDL failure, though their ultimate causes often lie in motivational quality or metacognitive readiness.
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Management]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Monitoring** **[perm-note]**
> In Garrison's three-component SDL model, the cognitive and metacognitive dimension of SDL: the learner's ongoing assessment of their own understanding, identification of comprehension failures, and deployment of remedial strategies. Self-monitoring requires metacognitive capacity that many learners do not apply spontaneously, particularly in domains where they lack background knowledge. The competence problem in SDL — that novices cannot accurately diagnose their own learning needs or evaluate their own understanding — is precisely a self-monitoring failure that must be addressed through in...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Monitoring]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Reference Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The robust empirical finding that encoding information with reference to the self (asking whether information applies to one's own traits or experiences) produces superior free recall compared to impersonal semantic encoding. Attributed to the self-concept functioning as a maximally rich, well-organized associative structure that provides numerous retrieval pathways. Modulated by cultural context: collectivistic cultures may show analogous effects for close in-group others.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Reference-Effect|Self-Reference Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Reflection Phase** **[perm-note]**
> The third phase of the cyclical model, encompassing self-judgment (comparing performance to goals and attributing outcomes causally) and self-reaction (emotional and adaptive/defensive inference responses). The output of this phase feeds directly into the next forethought cycle.
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Reflection-Phase|Self-Reflection Phase]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Regulated Learning** **[perm-note]**
> The capacity of learners to monitor, direct, and regulate their own cognitive, motivational, and behavioural processes in service of self-set or internalised learning goals. Self-regulated learners can generate their own feedback through self-monitoring, identify discrepancies between current and desired performance, and select and deploy strategies to close those gaps. The developmental endpoint toward which autonomy-supportive feedback design is aimed.
> 
> *Source: feedback-design-autonomy-mastery-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Regulated-Learning|Self-Regulated Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Self-Regulated Learning / SRL** **[perm-note]**
> The proactive, metacognitively-directed management of one's own cognitive, motivational, behavioural, and contextual processes in service of self-set learning goals. SRL is distinguished from simple learning effort by its cyclical, self-directed quality — learners monitor and regulate their own processes rather than simply responding to external instruction. *Note: SRL frameworks describe the cognitive architecture of regulation; SDT describes the motivational quality of its enactment.*
> 
> *Source: sdt-srl-comparative-synthesis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Self-Regulated-Learning-SRL|Self-Regulated Learning / SRL]]*

> [!definition] **Semantic Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The subsystem of declarative memory responsible for general knowledge about the world — facts, concepts, word meanings, encyclopaedic knowledge — divorced from the specific episodes in which they were acquired. When you know that Paris is the capital of France, you typically do not remember the specific occasion on which you learned this; the episodic context has been lost while the semantic content persists. Semantic memory is relatively resistant to the organic amnesias that devastate episodic memory, suggesting some neurological independence between the two systems.
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Semantic-Memory|Semantic Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Sensory Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The first stage of the Atkinson-Shiffrin model. A large-capacity, very brief-duration store that holds raw perceptual information — visual, auditory, or tactile — in a nearly unprocessed form for fractions of a second to perhaps one to two seconds. [[Iconic-Memory]] (visual) and [[Echoic-Memory]] (auditory) are the primary sensory registers. Most sensory information decays before receiving conscious attention; only information attended to is transferred to short-term memory. The sensory register is NOT the same as perception itself; it is a pre-attentive buffer preceding full conscious proc...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Sensory-Memory|Sensory Memory]]*

> [!definition] **The Seven Intellectual Traits** **[perm-note]**
> **Intellectual Humility**: Awareness of limits of one's knowledge, bias susceptibility, tendency toward self-deception. Opposite: intellectual arrogance.
> - Manifests as willingness to admit "I don't know" and "I was wrong"
> 
> **Intellectual Courage**: Willingness to examine threatening ideas, face cognitive dissonance, question cherished beliefs. Opposite: intellectual cowardice.
> - Manifests as engaging with uncomfortable evidence
> 
> **Intellectual Empathy**: Accurate understanding of others' viewpoints, including reasons for disagreement. Opposite: intellectual narrow-mindedness.
> - Manifests a...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-foundational-critical-thinking* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Seven-Intellectual-Traits|The Seven Intellectual Traits]]*

> [!definition] **Shallow Processing** **[perm-note]**
> Encoding that focuses on the physical or acoustic properties of a stimulus rather than its meaning. For verbal material, structural processing attends to visual features (uppercase or lowercase, font, length), while phonemic processing attends to sound (does this word rhyme with another?). Neither operation requires the processor to access semantic knowledge. Traces produced by shallow processing are relatively fragile and transient.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Shallow-Processing|Shallow Processing]]*

> [!definition] **Shared vs. Personal Agents** **[perm-note]**
> **Team-shared agents** (committed to git):
> - Project-specific domain experts
> - Standard workflow automation
> - Code review standards
> - Architecture validation rules
> 
> **Personal agents** (local only):
> - Individual coding style preferences
> - Personal productivity automation
> - Learning/experimentation agents
> - Developer-specific workflows
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Shared-vs.-Personal-Agents|Shared vs. Personal Agents]]*

> [!definition] **Short-Term Memory** **[perm-note]**
> In the Atkinson-Shiffrin model, a limited-capacity (~7 ± 2 chunks), brief-duration (~15–30 seconds without rehearsal) store holding information that is currently active in consciousness. Information in STM is maintained through **rehearsal** (deliberate repetition) and is lost through either decay or displacement when the capacity limit is exceeded. Transfer to long-term memory was theorised to depend on rehearsal duration, though this relationship later proved more complex. STM was conceived as a passive holding buffer, a conception later replaced by the more dynamic [[Working-Memory]] model.
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Short-Term-Memory|Short-Term Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Signal Threshold Failure** **[perm-note]**
> The monitoring-regulation decoupling mechanism in which a comprehension failure generates a monitoring signal, but that signal's strength does not reach the threshold required to activate regulatory action. Occurs because many real-world comprehension failures — subtle causal inconsistencies, unstated assumptions, imprecise inferences — generate weak monitoring signals that are interpreted as unfamiliarity rather than genuine failure. Primary failure mode at Tier 2 and Tier 3 of the three-tier monitoring architecture.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Signal-Threshold-Failure|Signal Threshold Failure]]*

> [!definition] **Silent Reading Fluency** **[perm-note]**
> The capacity to read connected text silently with sufficient speed, accuracy, and automaticity to support deep comprehension. Distinguished from oral reading fluency by the absence of prosodic production demands, and from simple reading speed by its comprehension criterion.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Silent-Reading-Fluency|Silent Reading Fluency]]*

> [!definition] **Silent Reading Rate** **[perm-note]**
> Words processed per minute during silent reading with a target comprehension criterion (typically ≥70% on delayed recall or comprehension questions). Rate measured without a comprehension criterion is a speed metric, not a fluency metric.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Silent-Reading-Rate|Silent Reading Rate]]*

> [!definition] **Simple View of Reading** **[perm-note]**
> Reading Comprehension = Decoding × Language Comprehension. Reading comprehension is the product — not the sum — of two distinct and separable components: (1) decoding ability, the capacity to translate print to language, and (2) oral language comprehension, the capacity to understand language once it has been decoded. The multiplicative relationship implies that weakness in either component drives the product toward zero regardless of strength in the other.
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Simple-View-of-Reading|Simple View of Reading]]*

> [!definition] **Situated Cognition** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The theoretical position that cognitive processes — including schema-like knowledge structures — are not solely in individual heads but are constituted by and distributed across social practices, material artifacts, and intersubjective relationships. Knowing is not a state of an individual mind but a way of participating in socially organized activity. NOT a denial of individual cognition; a recontextualization of it.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: Provides the "distributed account" alternative to the schema-in-individual-heads model; pairs with embodied cognition as a ch...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Situated-Cognition|Situated Cognition]]*

> [!definition] **Situation Model** **[perm-note]**
> In discourse comprehension theory (van Dijk & Kintsch, 1983), a representation of the *situation* described by a text — not merely a propositional encoding of the text's surface content but a multidimensional representation including spatial, temporal, causal, and intentional structure. Closely related to mental models theory but developed independently and with a stronger empirical tradition in comprehension research.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: cognitive-science*
> *Permanent Note: [[Situation-Model|Situation Model]]*

> [!definition] **Situation-Model|Situation Model** **[perm-note]**
> The mental representation of the situation described by a text, integrating textual propositions with world knowledge through inference generation. Situation model construction is the goal of skilled reading and the standard against which genuine comprehension should be measured.
> 
> *Source: silent-reading-fluency-speed-comprehension-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Situation-ModelSituation-Model|Situation-Model|Situation Model]]*

> [!definition] **Slot / Default Value** **[perm-note]**
> A variable component within a schema that accepts specific values when the schema is instantiated in a particular context. Slots carry *default values* — the most probable or typical values in the absence of specific information. Default values drive schema-based inference, filling in unstated information during comprehension and reconstruction.
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-and-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Slot-Default-Value|Slot / Default Value]]*

> [!definition] **Smart Connections** **[perm-note]**
> [**Smart-Connections**:: An [[Obsidian]] plugin that provides local-first [[Semantic Search]] using [[AI Embeddings]]. It enables finding conceptually related notes even when they don't share explicit links or keywords. The plugin runs entirely on-device after initial embedding, requiring no API calls for search functionality.]
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-text-generator-plugin-complete-api-interface-reference-2025121507* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Smart-Connections|Smart Connections]]*

> [!definition] **Socratic Method** **[perm-note]**
> A dialogic pedagogical technique in which the teacher poses a sequence of open, probing questions designed to surface the implicit assumptions in a learner's beliefs, reveal internal contradictions, and motivate the reconstruction of understanding on firmer evidential and logical grounds. Named for Socrates, whose practice is documented in Plato's dialogues.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Socratic-Method|Socratic Method]]*

> [!definition] **Soundness** **[perm-note]**
> A deductive argument is sound if it is both (a) valid and (b) actually has all true premises. Sound arguments guarantee true conclusions. Soundness is the gold standard for deductive arguments used to establish real-world conclusions, but evaluating soundness requires both logical skill (to check validity) and factual knowledge (to verify the premises).
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Soundness]]*

> [!definition] **Split-Attention Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The CLT-derived finding that instructional materials that require learners to mentally integrate spatially or temporally separated information impose extraneous cognitive load. The mental integration of split sources requires holding partial information in working memory while searching for and retrieving the related source, consuming working memory resources that could otherwise be devoted to schema construction. Eliminated by physically integrating related information in the presentation (e.g., labeling diagrams directly rather than providing separate legends).
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Split-Attention-Effect|Split-Attention Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Stage Characteristics and Training Implications** **[perm-note]**
> **Cognitive Stage**: Learners require explicit instruction, feedback, and conscious attention to every component. Performance is slow, effortful, variable, and error-prone. Training should emphasize understanding, provide models, and focus on accuracy over speed. **Associative Stage**: Errors decrease, performance becomes more consistent, speed increases following power law. Training should provide practice variability, maintain focus on error detection, and begin introducing realistic task conditions. **Autonomous Stage**: Performance becomes automatic, fast, reliable, and can be executed ...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-attentional-efficiency-and-skill-aquisition* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Stage-Characteristics-and-Training-Implications|Stage Characteristics and Training Implications]]*

> [!definition] **Standard Template Metadata** **[perm-note]**
> Based on the permanent note template structure, these are the queryable fields available in every note.
> 
> *Source: reference-taxonomy-current-metadata-system-2025121309* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Standard-Template-Metadata|Standard Template Metadata]]*

> [!definition] **State Management Approaches** **[perm-note]**
> **1. Shared Markdown Files** (simplest):
> - Single truth source: MULTI_AGENT_PLAN.md
> - Agents read → execute → write updates
> - Human-readable state (can inspect/debug)
> - Version controlled (git tracks state changes)
> 
> **2. JSON State Files** (structured):
> - .claude/state/features.json
> - Machine-readable for automation
> - Schema validation possible
> - Harder for humans to edit
> 
> **3. File System State** (implicit):
> - Presence of files indicates status
> - docs/requirements/X.md exists → requirements complete
> - docs/architecture/ADR-X.md exists → design complete
> - src/features/X/ exists → implementa...
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[State-Management-Approaches|State Management Approaches]]*

> [!definition] **Stereotype Threat** **[perm-note]**
> The situationally induced risk of confirming a negative stereotype about one's social group, which creates cognitive and emotional interference that undermines performance. Stereotype threat interacts with mindset: individuals who identify strongly with a domain and hold a fixed mindset about ability are most vulnerable to stereotype threat in that domain. Growth mindset interventions have been explored as potential buffers against stereotype threat.
> 
> *Source: growth-mindset-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Stereotype-Threat|Stereotype Threat]]*

> [!definition] **Testing Effect / Retrieval Practice Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The robust finding that actively retrieving information from memory produces substantially better long-term retention than re-studying the same material, even when total study time is held constant. The retrieval act, not the re-exposure, is the effective mechanism.
> 
> *Source: writing-techniques-effective-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Testing-Effect-Retrieval-Practice-Effect|Testing Effect / Retrieval Practice Effect]]*

> [!definition] **The Stoic Core Insight** **[perm-note]**
> **Events themselves are neutral.** Only our *judgments* about events create suffering or joy. This section externalizes those judgments for rational examination.
> 
> *Source: reference-instructional-daily-stoicism-2025120322* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Stoic-Core-Insight|The Stoic Core Insight]]*

> [!definition] **Stream of Consciousness** **[perm-note]**
> James's most famous psychological metaphor characterizing consciousness not as series of discrete states but as continuous, ever-changing flow: "Consciousness, then, does not appear to itself chopped up in bits. Such words as 'chain' or 'train' do not describe it fitly...It is nothing jointed; it flows. A 'river' or a 'stream' are the metaphors by which it is most naturally described." This conception revolutionized psychological thinking by emphasizing the [[continuity]], [[selectivity]], and [[personal]] character of conscious experience against earlier atomistic models treating mind as c...
> 
> *Source: cog-psy-william-james* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Stream-of-Consciousness|Stream of Consciousness]]*

> [!definition] **Structural Analogue** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition:** A source domain concept whose relational structure is isomorphic to a target domain concept, enabling structural mapping. A structural analogue is not a surface similarity (sharing perceptual or superficial features) but a deep similarity (sharing the pattern of relations among components). Used in schema construction to provide temporary organizational structure for a novel domain.
> 
> **Boundary conditions:** Structural analogy is not explanation — it does not tell the learner what the target concept IS, only how it is organized. Overextension of structural analogies (treatin...
> 
> *Attribution: adapted*
> *Source: schema-construction-problem-focused-analysis-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Structural-Analogue|Structural Analogue]]*

> [!definition] **Subagents vs. Coordinator** **[perm-note]**
> **Subagents** are specialized Claude instances with their own context windows, defined in markdown files with YAML frontmatter. The **coordinator** is not a separate file—it's the main Claude agent (the "general-purpose" agent) that you interact with directly. The coordinator analyzes tasks, routes them to appropriate subagents, and synthesizes their results.
> 
> *Source: multi-agent-systems-with-claude-code* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Subagents-vs.-Coordinator|Subagents vs. Coordinator]]*

> [!definition] **Subsequent Memory Effect** **[perm-note]**
> A neuroimaging finding in which activity during encoding — particularly in left inferior prefrontal and medial temporal regions — predicts subsequent memory success for the encoded item. Provides a neural-level validation of LOP predictions: deeper (semantic) encoding tasks produce greater subsequent memory effect magnitude, consistent with the claim that they produce more durable memory traces via richer cortical and hippocampal engagement.
> 
> *Source: levels-of-processing-foundational-report-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Subsequent-Memory-Effect|Subsequent Memory Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Subsumer / Anchoring Idea** **[perm-note]**
> An existing concept in a learner's long-term memory structure that is sufficiently inclusive and relevant to provide an organizational framework into which new, more specific material can be subsumed. The advance organizer either activates an existing subsumer (comparative organizer) or creates a new one (expository organizer). When no subsumer is available, meaningful learning cannot occur and rote memorization is the only available learning mode.
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Subsumer-Anchoring-Idea|Subsumer / Anchoring Idea]]*

> [!definition] **Surveillance** **[perm-note]**
> The awareness of being observed or monitored, even in the absence of explicit evaluative events. CET research (Lepper & Greene, 1975) shows that surveillance carries a controlling functional significance on its own: being watched introduces an external audience, makes the evaluative relationship salient, and tends to shift PLOC external. This finding has significant implications for digital learning environments in which learner behaviour is continuously logged and monitored, even if that monitoring is not connected to explicit rewards or consequences.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-evaluation-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Surveillance]]*

> [!definition] **Synaptic Consolidation** **[perm-note]**
> The cellular and molecular processes by which newly activated synaptic changes are stabilised over hours following encoding. The consolidation of synaptic changes requires gene expression and protein synthesis: disrupting protein synthesis shortly after learning (but not hours later) impairs long-term memory formation without affecting short-term retention. Synaptic consolidation transforms the initial, labile memory trace into a more structurally stable form. This process is largely automatic and does not require conscious rehearsal, though encoding conditions (depth of processing, emotion...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Synaptic-Consolidation|Synaptic Consolidation]]*

> [!definition] **Synthetic Model** **[perm-note]**
> In science education, a mental model that combines elements of a student's naive framework theory with partially absorbed scientific information, producing an internally consistent but scientifically incorrect model. Synthetic models arise when instruction presents correct scientific information without supporting genuine model revision: the student integrates the new information into the existing model framework rather than replacing it. The synthetic model concept explains why direct instruction on scientific facts often fails to resolve deep misconceptions.
> 
> *Source: mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Synthetic-Model|Synthetic Model]]*

> [!definition] **System 1** **[perm-note]**
> A mode of cognition that operates automatically, rapidly, and with little or no sense of conscious effort. System 1 processes generate immediate impressions, intuitions, feelings, and impulses. In political contexts, System 1 is implicated in quick evaluations of candidates based on facial appearance, gut-level reactions to policy proposals, and emotionally driven judgments about in-group and out-group members. System 1 is not inherently unreliable — many System 1 responses in familiar domains are accurate precisely because they encode learned patterns. But in novel, complex, or contested d...
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[System-1|System 1]]*

> [!definition] **System 1 / System 2** **[perm-note]**
> System 1 processes are fast, automatic, and largely unconscious — they produce rapid intuitive responses by pattern-matching current input to stored associations. System 2 processes are slow, effortful, and consciously controlled — they enable sequential, rule-governed reasoning including formal deductive inference. In deductive tasks, System 1 often generates plausible but logically invalid responses based on belief or semantic content; valid deductive reasoning typically requires System 2 to override these responses.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[System-1-System-2|System 1 / System 2]]*

> [!definition] **System 1 and System 2** **[perm-note]**
> The two processing systems of Dual Process Theory. System 1 is fast, automatic, pattern-based, and effortless — the default cognitive mode. System 2 is slow, deliberate, effortful, and logical — activated by specific triggers against the default. See Report 06 for extended treatment of System 1 governance.
> 
> *Source: report-01-the-inquiry-engine* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[System-1-and-System-2|System 1 and System 2]]*

> [!definition] **System 2** **[perm-note]**
> A mode of cognition that is deliberate, effortful, rule-governed, and conscious. System 2 is invoked when we carefully evaluate arguments, check evidence against claims, or deliberately override impulsive responses. In political contexts, System 2 engagement is what is typically meant by "careful reasoning" — but it is metabolically expensive and often deployed selectively, in the service of rationalizing conclusions that System 1 has already generated. The popular assumption that more System 2 engagement always means better political reasoning turns out to be empirically fragile.
> 
> *Source: political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[System-2|System 2]]*

> [!definition] **Systems Consolidation** **[perm-note]**
> The slower, large-scale process by which hippocampus-dependent memories are gradually transferred to neocortical long-term storage over months to years. The dominant account, the [[Standard Model of Consolidation]] (Squire & Alvarez, 1995), proposes that the hippocampus acts as an index that binds together distributed neocortical representations during encoding. During subsequent sleep and quiet wakefulness, coordinated replay between the hippocampus and neocortex allows the memory to become gradually independent of the hippocampus, as direct neocortical connections strengthen. This explain...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Systems-Consolidation|Systems Consolidation]]*

## _U

> [!definition] **Undermining Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The empirical phenomenon, first demonstrated by Deci (1971), in which the introduction of extrinsic rewards for an intrinsically interesting activity reduces subsequent intrinsic motivation when rewards are removed. Explained in CET as resulting from a shift in perceived locus of causality from internal (intrinsic interest) to external (reward). The replication literature for this effect is mixed; it is most robust for expected, tangible, contingent rewards for activities of initially moderate-to-high intrinsic interest.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-first-principles-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Undermining-Effect|Undermining Effect]]*

> [!definition] **The Undermining Effect** **[perm-note]**
> [[Undermining-Effect|The undermining effect]] (also called the [[Overjustification-Effect|overjustification effect]]) refers to the phenomenon whereby introducing external rewards for an intrinsically motivating activity reduces subsequent intrinsic motivation. It is predicted by [[Cognitive-Evaluation-Theory]] through the mechanism of the [[Perceived-Locus-of-Causality]]: when a person attributes their engagement to the reward rather than to themselves, the experience of volition is diminished, reducing intrinsic motivation. This is among SDT's most policy-consequential claims.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[The-Undermining-Effect|The Undermining Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Undermining-Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The empirical finding that external rewards contingent on intrinsically interesting activities reduce subsequent intrinsic motivation when the rewards are withdrawn. Mechanistically interpreted in this report as an existence proof that perceived locus of causality is causally potent — changes in how people experience their own agency produce measurable downstream motivational consequences.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Undermining-Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Undifferentiated Conception of Ability** **[perm-note]**
> In an undifferentiated conception of ability, effort and ability are treated as the same thing — or at minimum, as inseparable. A person who works harder is understood to be more able, and ability is seen as something that grows through effort. Under this conception, success means improving and learning, and failure means simply "I haven't learned this yet." This conception is developmentally earlier (young children tend to operate under it) and is characteristic of what Nicholls called *task involvement*. It corresponds roughly to what [[Carol-S.-Dweck]] would later call the [[Growth Minds...
> 
> *Source: achievement-goal-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Undifferentiated-Conception-of-Ability|Undifferentiated Conception of Ability]]*

> [!definition] **Universal Need** **[perm-note]**
> In SDT, a need is "universal" if its frustration produces ill-being for all humans regardless of cultural context, developmental stage, or individual difference. This is distinct from the claim that its satisfaction takes the same form across cultures. The dialectical process supports the former claim (universal frustration effects) much more strongly than the latter (universal satisfaction forms). A more precise SDT formulation would state: "All humans are reliably vulnerable to motivational deterioration when their autonomy, competence, or relatedness needs are frustrated, though what sat...
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Universal-Need|Universal Need]]*

> [!definition] **Heutagogy** **[perm-note]**
> An extension of SDL toward fully self-determined learning, in which learners not only take initiative in pursuing pre-specified goals but define their own competencies and learning pathways. Where SDL still operates within an institutional context of needs as diagnosed against some external standard, heutagogy imagines the learner as the author of both the standard and the process. Associated with double-loop learning (revision of underlying assumptions, not merely strategies) and capability development. Most applicable in professional and post-formal learning contexts where competencies ca...
> 
> *Source: self-directed-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Heutagogy]]*

## _V

> [!definition] **Validity** **[perm-note]**
> A deductive argument is valid if and only if there is no possible scenario — no logically conceivable world — in which the premises are all true and the conclusion is false. Validity is a formal, structural property of arguments; it says nothing about whether the premises are actually true. An argument can be perfectly valid while resting on wildly false premises, and the conclusion will then be guaranteed only conditionally: *if* the premises were true, the conclusion *would* have to be true.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Validity]]*

> [!definition] **Evaluation** **[perm-note]**
> Evaluation is the cognitive act of assessing the credibility, accuracy, logical validity, and contextual adequacy of claims, arguments, sources, and reasoning processes. Evaluation requires the application of intellectual standards — asking whether a claim is clear, accurate, precise, relevant, deep, broad, and logically sound. Importantly, evaluation applies to one's own reasoning as much as to external sources.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Evaluation]]*

> [!definition] **Vehicle Externalism** **[perm-note]**
> The position that the physical vehicles of mental states — the material processes that carry and implement cognitive states — can extend beyond the boundary of brain and body. Distinguished from content externalism, which holds that the contents of mental states are partly environmentally determined while the vehicles remain internal. Extended Mind Theory is the most radical form of vehicle externalism. Less radical forms hold that the body (rather than the brain alone) is the vehicle of cognition — a position associated with embodied cognition.
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Vehicle-Externalism|Vehicle Externalism]]*

> [!definition] **Verbal Efficiency Theory** **[perm-note]**
> Charles Perfetti's verbal efficiency theory refines LaBerge and Samuels by specifying the locus of the bottleneck more precisely. Verbal efficiency refers to the speed and quality of local text processes — particularly word identification and the maintenance of decoded words in [[Working-Memory]] long enough to be integrated into propositions and sentences. Inefficient local processes create a resource drain that impairs not just the speed of comprehension but its quality and completeness, because the products of word recognition may fade from working memory before they can be integrated in...
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Verbal-Efficiency-Theory|Verbal Efficiency Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Visuospatial Sketchpad** **[perm-note]**
> The component of working memory responsible for maintaining and manipulating visual and spatial information — mental images, spatial layouts, the appearance of objects. It appears to involve at least two subcomponents: a visual cache (storing object appearance) and an inner scribe (handling spatial and movement information). Evidence for its independence from the phonological loop comes from the fact that visual and verbal secondary tasks interfere with their respective working memory components selectively, not globally. The visuospatial sketchpad supports tasks like mental rotation, navig...
> 
> *Source: memory-systems-working-memory-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Visuospatial-Sketchpad|Visuospatial Sketchpad]]*

> [!definition] **Volitional Congruence** **[perm-note]**
> An alternative formulation of SDT's autonomy concept that emerges from this dialectical process. Rather than framing autonomy as self-authorship or independence, volitional congruence understands autonomy as *acting in ways that align with one's own values and relational commitments*. This formulation preserves the predictive content of SDT's autonomy need while making it coherent with collectivist cultural contexts where choosing to conform to one's group — when that choice is experienced as genuinely one's own — constitutes authentic autonomy rather than controlled compliance.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-19* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Volitional-Congruence|Volitional Congruence]]*

## _W

> [!definition] **Weak-Sense vs. Strong-Sense Critical Thinking** **[perm-note]**
> Weak-sense CT is the use of CT skills selectively, in service of one's own pre-existing beliefs and interests — deploying analytical tools to win arguments rather than to find truth. Strong-sense CT applies the same rigorous standards to one's own reasoning as to others', including the willingness to discover one's own errors and revise one's beliefs accordingly.
> 
> *Source: critical-thinking-educational-settings-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Weak-Sense-vs.-Strong-Sense-Critical-Thinking|Weak-Sense vs. Strong-Sense Critical Thinking]]*

> [!definition] **What is Frontmatter?** **[perm-note]**
> Frontmatter is a set of key-value pairs that defines the metadata for a specific note. It acts as the note's "specification sheet," providing context, status, and relational data that is separate from the note's main content (the "prose"). This separation is fundamental for automating [[03_notes/01_permanent-notes/01_cognitive-development/Knowledge Management]] workflows.
> 
> *Source: reference-guide-yaml-frontmatter-20251122124616* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[What-is-Frontmatter|What is Frontmatter?]]*

> [!definition] **Worked Example Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The empirically robust finding that novice learners acquire competence more efficiently by studying worked examples of solved problems than by solving equivalent problems themselves. Mechanistically explained by the finding that novices lack the schemas required for efficient problem-solving and therefore engage in working memory-intensive means-ends analysis that consumes resources without producing schemas. Worked examples provide schema-ready demonstrations that reduce this inefficiency. Effect size is consistent and substantial in well-structured domains; weakens and reverses with incre...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Worked-Example-Effect|Worked Example Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Working Memory** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive system responsible for temporarily holding and manipulating information during complex cognitive tasks. In Baddeley's influential model, working memory comprises a phonological loop (for verbal/auditory information), a visuospatial sketchpad (for visual and spatial information), a central executive (attentional control), and an episodic buffer (integration across subsystems and with long-term memory). Working memory capacity is strictly limited: most adults can hold approximately 7 ± 2 unrelated items simultaneously, and this capacity drops sharply when items must be processed...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Working-Memory|Working Memory]]*

> [!definition] **Working Memory Capacity** **[perm-note]**
> The amount of information that working memory can hold and process simultaneously. Often operationalized as Miller's "magic number" of 7 ± 2 chunks, though more recent research (Cowan, 2001) suggests a capacity of approximately 4 chunks under concurrent processing demands. Critically for CLT, working memory capacity is not a fixed limit on raw information quantity but on the number of distinct, unautomated elements that must be simultaneously active. Expertise increases effective capacity by replacing multiple elements with single automated schemas, not by expanding the underlying capacity ...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Working-Memory-Capacity|Working Memory Capacity]]*

> [!definition] **Working Memory in Reading** **[perm-note]**
> [[Baddeley's-Working-Memory-Model|Baddeley's working memory model]] includes three primary components relevant to reading: (1) the [[Phonological-Loop]], which holds verbal material in a phonological code for approximately 2 seconds through active rehearsal; (2) the visuospatial sketchpad, which processes visual features of text (orthography, spatial layout); and (3) the central executive, which allocates attentional resources, switches between tasks, and coordinates the interaction of long-term memory with active processing. Working memory capacity is limited — typically 3-4 chunks for the...
> 
> *Source: reading-fluency-automaticity-focused-analysis-2026-03-20* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Working-Memory-in-Reading|Working Memory in Reading]]*

## _X

> [!definition] **Excalidraw** **[perm-note]**
> [**Excalidraw**:: A powerful yet intuitive sketching tool seamlessly integrated into [[Obsidian]], enabling creation of hand-drawn style diagrams, sketches, UI wireframes, concept maps, and visual annotations directly within your vault.]^established-stable
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-excalidraw-and-markmind-2025121812* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Excalidraw]]*

> [!definition] **Excalidraw Library** **[perm-note]**
> [**Library-System**:: A personal collection of reusable drawing elements (shapes, icons, templates, components) that can be inserted instantly into any drawing, promoting consistency and accelerating creation.]^established
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-excalidraw-and-markmind-2025121812* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Excalidraw-Library|Excalidraw Library]]*

> [!definition] **ExcalidrawAutomate** **[perm-note]**
> [**ExcalidrawAutomate**:: A powerful scripting API that enables programmatic drawing creation, manipulation, and automation using JavaScript within [[Obsidian]]—transforming Excalidraw from manual tool to programmable system.]^established-stable
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-excalidraw-and-markmind-2025121812* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[ExcalidrawAutomate]]*

> [!definition] **Expert Blind Spot** **[perm-note]**
> **Definition**: The systematic tendency for subject-matter experts to be worse instructors of novices in proportion to the depth of their schematic automatization — because automated schemas operate below the threshold of conscious attention, the intermediate states that novices need to see are invisible to experts. NOT a failure of intelligence or motivation; a structural consequence of schema automation. Resolved not by de-automation but by cultivating pedagogical intermediate expertise.
> **Report-Specific Significance**: The practical prediction failure that motivated the practical lens c...
> 
> *Source: schema-theory-dialectical-re-examination-2026-03-22* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Expert-Blind-Spot|Expert Blind Spot]]*

> [!definition] **Expertise Reversal Effect** **[perm-note]**
> The finding that instructional supports that benefit novice learners become neutral and eventually harmful for more advanced learners. Mechanistically explained by the [[Redundancy-Effect]]: for an expert, a fully worked example is redundant with existing schemas; processing it generates extraneous load (effort to integrate redundant information) without schema-construction benefit. Implies that optimal instructional design must be responsive to current learner expertise and that a single, static instructional format cannot serve the full learning arc.
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Expertise-Reversal-Effect|Expertise Reversal Effect]]*

> [!definition] **Expository Advance Organizer** **[perm-note]**
> An advance organizer used when the new learning material is entirely unfamiliar to the learner — when there is essentially no relevant prior knowledge to activate. An expository organizer provides new, superordinate conceptual scaffolding that the learner did not already possess, giving them a framework within which the new material can be organized as it is encountered. Example: before teaching the concept of evolution to a student with no biology background, presenting a brief exposition of the idea that living things change over generations in response to environmental pressure provides ...
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Expository-Advance-Organizer|Expository Advance Organizer]]*

> [!definition] **Expository Organizer** **[perm-note]**
> A type of advance organizer used when learners lack relevant prior knowledge. Provides new, superordinate conceptual scaffolding at a higher level of abstraction than the material to follow, giving the learner a framework into which new specific material can be organized. Compare: [[Comparative-Organizer]].
> 
> *Source: advance-organizers-prior-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Expository-Organizer|Expository Organizer]]*

> [!definition] **Text Generator Plugin** **[perm-note]**
> [**Text-Generator-Plugin**:: An open-source [[Obsidian]] community plugin that provides a native interface to [[Large-Language-Models]] (LLMs) including [[OpenAI]], [[Anthropic-Claude]], [[Google-Gemini]], [[HuggingFace]], and local models via [[Ollama]]. It enables AI-assisted text generation, transformation, and automation directly within your vault using a sophisticated [[Template-System|template system]].]
> 
> *Source: reference-comprehensive-text-generator-plugin-complete-api-interface-reference-2025121507* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Text-Generator-Plugin|Text Generator Plugin]]*

> [!definition] **Textbase** **[perm-note]**
> A propositional representation of a text's explicit content — what the text actually says, represented as a network of linked propositions. Distinguished from the [[Situation-Model]] (what the world described by the text is like). Readers who construct only a textbase can answer questions about the text's wording but cannot reliably answer questions about the world it describes. Most monitoring instruction that targets verbatim recall operates at the textbase level; most consequential comprehension failures occur at the situation model level.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Textbase]]*

> [!definition] **Extended Mind Theory** **[perm-note]**
> The philosophical thesis that cognitive processes can genuinely extend beyond the boundary of brain and body into the environment. When an external element — a notebook, a tool, a digital device — plays the same functional role as an internal cognitive state, satisfying conditions of reliable availability, direct access, and automatic endorsement, it qualifies as a constituent part of the cognitive system. Distinguished from the weaker claim that the mind merely depends on or is causally influenced by environmental factors. The theory is a form of **vehicle externalism**: it holds not merel...
> 
> *Source: extended-mind-theory-foundational-report-2026-03-11* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Extended-Mind-Theory|Extended Mind Theory]]*

> [!definition] **Extended Thinking Modes** **[perm-note]**
> Claude supports multiple thinking modes controlled via the `<thinking_mode>` parameter:
> 
> - **`enabled`**: Thinking blocks generated when the model determines they would improve response quality
> - **`disabled`**: No thinking blocks generated (standard response mode)  
> - **`auto`**: Model autonomously decides when to use thinking based on task complexity
> - **`interleaved`**: Thinking can be interspersed with tool use and response generation for complex multi-step workflows
> 
> *Source: claudes-extended-thinking* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Extended-Thinking-Modes|Extended Thinking Modes]]*

> [!definition] **External-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> Behavior regulated by external contingencies — rewards, punishments, deadlines, surveillance. The [[Perceived-Internal-Locus-of-Causality|locus of causality]] is entirely external; the person acts to obtain or avoid consequences that originate outside the self. This is the most heteronomous form of intentional regulation. Crucially, external regulation does not cease when the contingency is removed — but behavior typically does, because the regulatory structure has not been internalized at all.
> 
> *Source: self-determination-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-19* | *Domain: motivational-psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[External-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Externalization Protocol** **[perm-note]**
> An intervention that moves the monitoring process from internal (invisible, suppressible) to external (visible, documented) form. Examples include annotation, think-aloud, self-explanation, and structured dialogue. The analytical basis for externalization's effectiveness in closing the monitoring-regulation gap is that it bypasses the fluency trap: once monitoring is externalized, fluency signals in the primary reading process cannot suppress it because it is occurring outside that process.
> 
> *Source: metacognition-silent-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-21* | *Domain: Cognitive Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Externalization-Protocol|Externalization Protocol]]*

> [!definition] **Extraneous Cognitive Load** **[perm-note]**
> The cognitive load imposed not by the content itself but by how it is presented — the instructional design decisions that require cognitive effort without contributing to schema construction. Split-attention layouts (where related information must be mentally integrated because it is spatially separated), redundant explanations that duplicate information already available in diagrams, and cluttered or poorly organized interfaces all generate extraneous load. This is the "wasteful" load — theoretically eliminable through good design. Reducing extraneous load is CLT's most actionable instruct...
> 
> *Source: cognitive-load-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Extraneous-Cognitive-Load|Extraneous Cognitive Load]]*

> [!definition] **Extrinsic Goal Content** **[perm-note]**
> The three canonical extrinsic goal domains are: (1) **Financial success** — aspiring toward wealth, material possessions, and financial security beyond baseline needs; (2) **Fame and popularity** — seeking widespread public recognition, celebrity, and social approval from large audiences; and (3) **Attractive appearance** — prioritizing one's physical appearance as evaluated by others, including the pursuit of beauty ideals and bodily perfection. What unites these three domains is that they are primarily valued for the social comparison advantages and external approval they provide, and the...
> 
> *Source: goal-contents-theory-focused-analysis-2026-03-13* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Extrinsic-Goal-Content|Extrinsic Goal Content]]*

> [!definition] **Extrinsic Motivation** **[perm-note]**
> Engagement with an activity as a means to a separable outcome — reward, praise, grade, the avoidance of punishment or shame. Critically, [[Self-Determination-Theory]] does not treat extrinsic motivation as monolithic. It posits a continuum of *internalization*: from pure external regulation (behavior controlled entirely by external forces) through introjection (behavior driven by internal pressures like shame or pride) to identification (behavior driven by personally endorsed values) and finally to integration (behavior fully assimilated with the self's values). The more internalized the ex...
> 
> *Source: metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Extrinsic-Motivation|Extrinsic Motivation]]*

## _Y

> [!definition] **Hypothetico-Deductive Method** **[perm-note]**
> The logical structure of empirical scientific inference, formalised by Karl Popper and others. A scientist proposes a hypothesis (H), deduces observational predictions from it (O: if H, then O), conducts an experiment, and interprets the result: a positive result is consistent with H (but does not prove it); a negative result (not-O) logically refutes H via modus tollens (not-O; if H then O; therefore not-H). The hypothetico-deductive method explains why experiments can falsify but not definitively verify scientific theories.
> 
> *Source: deductive-reasoning-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-10* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Hypothetico-Deductive-Method|Hypothetico-Deductive Method]]*

## _Z

> [!definition] **Zimmerman's Cyclical Model of Self-Regulated Learning** **[perm-note]**
> A [[Social-Cognitive-Theory-—-Bandura|social cognitive]] account of self-regulated learning organized as a three-phase cycle — Forethought, Performance, and Self-Reflection — each containing two sub-process categories. The cyclical claim asserts that self-reflection outputs directly shape subsequent forethought inputs, making the cycle self-modifying: learners who close the cycle with accurate adaptive inferences enter the next forethought phase with calibrated expectations, while learners who close with defensive inferences enter the next forethought phase with distorted ones.
> 
> *Source: zimmerman-cyclical-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23* | *Domain: Educational Psychology*
> *Permanent Note: [[Zimmerman's-Cyclical-Model-of-Self-Regulated-Learning|Zimmerman's Cyclical Model of Self-Regulated Learning]]*

> [!definition] **Zimmerman's Cyclical Model of Self-Regulation** **[perm-note]**
> Zimmerman's model describes self-regulation as a three-phase cycle: the **[[Forethought-Phase]]** (task analysis through goal-setting and strategic planning, underpinned by self-motivational beliefs including [[Self-Efficacy|self-efficacy]] and goal orientation); the **[[Performance-Phase]]** (self-control processes such as imagery, self-instruction, and attention focusing, combined with **[[Self-Monitoring|self-monitoring]]** of performance quality); and the **[[Self-Reflection-Phase]]** (self-judgment comparing current performance to goals, and self-reactions including self-satisfaction, ...
> 
> *Source: self-regulated-learning-focused-analysis-2026-03-06* | *Domain: Other / Uncategorised*
> *Permanent Note: [[Zimmerman's-Cyclical-Model-of-Self-Regulation|Zimmerman's Cyclical Model of Self-Regulation]]*

> [!definition] **Zone of Proximal Development** **[perm-note]**
> The distance between what a learner can accomplish independently and what they can accomplish with support from a more knowledgeable other. Vygotsky argued that learning is most productive within this zone — neither so easy as to require no effort nor so hard as to exceed capacity even with support. From a metacognitive perspective, identifying one's own ZPD requires accurate self-assessment of current capability and appropriate challenge calibration.
>
